P·L MORALITATES IN CANTICA CANTICORUM 203

Patrologia Latina · Vol. 203 ·

MORALITATES IN CANTICA CANTICORUM

PL 203, coll. 489–584  ·  Latin from the Migne printing  ·  First English translation  ·  column numbers follow the original plates, not the Garnier reprint

Philip of Harvengt

Philippus de Harveng

c. 1100 – 11 April 1183

Premonstratensian abbot of Bonne-Espérance in Hainaut, and one of the most prolific writers of his order. His early career was interrupted by a long quarrel over a monk who had transferred houses, in which Bernard of Clairvaux wrote against him and he was for some years suspended and away from his abbey; he was restored, and elected abbot in 1157. Besides the two commentaries on the Song of Songs printed in PL 203 — this one, on the moral sense, and a much larger *Commentaria* — he left a substantial treatise on the training of clergy, works on obedience and on silence, saints' lives, and a large body of letters.

INCIPIT PRIMUS TOMUS.

HERE BEGINS THE FIRST TOME.

PROOEMIUM

  • 489C

Dominis atque per omnia merito sanctitatis reverendis Patribus suis, MILONI Morinensium episcopo, HUGONI Praemonstratensium abbati, ille servorum Dei pejor et ultimus, cujus nomen continetur in quinque primis litteris primarum quinque partium primi tomi: discretam ungulam habere pedis Ezechielis vituli.

Opusculum subjectum in Canticis canticorum divina gratia praeeunte incoepi, hortatu et animatione cujusdam viri fidelis qui aliquando audierat me disputantem de quibusdam sententiis Divinitatis. Praeterea quaedam infirmitas a pueritia mea, licet per intervalla, frequenter me tamen pulsat et pulsavit, quae maxima ex parte me ad hoc opus incitavit. 489DArbitratus sum enim, si huic operi ardentius insisterem, quod pulsantis infirmitatis interdum molestias minus sentirem. Ratus sum, inquam, quod instantia hujus operis a me expelleret infestationem praefatae infirmitatis. Sane fateor omnibus rei veritatem. Opus quidem Deo aspirante a me incoeptum est, sed tamen praedicta infirmitas parum mihi adhuc 490Cmitigata est. Sed fortasse erunt aliqui mei vel alterius ordinis qui zelum Dei simulantes se habere, quamvis non secundum scientiam, dicant: Ille quis est, aut quae est persona ejus vel vita ipsius, seu sapientia, quia hoc praesumpsit facere? Quibus inquam: Ego quidem, sicut supra dixi, pejor et ultimus sum servorum Dei, persona corporis infirma, sermo contemptibilis, vita satis coram Deo despicabilis, sapientia vero mea nulla est.

« Magna, inquit, multitudo dulcedinis tuae, Domine, quam abscondisti timentibus te (Psal. XXX). » Vere magna et admirabilis est multitudo dulcedinis Dei, quam abscondit Deus timentibus et diligentibus se, et hic in divinis Scripturis et in futuro saeculo in contemplatione suae majestatis. Sed hanc dulcedinem 490Dsaeculi amatores capere non possunt, quia delectationibus vanis et concupiscentiis mortiferis repleti ad ineffabilia gaudia coelestis patriae nullatenus suspirant. Hanc ergo brevem praefationem idcirco praemittimus, antequam ad librum perveniamus, quatenus auditorem nostrum ad intelligenda divinae lectionis sacramenta studiosum et sollicitum 491Afaciamus, ne in hoc libello quidquam corporale vel carnale requirat. Est enim hujus libelli littera ab eo quod sonare videtur satis aliena, et nisi hanc a Spiritu sancto per certo editam esse crederemus, indignum esse per omnia Spiritus sancti charismatibus adjudicaremus. Quis enim oscula audiens, oculos, et genas, et labia Sponsae nominari, non statim ipso auditu merito indignaretur, eo quod talia in divinis eloquiis reperirentur, nisi Spiritu sancto dictante, de adventu Filii Dei ad humanum genus, de salute animarum fidelium haec scripta esse crederentur? Verum quanto durius littera nos a carnali et exteriori intellectu repellit, tanto nos ad interiorem et Deo amabilem sui intelligentiam suavius introducit. Sane in hoc creditur quod a septiformi 491Bgratia Spiritus sancti liber iste conditur, quia omnes et singulae sententiae, quae in eo reperiuntur, septem in se ad minus continere videntur, scilicet, 287 divinitatem et humanitatem Christi; conjunctionem Sponsi et Sponsae, id est Christi et Ecclesiae. Secundum vero rhetoricos flores, unimembrem vel bimembrem seu trimembrem aut quadrimembrem orationem. Juxta autem saeculares, disciplinas septem liberalium artium, tres partes philosophiae: logicam, ethicam, physicam, id est rationalem, moralem, naturalem, sine quibus partibus philosophiae non valet liber iste exponi.

Praeterea, titulus qui huic libello praefertur, totius libri sensum in se continere videtur. Sicut enim in coelo sol positus diffusis ubique radiis suis, et 491Cdilatata admirabili claritate sua, totum mundum praesentialiter illustrat, sic iste titulus, qui huic libello praeponitur, quid iste libellus velit dicere, maxima ex parte jam nobis manifestat dicens: Vox optantis Christi adventum.

Quaeritur in isto primo titulo cum duae personae introduci videantur, Christi videlicet et cujusdam optantis, cur istius optantis proprium nomen non exprimitur, cum Christi nomen manifeste nominetur? Ad quod dicendum, quia si quaelibet persona nominatim exprimeretur vel proprie poneretur, aliae personae quae in isto libro variis modis loquuntur, fortassis exclusae viderentur. Christi vero nomen idcirco manifeste et sine velamine ponitur, quia de 491Dnullius alterius adventu, nisi de solius Dei Filii adventu et incarnatione prophetia libri hujus contexitur. Tum praeterea possunt aliqui mirari cur Christus, qui dignior et excellentior persona est, in hoc libro primitus non incipit loqui. Sed ratio justa et auctoritate subnixa hoc expostulabat, ut inferior persona in primis loqueretur, et ipsum, sine quo salvari non poterat, ut ad sui liberationem veniret, humiliter deprecaretur, et diceret: Osculetur me osculo oris sui (Cant. I). Beatus Paulus apostolus nos instruit, dicens: Quoniam quidem omnia quae contigerunt patribus nostris, in figura contigerunt illis (I Cor. X).

Si ergo apud illos figura fuit, apud nos veritas esse debet. Multam igitur Scripturam Veteris Testamenti 492Aapud memetipsum revolvens, hujus veritatis figuram requirebam, si forte aliquis patriarcharum, vel prophetarum alicubi osculatus fuisset uxorem suam; quatenus figura praecedens, clarius nobis manifestaret subsequentem. Verumtamen hoc de nullo alio, nisi de solo Jacob patriarcha scriptum reperi. Siquidem de eo solo scriptum est quod osculatus sit aliquando uxorem suam, illam videlicet famosissimam Rachelem de qua nulli dubium est, quin teneat sanctae Ecclesiae figuram. Jacob vero et nominis interpretatione, et virtutis operatione, figuram Christi dignoscitur tenuisse.

Scriptum est enim in libro Genesis (cap. XXIX) quod quondam idem patriarcha Jacob ad terram orientalem proficiscebatur, qui veniens ad quemdam 492Bputeum, cum pastoribus regionis illius loquebatur, et ecce, inquit Scriptura, Rachel cum grege suo veniebat, nam gregem patris sui ipsa pascebat. Quam ergo cum cognovisset Jacob esse consobrinam suam, gregem illius adaquavit et osculatus est eam.

Postea vero flevit Jacob, et indicavit ei eo quod esset consanguineus ejus. Quis igitur ille est, qui ad terram orientalem proficiscitur, nisi Christus de quo propheta ait: Ecce vir, Oriens nomen ejus (Zach. VI). Quae vero est terra orientalis, nisi beata et perpetua Dei Genitrix Virgo Maria, de qua Christus oriri dignatus est, quam Ezechiel (cap. XI) portam orientalem appellat? Quid puteus ille praefiguravit, nisi mysticam et occultam Scripturam quae 492CChristum nasciturum esse praedixit? Quid denique Rachelis grex significavit, quem Jacob adaquavit nisi gregem prophetarum, quem Christus aqua fontis salientis in vitam aeternam potavit? Quid vero fletus Jacob insinuavit, nisi compassionem quam Christus de nobis habuit quando pro nobis mori disposuit? Quid est autem quod sanctus Jacob Racheli indicat quod consanguineus ejus est, nisi hoc quod Dominus per prophetam dixit: Narrabo nomen tuum fratribus meis (Psal. XXI), et in Evangelio: Ite, nuntiate fratribus meis etc. (Matth. XXVIII.) Quid vero designat osculum quod Jacob Racheli dedit, nisi hoc quod Christus naturam generis humani per incarnationis mysterium sibi sociavit? 492Dunde idem genus humanum in hoc loco de eodem Christo dicit: Osculetur me osculo oris sui. Idem ille qui quondam a patriarchis praefiguratus, et a prophetis praenuntiatus est, ille, inquam, veniat, et de mea natura carnem immaculatam assumat, et me ad paradisi gaudia, quae per Adam perdidi, mirabiliter reducat. Dicat et sancta Ecclesia de Christo: Osculetur me osculo oris sui, id est Verbum caro fiat, et sicut olim de Adam dormiente Eva fabricata est (Gen. II), ita idem Verbum caro factum (Joan. I), scilicet, Deus homo factus, moriens in cruce, me sibi sponsam sanguine suo subarrhatam despondeat. Dicat et beata Maria mater Christi, dicat, inquam, et ipsa de Christo: Osculetur me osculo oris sui, id est sicut dudum per Isaiam prophetam promisit 493Adicens: Ecce virgo concipiet (Isa. VII), Filius Dei in me veniat, et de mea virginea carne, corpus sacrosanctum accipiat, ut tanquam sponsus de thalamo suo (Psal. XVIII), sic ipse carnis meae purpureo decoratus pallio procedat de utero meo. Dicat etiam unaquaeque anima fidelis de Christo: Osculetur me osculo oris sui, id est sic me sibi societ per fidem et operationem in hoc saeculo, quatenus in aeterna felicitate 288 unus spiritus effici merear cum ipso. De hoc osculo Christi et sanctae Ecclesiae, id est Incarnationis mysterio, David prophetae dicit: Justitia et pax osculatae sunt (Psal. LXXXIV). Junge ergo, si tali placet, lilium violae vel rosae, tale est osculum Sponsi et Sponsae, Christi et Ecclesiae, id est conjunctio divinae et humanae naturae. 493BOsculetur me osculo oris sui. In ista sententia invenies septem sacramenta, Jacob et Rachelis, Dei et generis humani, Christi et sanctae Ecclesiae, Filii Dei et beatae Mariae, Christi et fidelis animae, pacis et justitiae, lilii et violae.

PROEM

  • 489C

To his lords and, in all things, by the merit of holiness reverend fathers, MILO bishop of the Morini, HUGH abbot of the Premonstratensians: that worse and last one of the servants of God, whose name is contained in the first five letters of the first five parts of the first Tome, [wishes them] to have the divided hoof of the foot of Ezekiel's calf.

The little work set out below upon the Song of Songs I began, divine grace going before, at the exhortation and encouragement of a certain faithful man who had once heard me disputing upon certain sentences of the Divinity. Besides, a certain infirmity, from my boyhood — though by intervals — yet frequently assails me and has assailed me, which for the greatest part urged me on to this work. 489DFor I judged that, if I pressed upon this work the more ardently, I should the less feel meanwhile the troubles of the infirmity that assails me. I reckoned, I say, that the pressing of this work would drive from me the harassment of the aforesaid infirmity. Truly I confess to all the truth of the matter. The work indeed was begun by me at God's inspiring, but nevertheless the aforesaid infirmity has as yet been little 490Cmitigated for me. But perhaps there will be some, of my own order or of another, who, feigning that they have the zeal of God — though not according to knowledge — will say: Who is he, or what is his person, or his own life, or his wisdom, that he has presumed to do this? To whom I say: I indeed, as I said above, am the worse and the last of the servants of God, infirm in the person of my body, contemptible in speech, in life despicable enough before God, and my wisdom is truly none.

« Great, he says, is the multitude of thy sweetness, O Lord, which thou hast hidden for them that fear thee (Psal. XXX). » [Vulg. Quam magna multitudo, "How great is the multitude"] Truly great and wonderful is the multitude of the sweetness of God, which God hides for those who fear and love him, both here in the Holy Scriptures and in the age to come in the contemplation of his majesty. But this sweetness 490Dthe lovers of the world cannot take in, because, filled with empty delights and deadly desires, they in no wise sigh after the unspeakable joys of the heavenly fatherland. This brief preface, therefore, we send before, ere we come to the book, that we may make our hearer studious and solicitous 491Afor the understanding of the mysteries of the divine reading, lest in this little book he seek anything bodily or carnal. For the letter of this little book is quite alien from that which it seems to sound; and unless we believed it to have been assuredly published by the Holy Spirit, we should adjudge it in all things unworthy of the charisms of the Holy Spirit. For who, hearing kisses, and the eyes and cheeks and lips of the Bride named, would not at once and deservedly be indignant at the very hearing, in that such things were found in the divine oracles, unless it were believed that these things were written — the Holy Spirit dictating — concerning the coming of the Son of God to the human race, and concerning the salvation of faithful souls? But the more harshly the letter drives us back from the carnal and outward understanding, the more sweetly it leads us in to that inward understanding of itself which is lovely to God. Assuredly in this it is believed that by the sevenfold 491Bgrace of the Holy Spirit this book is composed: because all and every one of the sentences that are found in it seem to contain in themselves at the least seven things, namely, 287 the divinity and the humanity of Christ; the conjunction of the Bridegroom and the Bride, that is, of Christ and the Church. And according to the rhetorical flowers, a one-membered or two-membered or three-membered or four-membered sentence. And according to secular men, the disciplines of the seven liberal arts, the three parts of philosophy: logic, ethics, physics, that is, rational, moral, natural — without which parts of philosophy this book cannot be expounded.

Moreover, the title which is set before this little book seems to contain in itself the sense of the whole book. For as the sun, set in heaven, with its rays poured out everywhere and 491Cits wonderful brightness spread abroad, lights up the whole world by its presence, so this title, which is set before this little book, already for the greatest part makes plain to us what this little book means to say, saying: The voice of one desiring the coming of Christ.

It is asked, in this first title — since two persons seem to be brought in, namely Christ's and that of a certain one desiring — why the proper name of that one desiring is not expressed, when the name of Christ is manifestly named? To which it must be said: that if any person whatever were expressed by name or set down properly, the other persons who in this book speak in various ways would perhaps seem to be shut out. But the name of Christ is set down manifestly and without a veil for this reason, that concerning 491Dthe coming of no other, save of the Son of God alone — his coming and incarnation — is the prophecy of this book woven together. Then, besides, some may wonder why Christ, who is the worthier and more excellent person, does not first begin to speak in this book. But a just reason, propped upon authority, demanded this: that the inferior person should speak among the first, and should humbly entreat him, without whom she could not be saved, to come for her deliverance, and should say: Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth (Cant. I). The blessed apostle Paul instructs us, saying: Since indeed all the things that befell our fathers, befell them in a figure (I Cor. X). [Vulg. Haec autem omnia in figura contingebant illis, "Now all these things happened to them in figure"]

If therefore among them there was a figure, among us there ought to be the truth. Turning over therefore much Scripture of the Old Testament 492Awithin myself, I sought after the figure of this truth, whether perchance any of the patriarchs, or of the prophets, had anywhere kissed his wife, so that the figure going before might make the one that follows more clearly plain to us. Nevertheless I found this written of no other save of Jacob the patriarch alone. For of him alone it is written that he once kissed his wife, that most famous Rachel, namely, of whom it is doubtful to none that she holds the figure of holy Church. But Jacob, both by the interpretation of his name and by the working of virtue, is discerned to have held the figure of Christ.

For it is written in the book of Genesis (cap. XXIX) that this same patriarch Jacob once was journeying to the eastern land; who, coming to a certain 492Bwell, was speaking with the shepherds of that region; and behold, says the Scripture, Rachel was coming with her flock, for she herself was pasturing her father's flock. When therefore Jacob had known her to be his cousin, he watered her flock and kissed her.

But afterwards Jacob wept, and made known to her that he was her kinsman. Who then is he who journeys to the eastern land, save Christ, of whom the prophet says: Behold a man, the Orient is his name (Zach. VI). And what is the eastern land, save the blessed and everlasting Mother of God, the Virgin Mary, of whom Christ deigned to arise, whom Ezekiel (cap. XI) calls the eastern gate? What did that well prefigure, save the mystical and hidden Scripture which 492Cforetold that Christ was to be born? What, finally, did Rachel's flock signify, which Jacob watered, save the flock of the prophets, which Christ gave to drink of the water of the fountain leaping up unto life everlasting? And what did Jacob's weeping intimate, save the compassion which Christ had upon us when he purposed to die for us? And what is it that holy Jacob makes known to Rachel, that he is her kinsman, save this which the Lord said through the prophet: I will declare thy name to my brethren (Psal. XXI), and in the Gospel: Go, tell my brethren etc. (Matth. XXVIII.) And what does the kiss betoken which Jacob gave to Rachel, save this: that Christ joined to himself the nature of the human race through the mystery of the incarnation? 492Dwhence the same human race in this place says of the same Christ: Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. Let that same one, who once was prefigured by the patriarchs and foretold by the prophets — let him, I say, come, and take unspotted flesh of my nature, and wondrously lead me back to the joys of paradise which I lost through Adam. Let holy Church also say of Christ: Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth, that is, let the Word be made flesh; and as of old Eve was fashioned from the sleeping Adam (Gen. II), so let the same Word made flesh (Joan. I) — that is, God made man, dying upon the cross — betroth me to himself as a bride pledged with his own blood. Let blessed Mary also, the mother of Christ, say — let her too, I say, say of Christ: Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth, that is: as long ago he promised through Isaiah the prophet, 493Asaying: Behold a virgin shall conceive (Isa. VII), let the Son of God come into me, and of my virgin flesh take a most holy body, that as a bridegroom out of his chamber (Psal. XVIII), so he himself, adorned with the purple cloak of my flesh, may come forth out of my womb. Let every faithful soul also say of Christ: Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth, that is: so let him join me to himself by faith and by working in this world, that in eternal happiness 288 I may deserve to be made one spirit with him. Of this kiss of Christ and holy Church, that is, of the mystery of the Incarnation, David says to the prophet: Justice and peace have kissed (Psal. LXXXIV). Join therefore, if it please such a one, the lily to the violet or to the rose: such is the kiss of the Bridegroom and the Bride, of Christ and the Church, that is, the conjunction of the divine and the human nature. 493BLet him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. In this sentence thou wilt find seven mysteries: of Jacob and Rachel, of God and the human race, of Christ and holy Church, of the Son of God and blessed Mary, of Christ and the faithful soul, of peace and justice, of the lily and the violet.

RECAPITULATIO A SUPERIORIBUS.

Summa intentione et puro corde a nobis Deus exorandus est, declaratio sermonum cujus illuminat et spirituale intellectum parvulis malitia manifestat (I Cor. XIV), quatenus dignetur oculos cordis nostri revelare, ut valeamus mirabilia, quae sunt in lege ejus, considerare. Auctorem igitur hujus libri Spiritum sanctum nullus sane sapiens dubitat. Salomonem 493Cvero organum fuisse Spiritus sancti, eo tempore quo librum istum edidit nullus ecclesiasticis litteris imbutus ignorat. Sine enim Spiritus sancti gratia non potuit proferre tanta ac talia Christi et Ecclesiae sacramenta. Cum ergo ratum teneamus, quod Spiritus sanctus hunc librum fecerit, quaerendum nobis est, quare idem Spiritus sanctus eumdem librum ab O littera incoeperit vel quid sibi in hac littera, plus quam in aliis litteris placuerit. Verumtamen sacramentum hujus litterae quae est O, quantae sit auctoritatis et dignitatis, ipse dignatus est ostendere, qui ejusdem litterae nomen sibi imposuit, dicens: Ego sum α alpha et ω (Apoc. I), id est principium et finis. Principium, inquit, sum, dans principia existendi omnibus creaturis. Finis sum, 493Did est consummatio; quia omnibus hominibus deficientibus, solus remaneo, quoniam ego sum Deus et mutabilitati nequaquam subjaceo, unde Jacobus apostolus ait: Apud quem non est transmutatio nec vicissitudinis obumbratio (Jacob. I). Quod autem Christus finis sit, id est consummatio, Paulus manifestat cum dicit: Finis legis Christus ad justitiam omni credenti (Rom. X). Denique si sacramentum hujus litterae, quae est O, adhuc subtilius indagare voluerimus, in nulla alia littera tantam figuram, non solum totius orbis, verum etiam ipsius Dei reperiemus. Ex omni enim parte sui rotunda et perfecta invenitur, sicut orbis hujus saeculi a philosophis in modum spherae rotundus describitur. Praeterea si secundum Graeca elementa attentius inspicimus, 494Aistam litteram ω procul dubio invenimus eam esse trimembrem, id est tria membra habentem. Quid igitur designant tria membra in unam ω litteram sursum respicientia, nisi tres personas in unius summae Trinitatis substantia? Non ergo casu, sed certo Divinitatis mysterio Spiritus sanctus exordium hujus libri ab ω littera inchoat, quia illum liber iste per omnen textum sui praedicat, qui totum orbem de nihilo creavit, et seipsum unam esse in Trinitate personam in Evangelio declarat, dicens: Ego et Pater unum sumus (Joan. X). Tria igitur ω litterae membra in coelum respicientia, tres personas, ut praediximus, declarant in una Trinitatis substantia. Et bene ejusdem litterae membra sursum aspiciunt, et quasi de coelo venientia deorsum litteram 494Bunam faciunt, quia cognitio sanctae Trinitatis per Dominum Jesum ad nos descendit, et in unam sanctam Ecclesiam catholicam per fidei unitatem, sedem sibi collocavit. Eapropter in Evangelio ipse Dominus Jesus ait: Haec est autem vita aeterna ut cognoscant te et quem misisti Jesum Christum (Joan. XVII) subauditur: et Spiritum sanctum, unum et verum Deum esse. Sane neque hoc a prudenti lectore negligenter arbitror praetereundum, quod iste liber ab osculo (quod est signum charitatis et pacis), sumit exordium. Illum namque mysticis et apertis locutionibus nobis insinuat, in cujus nativitate angelica multitudo clamat. Gloria in excelsis Deo, et in terra pax hominibus bonae voluntatis (Luc. II). Est praeterea aliud sacramentum quod adhuc de osculo 494Cpotest dici, quia non valet nisi a duobus osculum fieri. Duo ejusdem e diverso dentes accedunt, in uno pacis osculo conveniunt, sicque simul conjunctis labiis in charitate osculum perficiunt. Duo quippe e diverso accedentes et in unum convenientes, divinitas et humanitas Christi non incongruenter debet intelligi, quae valde ex diverso accesserunt, quando altera de coelo, altera de terra in unum beatae Mariae semper Virginis uterum simul convenerunt, et quasi perfectum osculum dederunt sibi invicem istae duae naturae, quia sic operante gratia Spiritus sancti sunt inseparabiliter foederatae, quatenus sine fidei periculo ulterius non debeat dici Deus sine homine nec homo sine Deo; et hoc est quod dicere videtur 494Deadem nostra natura in hoc nostro libro: Osculetur me osculo oris sui; ac si diceret: Opto ut sic me Deus Dei Filius in uterum Virginis assumat, id est, Verbum caro fiat, quatenus quod erat, semper verus Deus permaneat, et me immutatam de mortalitate ad immortalitatem secum in unam personam Dei et hominis aeternaliter constit

Auctoritate Scripturae sacrae didicimus (Job V), quia nihil fit in terra sine causa, eo 289 quod in manu Dei sumus, et nos, et sermones nostri. Quia ergo constat sine dubio quod quoniam in manu ejus sumus et nos et sermones nostri, profecto liquide patet, quia non possumus quidquam bene loqui, nisi aut illo cooperante, aut ab illo permissi. Denique, et si nihil sine causa in terra fieri dicitur, nec 495Ahoc utique absque ratione factum esse arbitramur, ut in principio expositionis hujus libelli, liliis et violis et caeteris floribus uteremur. Dignum plane fuit ut expositio nostra a floribus sumeret exordium, quia de illo ipso adjuvante loqui disponimus qui ait: Ego flos campi et lilium convallium (Cant. II). Siquidem ipse est flos ille admirabilis, qui secundum Isaiam prophetam (cap. XI) de radice Jesse ascendit, id est Christus, qui de Maria Virgine carnem assumpsit, et sicut flos suaviter redolens, undique suum gratissimum emanat odorem, sic ipse notitiam suae divinitatis et humanitatis in universum sparsit per orbem. Praeterea sicut flores sine humano cultu ex se gignunt favos et mella, sic Deum et hominem absque virili contactu de se genuit 495BVirgo Maria. Dicamus ergo hunc librum, qui de illo admirabili flore loquitur, florem esse florum, id est florem esse aliorum librorum, praeter ipsius summi floris, id est Christi.

Omnes quippe sancti, flores in sancto colloquio sunt appellati, unde est illud: Justus ut palma florebit, etc. (Psal. XCI.) Justi florebunt sicut lilium ante Dominum. Sane omnes sacrae Scripturae libri, flores appellari possunt, pro eo quod nos floribus, id est virtutibus spiritualibus florere faciunt. Igitur sicut Christus flos est aliorum florum, id est honos, gloria et decus suorum sanctorum, sic liber iste, qui appellatur Canticum canticorum, sicut supra diximus, excepto Evangelio Christi, flos est florum, id est flos aliorum librorum. Ecce, ut audivimus, 495CSponsus, id est Christus in hoc libro lilium se appellat, et ideo quia in omnibus pene sacri eloquii locis lilium virginitatem et castitatem munditiamque significat. Et quoniam ipse virgo et Virginis filius fuit, propterea maxime in Evangelio suo, inter reliquas virtutes, castitatem nobis commendavit, dicens: Sint lumbi nostri praecincti (Luc. XXII.) Ac si diceret: Quoniam amatorem virginitatis, castitatis et munditiae me esse non dubitatis, videte ut in cogitatione, locutione et opere, castitatem, quae mihi prae omnibus virtutibus grata est, custodiatis. Praeterea denique et in Nazareth quae interpretatur flos, sum nutritus, quia anima illa delectabiliter me pascit et nutrit piis operibus, quae virginitatem 495Dspirat, castitate flagrat, munditiam amat.

Sicut igitur per lilium Sponsus idem Christus in hoc libro potest intelligi, sic etiam per violae florem, Sponsa ejus, id est sancta Ecclesia debet interpretari. Et merito per hunc florem, scilicet violam, quae purpurei vel aetherei coloris est, pia Mater Ecclesia designatur, quae filios suos incessanter ad gloriosa martyrum certamina et ad superna perpetuae beatitudinis gaudia adhortatur. Per purpureum igitur colorem accipitur gloria martyrii, per aethereum vero felix et gloriosa exspectatio regni Dei. Sed neque hoc sub silentio praetereundum videtur, quod idem flos, scilicet viola, temperativae naturae esse describitur, adeo ut illis mederi valeat, quos ultra vires immoderatus ardor carnis fatigat. 496AQuod profecto sanctae Ecclesiae eleganter assignari debet, quae filiis suis ab omnigena tentationum infestatione vulneratis, piae confessionis et poenitentiae malagmata adhibet, unde et unusquisque fidelis debet dicere ad matrem suam Ecclesiam: Secundum multitudinem tentationum mearum in corde meo consolationes tuae laetificaverunt animam meam.

RECAPITULATION FROM THE FOREGOING.

With the utmost intention and with a pure heart God must be entreated by us, the declaration of whose words gives light and makes manifest spiritual understanding to those who are little ones in malice (I Cor. XIV), so that he may deign to unveil the eyes of our heart, that we may be able to consider the wonders that are in his law. That the author of this book, therefore, is the Holy Spirit, no man of sound wisdom doubts. And that Solomon 493Cwas the instrument of the Holy Spirit at the time when he published this book, no one steeped in ecclesiastical letters is ignorant. For without the grace of the Holy Spirit he could not have brought forth so many and such mysteries of Christ and the Church. Since therefore we hold it settled that the Holy Spirit made this book, it must be asked by us why the same Holy Spirit began the same book from the letter O, or what pleased him in this letter more than in the other letters. Nevertheless, of how great authority and dignity is the mystery of this letter which is O, he himself deigned to show, who laid the name of that same letter upon himself, saying: I am α alpha and ω (Apoc. I), that is, the beginning and the end. I am the beginning, he says, giving to all creatures the beginnings of being. I am the end, 493Dthat is, the consummation; because, when all men fail, I alone remain, since I am God and am in no way subject to mutability; whence the apostle James says: With whom there is no change, nor shadow of alteration (Jacob. I). And that Christ is the end, that is, the consummation, Paul makes plain when he says: The end of the law is Christ, unto justice to everyone that believeth (Rom. X). Finally, if we should wish to search out yet more subtly the mystery of this letter which is O, in no other letter shall we find so great a figure, not only of the whole world, but even of God himself. For it is found round and perfect on every side of itself, just as the world of this age is described by the philosophers as round after the manner of a sphere. Moreover, if according to the Greek letters we look more attentively, 494Awe find that this letter ω is beyond doubt three-membered, that is, having three members. What then do the three members in the one letter ω, looking upward, betoken, save three persons in the substance of the one most high Trinity? Not by chance, therefore, but by a sure mystery of the Godhead does the Holy Spirit begin the opening of this book from the letter ω, because this book through the whole text of itself preaches him who created the whole world out of nothing, and who declares in the Gospel that he himself is one person in the Trinity, saying: I and the Father are one (Joan. X). The three members, therefore, of the letter ω looking toward heaven declare, as we have said above, three persons in one substance of the Trinity. And well do the members of that same letter look upward, and, as though coming down from heaven, make below one letter 494B; because the knowledge of the holy Trinity came down to us through the Lord Jesus, and set its seat for itself in one holy catholic Church through the unity of faith. For which cause the Lord Jesus himself says in the Gospel: And this is life eternal, that they may know thee, and Jesus Christ whom thou hast sent (Joan. XVII) — it is understood withal: and the Holy Spirit, to be one and true God. [Vulg. ut cognoscant te solum Deum verum, et quem misisti Iesum Christum, "that they may know thee, the only true God"] Assuredly neither do I judge that this is to be passed over carelessly by a prudent reader, that this book takes its opening from a kiss (which is the sign of charity and of peace). For it intimates to us in mystical and open utterances him at whose nativity the angelic multitude cries out: Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace to men of good will (Luc. II). [Vulg. Gloria in altissimis Deo, "Glory to God in the highest"] There is besides another mystery which can yet be said concerning the kiss 494C: that a kiss cannot be made save by two. Two teeth of the same one draw near from opposite sides, they meet in one kiss of peace, and so, with lips joined together, they accomplish the kiss in charity. For the two drawing near from opposite sides and meeting into one ought not unfittingly to be understood as the divinity and the humanity of Christ, which drew very near from opposite sides when the one from heaven and the other from earth met together into the one womb of blessed Mary ever Virgin, and these two natures gave, as it were, a perfect kiss to one another, because they are so inseparably leagued together, the grace of the Holy Spirit working it, that henceforth it must not be said, without peril to faith, that there is God without man, nor man without God; and this is what our same nature seems to say 494Din this our book: Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth; as though it should say: I desire that God the Son of God so take me up into the womb of the Virgin — that is, that the Word be made flesh — that he may remain always what he was, true God, and eternally constit me, changed from mortality to immortality, into one person of God and man together with himself [The sentence breaks off in mid-word here: our digitized source ends the paragraph with a truncated verb and supplies nothing further. Recovery would require reading the column on Migne's plate.]

By the authority of Holy Scripture we have learned (Job V) that nothing is done on the earth without a cause, for the reason 289 that we are in the hand of God, both we and our words. Since therefore it stands beyond doubt that, because we are in his hand, both we and our words, it plainly and clearly appears that we cannot speak anything well, unless either with him working together with us, or being permitted by him. Finally, if nothing is said to be done on earth without a cause, neither 495Ado we judge that this at all was done without a reason: that at the beginning of the exposition of this little book we should use lilies and violets and the other flowers. It was plainly worthy that our exposition should take its opening from flowers, since with his help we set ourselves to speak of him who says: I am the flower of the field and the lily of the valleys (Cant. II). For he himself is that wonderful flower who, according to Isaiah the prophet (cap. XI), comes up from the root of Jesse — that is, Christ, who took flesh of the Virgin Mary; and as a flower sweetly fragrant sends forth on every side its most pleasing odor, so he himself scattered the knowledge of his divinity and his humanity throughout the whole world. Moreover, as flowers without human tillage bring forth of themselves honeycombs and honey, so without man's touch did the 495BVirgin Mary bring forth of herself God and man. Let us say, therefore, that this book, which speaks of that wonderful flower, is the flower of flowers, that is, is the flower of the other books, saving that of the highest flower himself, that is, of Christ.

For all the saints are called flowers in holy converse, whence is that word: The just shall flourish like the palm tree, etc. (Psal. XCI.) The just shall flourish like the lily before the Lord. Assuredly all the books of Holy Scripture can be called flowers, for this reason, that they make us to flourish with flowers, that is, with spiritual virtues. Therefore, as Christ is the flower of the other flowers — that is, the honor, the glory and the comeliness of his saints — so this book, which is called the Song of Songs, is, as we said above, saving the Gospel of Christ, the flower of flowers, that is, the flower of the other books. Behold, as we have heard, 495Cthe Bridegroom, that is, Christ, calls himself in this book a lily; and this because in almost all places of the sacred oracles the lily signifies virginity and chastity and cleanness. And since he himself was a virgin and the son of a Virgin, therefore above all in his Gospel, among the rest of the virtues, he commended chastity to us, saying: Let our loins be girded (Luc. XXII.) [Vulg. Luc. XII, 35 Sint lumbi vestri praecincti, "let your loins be girded"] As though he should say: Since you do not doubt that I am a lover of virginity, of chastity and of cleanness, see that in thought, in speech and in work you keep that chastity which is pleasing to me above all virtues. Moreover, finally, I was also nourished in Nazareth, which is interpreted flower, because that soul delightfully feeds and nourishes me with pious works, which breathes virginity 495D, burns with chastity, loves cleanness.

As, therefore, by the lily the Bridegroom, the same Christ, can be understood in this book, so also by the flower of the violet his Bride, that is, holy Church, ought to be interpreted. And rightly by this flower, namely the violet, which is of a purple or airy color, is the loving Mother Church betokened, who without ceasing exhorts her sons to the glorious contests of the martyrs and to the joys on high of everlasting blessedness. By the purple color, then, is taken the glory of martyrdom; by the airy, the happy and glorious expectation of the kingdom of God. But neither does it seem that this should be passed over in silence, that the same flower, namely the violet, is described as being of a tempering nature, so much so that it can heal those whom the immoderate ardor of the flesh wearies beyond their strength. 496AWhich assuredly ought elegantly to be assigned to holy Church, who applies to her sons, wounded by the harassment of temptations of every kind, the poultices of loving confession and penance; whence also every one of the faithful ought to say to his mother the Church: According to the multitude of my temptations in my heart, thy consolations have gladdened my soul. [Vulg. Psal. XCIII, 19 dolorum meorum, "of my sorrows"]

SECUNDA ALLEGORIA DE JACOB.

In memoria abundante, in Spiritus sancti delectatione recordari debemus quod in superiori parte hujus libri de profectione Jacob patriarchae ad terram Orientalem locuti sumus, et (prout Spiritus sanctus eo tempore vires nobis subministravit) paupertas nostri ingenii ipsum Jacob, et terram Orientalem allegorico sensu interpretando discussit. Restat 496Bigitur, ut itidem repetamus, et non, ut dignum est, sed ut raritas nostri sensus utcunque capere potest, secundae allegoriae flores suaviter redolentes perquiramus. Ager enim quidam est redundans floribus divina pagina, in quo delectari debet fidelis anima, colligendo diversos allegoriae flores, id est meditando sensus spirituales. Unde David propheta dicit: Delectare in Domino et dabit tibi petitiones cordis tui (Psal. XXXVI.) Terra igitur Orientalis, juste appellatur coelestis Hierusalem, scilicet habitatio supernorum civium, de qua Psalmista ait: Portio mea in terra viventium (Psal. CXLI.) Ad quam profecto qui bene operando pervenire poterit, mortem ultra non gustabit, quia tunc in Deo aeterna nativitate sanctus quisque renascitur, cum ejus vita temporalis conditionali 496Cmorte terminatur. Eapropter elegans consuetudo in sancta Ecclesia inolevit, ut tunc sanctorum natalitia pronuntiemus, quando illos ex hac luce migrasse cognoscimus. Quis ergo est ille Jacob, qui luctator interpretatur, nisi spiritus 290 noster, cui colluctatio adversus spirituales nequitias sine intervallo indicitur? Proficiscatur ergo noster Jacob ad terram Orientis, id est festinet spiritus noster passibus bonae operationis ad terram repromissionis: ad quam repromissionis terram Paulus suspirabat, cum dicebat: Festinemus ingredi in illam requiem (Hebr. IV). Sed festinantibus nobis ad coelestem Hierusalem, puteus qui grandi lapide clauditur, obviat, quia sicut David propheta ait: Judicia Dei abyssus 496Dmulta (Psal. XXXV). Cui Salomon concordat: Omnia, inquiens, reservantur homini post mortem incerta (Eccle. IX). Quid vero dicimus esse hunc puteum qui grandi lapide clauditur, nisi occultum et terribile Dei consilium, quod nullatenus usque ad diem judicii manifestatur? Quod propheta expavescens ait: Venite et videte opera Dei, terribilis in consiliis super filios hominum (Psal. LXV). Quid autem pastores illic cum gregibus suis significant, qui circa puteum accubant, nisi fideles animas tam praelatorum quam subditorum, quae judicium Dei exspectant? Unde bene dicitur quod congregatis pastoribus cum gregibus tunc lapis ab ore putei removebatur, et sic oves adaquabantur, quia venientibus omnibus hominibus ad judicium Dei, terribile consilium ejus, 497Aquod modo occultum est, manifestabitur. Tunc demum oves adaquabuntur aqua putei reserati, cum unusquisque damnabitur aut salvabitur secundum justum judicium occulti consilii Dei: Ite, maledicti, in ignem aeternum qui preparatus est (Matt. XXV); illis vero qui a dextris erunt: Venite, benedicti Patris mei, percipite regnum (ibid.) Sicque mirabili et ineffabili modo Jacob recognoscet faciem cognatae suae Rachelis, cum spiritus noster contemplativam vitam aspiciet in coelis, ad quam diu suspiravit adhuc positus in terris. Et inde est quod Rachel interpretatur visum principium, quia tunc non in aenigmate neque per speculum, sed facie ad faciem (I Cor. XIII) contemplabitur noster spiritus ipsum Deum. Osculum autem quod dedit Jacob Racheli 497Bpossumus sub illa Dominica sententia interpretari qua dicitur: Pater, volo ut sicut ego et tu unum sumus, ita et ipsi unum sint in nobis (Joan. XVIII). Luce ergo clarius constat quia quando alter alteri osculum pacis dat, sine interpositione alicujus rei, charitativo affectu, os ori consociat. Hinc est quod beatus Joannes dicit: Filii Dei sumus, et nondum apparet quid erimus, scimus autem cum apparuerit similes ei erimus, quia videbimus eum sicuti est (Joan. III). Non erit ergo inter Creatorem et creaturam aliqua oppositio, alioquin non esset vera et perfecta beatitudo.

THE SECOND ALLEGORY CONCERNING JACOB.

With abounding memory, in the delight of the Holy Spirit, we ought to call to mind that in the earlier part of this book we spoke of the journey of Jacob the patriarch to the Eastern land, and (in so far as the Holy Spirit at that time supplied us with strength) the poverty of our wit discussed that same Jacob and the Eastern land by interpreting them in an allegorical sense. It remains, 496Btherefore, that we go over it again in like manner, and — not as is worthy, but as the thinness of our understanding can in some fashion take it in — seek out the sweetly fragrant flowers of a second allegory. For the divine page is a certain field overflowing with flowers, in which the faithful soul ought to take delight, gathering the various flowers of allegory, that is, meditating upon the spiritual senses. Whence David the prophet says: Delight thou in the Lord, and he will give thee the petitions of thy heart (Psal. XXXVI.) The Eastern land, therefore, is justly called the heavenly Jerusalem, namely the dwelling of the citizens on high, of which the Psalmist says: My portion in the land of the living (Psal. CXLI.) To which land whoever shall be able to come by working well shall not taste death any more; because then every holy man is born again in God by an eternal birth, when his temporal life is ended by a 496Cconditional death. For this cause an elegant custom has grown up in holy Church, that we proclaim the birthdays of the saints then, when we learn that they have passed out of this light. Who then is that Jacob, who is interpreted wrestler, save our spirit 290, upon whom a wrestling against spiritual wickednesses is enjoined without interval? Let our Jacob, therefore, journey to the land of the East, that is, let our spirit hasten with the steps of good working to the land of promise: to which land of promise Paul was sighing, when he said: Let us hasten to enter into that rest (Hebr. IV). But as we hasten toward the heavenly Jerusalem, a well which is closed with a great stone meets us, because, as David the prophet says: The judgments of God are a great 496Ddeep (Psal. XXXV). [Vulg. Psal. XXXV, 7 judicia tua, "thy judgments"] With whom Solomon agrees: All things, he says, are reserved uncertain for man after death (Eccle. IX). [Vulg. Eccle. IX, 2 sed omnia in futurum servantur incerta, "but all things are kept uncertain for the time to come"] But what do we say that this well is, which is closed with a great stone, save the hidden and terrible counsel of God, which is in no wise made manifest until the day of judgment? At which the prophet, in dread, says: Come and see the works of God, terrible in his counsels over the sons of men (Psal. LXV). And what do the shepherds there with their flocks signify, who lie down about the well, save the faithful souls both of prelates and of subjects, who await the judgment of God? Whence it is well said that, when the shepherds had been gathered together with their flocks, then the stone was removed from the mouth of the well, and so the sheep were watered: because, when all men come to the judgment of God, his terrible counsel, 497Awhich is now hidden, shall be made manifest. Then at last shall the sheep be watered with the water of the unsealed well, when every man shall be damned or saved according to the just judgment of the hidden counsel of God: Go, ye cursed, into everlasting fire which is prepared (Matt. XXV); [Vulg. Matth. XXV, 41 Discedite a me maledicti in ignem aeternum, qui paratus est diabolo, "Depart from me, ye cursed, into everlasting fire, which was prepared for the devil"] but to those who shall be on the right hand: Come, ye blessed of my Father, receive the kingdom (ibid.) [Vulg. Matth. XXV, 34 possidete paratum vobis regnum, "possess the kingdom prepared for you"] And so in a wonderful and unspeakable manner shall Jacob know again the face of his kinswoman Rachel, when our spirit shall look upon the contemplative life in the heavens, for which it long sighed while yet set upon the earth. And thence it is that Rachel is interpreted a beginning seen, because then not in a riddle nor through a glass, but face to face (I Cor. XIII) shall our spirit contemplate God himself. But the kiss which Jacob gave to Rachel 497Bwe can interpret under that saying of the Lord in which it is said: Father, I will that, as I and thou are one, so they also may be one in us (Joan. XVIII). [Vulg. Joan. XVII, 21 ut omnes unum sint, sicut tu Pater in me, et ego in te, ut et ipsi in nobis unum sint, "that they all may be one, as thou, Father, in me, and I in thee"] Clearer than light, therefore, it stands, that when one gives to another the kiss of peace, without the setting of any thing between, he joins mouth to mouth with a charitable affection. Hence it is that the blessed John says: We are the sons of God, and it doth not yet appear what we shall be; but we know that, when he shall appear, we shall be like to him, because we shall see him as he is (Joan. III). [Vulg. I Joan. III, 2 nondum apparuit, "it hath not yet appeared"] There shall not be, therefore, between the Creator and the creature any opposition; otherwise it would not be true and perfect blessedness.

RECAPITULATIO A SUPERIORIBUS SECUNDUM ETHICAM, Id est moralem intelligentiam.

497CAd moralitatem accedentes, et de moralitate disserere cupientes, morigeratos nos oportet esse, neque enim de moribus hominum possumus eleganter quidquam loqui, nisi parte ex aliqua fuerimus moribus instructi vel adornati. Superius praefati sumus (nisi forte a mente nostra exciderit) quod liber iste a perfectione, hoc est ab O littera, quae perfectionem significat, initium sumit. Quae littera secundum Latinos, ut philosophi aiunt, totius orbis formam in seipsa insinuat, et secundum Graecos formata sanctam Trinitatem pro modulo suo nobis repraesentat. Qui ergo istum librum legis vel audis, perfectioni stude, perfectus esse satage. Audi non me, sed Dominum in Evangelio dicentem: Perfecti 497Destote, sicut et Pater vester perfectus est qui in coelis est (Matth. V.) Verumtamen perfectus esse nullatenus poteris, nisi summae perfectioni, quae Deus est, remota iniquitate perfecte adhaeseris. Quam utique perfectionem David desiderabat qui dicebat: Mihi autem adhaerere Deo bonum est (Psal. LXXII.) Perfecte ergo perfectus esse potes, si fidem sanctae Trinitatis corde credis et retines, et eamdem fidem operibus bonis adimples. Perfecte, inquam, perfectum te Deo in die judicii repraesentabis, si in hoc saeculo, quantum vales, ab omni peccato immunem te custodis. Sed quia, ut beatus Gregorius ait, bonum opus nihil valet sine castitate, sicut nec castitas aliquid valet sine bono opere, ideo bene quidem in nostra recapitulatione sequantur lilia virginitatem, 498Acastitatemque, et munditiam significant. Sunt praeterea quidam qui virgines et casti sunt, sed mundi non sunt, quia de virginitatis seu castitatis custodia insolenter superbiunt. Eva enim prima parens nostra, corpore quidem, virgo et casta fuit, sed mente immunda exstitit, quia magis diabolo falsa promittenti, quam Deo vera comminanti obedivit. Inde dicit divina Scriptura: Qui exaltat cor suum, mundus est coram Deo, nec prodest quidquam virginem et castum esse corpore, si fuerit incestus in mente. Refloreant ergo in nobis lilia spiritualia, scilicet virginitatis et castitatis et munditiae cordis insignia, quatenus corpore et anima castificati, illa evangelica benedictione mereamur insigniri, qua nobilitantur illi quibus a Domino dicitur: Beati 498Bmundo corde, quoniam ipsi Deum 291 videbunt (Matth. V.) Unde merito violae in ordine recapitulationis sequuntur, quae aetherea claritate refulgent, quia illi bene castitatem custodiunt, qui per illius observantiam pervenire quaerunt ad coelestis regni gloriam. Flores autem caeteri qui in hac expositione speciali nomine non exprimuntur, reliquas virtutes decenter insinuant, quibus supradictae virtutes exornantur. Sine enim humilitate, obedientia, charitate, patientia, longanimitate, perseverantia, prudentia, et discretione, quae mater est omnium virtutum, nullus potest perfectionem retinere, nec pacem, sive concordiam (quae designantur per osculum Sponsi et Sponsae) perfecte possidere.

RECAPITULATION FROM THE FOREGOING ACCORDING TO ETHICS, That is, the moral understanding.

497CComing to morality, and desiring to discourse of morality, we must be men of good manners; for neither can we say anything elegantly of the manners of men, unless in some part we have been instructed or adorned with manners. We said above (unless perchance it has slipped from our mind) that this book takes its beginning from perfection, that is, from the letter O, which signifies perfection. Which letter, according to the Latins, as the philosophers say, intimates in itself the form of the whole world; and, formed according to the Greeks, represents to us, after its little measure, the holy Trinity. Thou, therefore, who readest or hearest this book, be zealous for perfection, be busy to be perfect. Hear not me, but the Lord saying in the Gospel: Be 497Dye perfect, as your Father also is perfect who is in the heavens (Matth. V.) [Vulg. Matth. V, 48 Estote ergo vos perfecti, sicut et Pater vester caelestis perfectus est, "Be you therefore perfect, as also your heavenly Father is perfect"] Nevertheless thou shalt in no wise be able to be perfect, unless, iniquity being put away, thou hast perfectly cleaved to that highest perfection which is God. Which perfection assuredly David desired, who said: But for me it is good to cleave to God (Psal. LXXII.) Perfectly, therefore, canst thou be perfect, if thou believest and holdest in thy heart the faith of the holy Trinity, and fulfillest that same faith with good works. Perfectly, I say, shalt thou present thyself perfect to God in the day of judgment, if in this world, so far as thou art able, thou keepest thyself free from all sin. But since, as the blessed Gregory says, a good work avails nothing without chastity, as neither does chastity avail anything without a good work, therefore rightly indeed in our recapitulation let the lilies follow, they signify virginity 498Aand chastity and cleanness. There are, moreover, certain men who are virgins and chaste, but are not clean, because they are insolently proud of the keeping of their virginity or chastity. For Eve, our first parent, was indeed in body a virgin and chaste, but she stood forth unclean in mind, because she obeyed the devil promising falsehoods rather than God threatening truly. Thence the Holy Scripture says: He that exalteth his heart is clean before God; nor does it profit anything to be a virgin and chaste in body, if one has been unchaste in mind. [Vulg. Prov. XVI, 5 abominatio Domini est omnis arrogans; the maxim as commonly cited runs immundus est coram Deo omnis qui exaltat cor suum, "unclean before God"] Let there flower again in us, therefore, the spiritual lilies, namely the marks of virginity and of chastity and of cleanness of heart, so that, made chaste in body and soul, we may deserve to be marked with that evangelical blessing by which those are ennobled to whom it is said by the Lord: Blessed 498Bare the clean of heart, for they themselves 291 shall see God (Matth. V.) Whence deservedly the violets follow in the order of the recapitulation, which shine again with an airy brightness, because those keep chastity well who through the observance of it seek to come to the glory of the heavenly kingdom. But the other flowers which in this exposition are not expressed by a special name fittingly intimate the remaining virtues, by which the aforesaid virtues are adorned. For without humility, obedience, charity, patience, long-suffering, perseverance, prudence, and discretion, which is the mother of all the virtues, no man can hold fast perfection, nor perfectly possess peace or concord (which are betokened by the kiss of the Bridegroom and the Bride).

PRIMA MORALITAS DE JACOB.

498CIn veteri lege divina Scriptura nos edocet (Levit. I), ut qui de grege arietem, vel quodlibet animal sacrificiis aptum Deo offert, prius pellem detrahat, secundo carnem sacris ignibus admoveat, tertio adipes et ea quae adipibus velantur, in holocaustum Domino offerat. Aries quippe sive vitula, est quaelibet sententia, ex Veteri Testamento excepta. Cui sententiae quasi pellem detrahimus, cum historicam intelligentiam pertractantes, veram esse sancimus. Caro autem tunc sacris ignibus traditur, cum allegoria spiritualibus sensibus intellecta delucidatur. Adipes vero, et ea quae infra sunt, Deo offerre nos arbitramur, cum moralitatem, et illa quae de moribus pendent, ad utilitatem fidelium animarum, et 498Dlaudem ipsius Dei perscrutamur. Quoniam igitur in superiori tractatu historialem Jacob allegorico et spirituali sensu interpretati sumus, quasi pelle subtracta, carnes sacro igne concremavimus. Superest ergo ut adipes igni divino admoveamus, id est ut moralitatem morali et excelsiori intellectu in lucem proferamus. Caro equidem nostra tunc terra Orientalis efficitur, cum in illa vitia mortificantur, et virtutes spirituales oriuntur. Quam profecto Jacob spiritualis, id est Christus invisere dignabitur, si talis fuerit, ut ejus visitatione digna habeatur. Puteus autem ille ad quem historialis Jacob venit, cor nostrum significavit, quod si perfecte Deum dilexerit, et sermonem ejus servaverit, Christus ad illud veniet, et in illo manebit, unde ipse in Evangelio 499Adicit: Si quis diligit me, sermonem meum servabit, et Pater meus diliget eum, et ad eum veniemus, et mansionem apud eum faciemus (Joan. XIV). Lapis vero quo puteus clauditur, recte duritia cordis nostri intelligitur. Quam nimirum duritiam Dominus se nobis auferre pie repromittit, cum per Ezechielem prophetam dicit (cap. XI et XXXVI): Tollam a vobis cor lapideum, et dabo vobis cor carneum, et faciam ut in praeceptis meis ambuletis. Tres greges ovium cum pastoribus suis qui juxta puteum a Jacob reperiuntur, tria genera cogitationum sunt, quae circa cor nostrum versantur. Recordamur enim in cogitationibus nostris plerumque malorum quae fecimus, praesentia Deo auxiliante praecavemus, pro futuris, ne nos dimersos obruant, Deum exoramus. Et bene 499Bquidem historia refert quod remoto lapide greges illi adaquabantur, quia ablata cordis nostri duritia praefatae cogitationes spirituali compunctione et salubri lacrymarum irroratione debriantur. Pastores autem qui custodiunt supradictas oves, fides, spes, charitas sunt. quae nostras simplices enutriunt cogitationes. Liquet ergo quia qui pro peccatis praeteritis luget, praesentia cavet, pro futuris exorando timet, omni modo ab omni peccato immunem se exhibet. Sic demum ad illam virtutem quae impeccantia dicitur, redit, in qua Deus ad imaginem suam primum hominem creavit. Eleganti denique stylo historia describit, quoniam quidem ibi Jacob consobrinam suam Rachelem recognovit, quia sine dubio tunc perfectius creatura recognoscitur a Creatore 499Csuo, quando secundum suum posse, homo se observat a peccato. Unde merito Rachel, ovis vel visum principium interpretatur, quia sic plane creatura ad initium suae creationis revertitur, cum per simplicitatem et impeccantiam sine naevo peccati, suo Creatori repraesentatur. Fletus vero quo Jacob visa Rachel flevit, significat gaudium ineffabile et jubilationem incomprehensibilem, quam Deus apparens fidelibus suis donabit. Plerumque enim prae gaudio nimio solemus flere, sicut iste Jacob agnita consobrina sua Rachel prae exsultatione dignoscitur flevisse. Osculum autem quod Jacob Racheli donat, unitatem illam qua uniti ipsi Deo erimus, designat. De qua nimirum unitate Paulus apostolus mihi videtur dicere: Qui adhaeret Deo, unus spiritus efficitur 499Dcum ipso (I Cor. VI). Praeterea hoc quod historia non praetermittit, quia Jacob Racheli quod consanguineus ejus esset indicavit, non incongrue ita interpretari possumus, quia finito judicio nostram substantiam in nostro Creatore recognoscemus, quam dudum, nostri misertus assumpsit, cum de beata Virgine Maria nasci voluit.

THE FIRST MORALITY CONCERNING JACOB.

498CIn the old law the Holy Scripture teaches us (Levit. I) that he who offers to God from the flock a ram, or any animal fit for sacrifices, should first draw off the skin, secondly bring the flesh to the sacred fires, thirdly offer the fat and those things which are veiled by the fat as a holocaust to the Lord. For the ram or the heifer is any sentence whatever taken out of the Old Testament. From which sentence we draw off, as it were, the skin, when, handling the historical understanding, we establish it to be true. And the flesh is then delivered to the sacred fires, when the allegory, understood in spiritual senses, is made clear. But the fat, and those things which are beneath, we judge that we offer to God, when we search out the morality, and those things which hang upon manners, for the profit of the souls of the faithful and for 498Dthe praise of God himself. Since, therefore, in the earlier treatise we interpreted the historical Jacob in an allegorical and spiritual sense, we have, as it were, with the skin drawn off, burned up the flesh with sacred fire. It remains, therefore, that we bring the fat to the divine fire, that is, that we bring forth into the light the morality with a moral and loftier understanding. Our flesh indeed is then made the Eastern land, when in it the vices are mortified and the spiritual virtues arise. Which land assuredly the spiritual Jacob, that is, Christ, will deign to visit, if it shall be such as to be held worthy of his visitation. But that well to which the historical Jacob came signified our heart; which, if it shall perfectly have loved God and kept his word, Christ will come to it and will abide in it, whence he himself in the Gospel 499Asays: If any man love me, he will keep my word, and my Father will love him, and we will come to him, and will make our abode with him (Joan. XIV). And the stone with which the well is closed is rightly understood as the hardness of our heart. Which hardness indeed the Lord lovingly promises that he will take away from us, when he says through Ezekiel the prophet (cap. XI et XXXVI): I will take away from you the stony heart, and I will give you a heart of flesh, and I will cause you to walk in my precepts. [Vulg. Ezech. XXXVI, 26 auferam cor lapideum de carne vestra, "I will take away the stony heart out of your flesh"] The three flocks of sheep with their shepherds which are found by Jacob beside the well are the three kinds of thoughts which turn about our heart. For in our thoughts we for the most part remember the evils which we have done; the present we guard against with God's help; and for the things to come, lest they overwhelm us and sink us down, we entreat God. And well 499Bindeed does the history relate that, when the stone was removed, those flocks were watered; because, when the hardness of our heart has been taken away, the aforesaid thoughts are made drunk with spiritual compunction and with the healthful bedewing of tears. And the shepherds who guard the aforesaid sheep are faith, hope, charity. which nourish our simple thoughts. It is clear, therefore, that he who mourns for past sins, guards against present ones, and fears for those to come by entreating, shows himself in every way free from all sin. So at last he returns to that virtue which is called sinlessness, in which God created the first man after his own image. Finally, the history describes with an elegant style how indeed there Jacob knew again his cousin Rachel; because without doubt the creature is then more perfectly known again by its Creator 499C, when, according to his power, a man keeps himself from sin. Whence deservedly Rachel is interpreted a sheep or a beginning seen, because so plainly does the creature return to the beginning of its creation, when through simplicity and sinlessness, without the blemish of sin, it is presented to its Creator. But the weeping with which Jacob wept when he saw Rachel signifies the unspeakable joy and the incomprehensible jubilation which God, appearing, will give to his faithful ones. For very often we are wont to weep for exceeding joy, just as this Jacob, when he recognized his cousin Rachel, is known to have wept for exultation. But the kiss which Jacob gives to Rachel betokens that unity by which we shall be united to God himself. Of which unity indeed the apostle Paul seems to me to speak: He that cleaveth to God is made one spirit 499Dwith him (I Cor. VI). [Vulg. I Cor. VI, 17 Qui autem adhaeret Domino, unus spiritus est, "but he who cleaveth to the Lord is one spirit"] Moreover, this which the history does not pass over — that Jacob made known to Rachel that he was her kinsman — we can not unfittingly interpret thus: that, the judgment being ended, we shall know again our own substance in our Creator, which long since, having pity on us, he took up, when he willed to be born of the blessed Virgin Mary.

292 INCIPIT MORALITAS SECUNDA FLORIBUS ALLEGORIAE INTERMISTA.

De Isaac patriarcha textus historiae nostris auribus retulit, quomodo olim, ut foderent puteum, servis suis praecepit. Qui altius fodientes, aquam abundanter repererunt, et propterea puteum illum Abundantiam appellaverunt. Quid namque puteus 500Aille quem foderunt pueri Isaac, praetendebat, nisi Vetus Testamentum, quod, antequam exponeretur a doctoribus, terra obice obstrusum manebat? Spirituali ergo sarculo puteum istum fodiamus, et sine dubio aquas abundantes, id est sensus spirituales reperiemus. In praecedenti tractatu superius de Jacob allegorice et moraliter locuti sumus; nunc vero ad eumdem Jacob spirituali itinere redeamus, et in eo secundam moralitatem altiori intellectu perquiramus. Quae moralitas interdum quibusdam allegoricis floribus fit intermista, ne a prudentibus et strenuis viris judicetur esse inculta. Intuere, o homo, temetipsum, et te considerato, invenies inter te, per figuram, totum mundum, et ut audacius loquar, si bene vixeris, ipsum Creatorem tuum. Si 500Bergo tantae dignitatis est homo, ut non solum totius orbis, verum etiam ipsius Dei similitudinem quodammodo teneat in seipso, ad imaginem quippe et similitudinem Dei factus est homo (Gen. I), non debet aliquibus absurdum videri, si spiritum vel animam nostram alternantibus vicibus, modo Jacob, modo terram Orientalem volumus interpretari.

Dicamus quod spiritus noster ipse sit terra Orientalis, et interim Jacob spiritualis. Sicut enim Jacob historialis cum Angelo luctatus est, et contra eum invaluit, et quomodo allegoricus Jacob, id est Christus mortem et diabolum triumphans, in ligno suae crucis superavit; sic moralis Jacob, id est spiritus noster debet sibi crucifigere mundum istum, et pugnare contra se ipsum, ut dominans animo suo, 500Cfortior ut expugnatore urbium. Crucifigere, inquam, debet sibi mundum homo spiritualis, ut dicere queat cum apostolo Paulo: Mihi mundus crucifixus est, et ego mundo (Galat. VI). Debet, inquam, ultra vires laborare interior homo, ut vineat seipsum et mundum, animatus exemplo Domini Jesu, qui ait: Confidite quia ego vici mundum (Joan. XVI). Nec minus summopere studeat ut terra Orientalis effici queat, de qua assidue vitia exstirpentur, et in qua spirituales virtutes oriantur. Via autem spiritualis Jacob, id est spiritus nostri, Christus recte debet intelligi, qui ait: Ego sum via (Joan. XIV). Via, inquam, quae ducit ad Deum recta, ipsa est simplex obedientia. Unde et secundum historiam Jacob matri suae Rebeccae 500Dobediebat, quando viam istam de qua loquimur pergebat. Non est igitur rectior via obedientia, et non est vera et recta obedientia, nisi in ipso qui dixit: Ego sum veritas et vita (ibid.). Locus ille ad quem Jacob venisse prima mansione perhibetur, id est domus Dei, vel Luza, hoc est nux Beth-el vel amygdalum interpretatur. Spiritus ergo noster orationibus assiduis intentus, domus Dei efficiatur, quia sic Dominus dicit: Domus mea, domus orationis vocabitur (Matth. XXI). Verumtamen qui domus Dei effici optat, nux vel amygdalum fieri non renuat. Neque enim potest spiritus noster esse habitaculum Dei sine fortitudine et patientia, longanimitate et prudentia; quod significat horum fructuum durissima et pene indomabilis testa. Quod autem Jacob 501Avoluit requiescere post solis occasum, significat quod spiritus noster tunc perfecte requiescit in Deum, cum in se mortificat et occidit omne quod pertinet ad peccatum. Lapides vero quos collegit et capiti suo supposuit, designant exempla sanctorum quae nos in capite, id est in mente nostra recondentes, et secundum ipsa viventes, perfecte contemnimus mundum. Quod autem sancti, lapides sint appellati, testatur Petrus apostolus qui dicit: Et vos tanquam lapides vivi superaedificamini in habitaculo Spiritus sancti (I Petr. II). Nec mirum sane, cum ipse Dominus Jesus in David (Psal. CXVII) lapis angularis sit appellatus, et in Daniele: Lapis abcissus de monte sine manibus (Dan. II). Jacob obdormitio praetendit quod nos debemus perfecte renuntiare 501Bmundo et adhaerere Christo; vel significat contemplationem summae Divinitatis et oblivionem totius saecularis vanitatis. Scala e duobus lignis consistens quam Jacob in summis vidit, cujus cacumen coeli secreta penetravit, duo Testamenta sunt, quae nos docent et instruunt, et ad cognitionem divinae majestatis perducunt. Unde bene historia dicit, quia Jacob Dominum in summitate scalae innixum vidit. Nulla enim ratione possumus pervenire ad ipsum Dominum, nisi pro modulo nostro spiritualiter impleverimus mandata duorum Testamentorum. O quam magna est clementia nostri Redemptoris Quid enim innixio Dei in summitate scalae praefigurabat, nisi descensionem ipsius ad redemptionem generis humani, quam tunc ex eo 501Ctempore jam quodammodo praeparabat? Lapis ille quem Jacob peracto somno unxit prae caeteris lapidibus, significavit Christum prae omnibus sanctis impletum Spiritus sancti charismatibus.

293 De qua unctione spirituali et incomprehensibili infusione Spiritus sancti, David propheta loquitur ad Filium Dei: Quia tu justitiam dilexisti, et iniquitatem odio habuisti, idcirco unxit te Deus Pater tuus oleo jubilationis prae omnibus sanctis tuis (Psal. XLIV). Lapis vero quem Jacob unxit et erexit in titulum, significat quod Christus per Incarnationis mysterium in sancta Ecclesia locum pontificalis dignitatis debebat obtinere, et per universum mundum regiae auctoritatis sceptra possidere. Unde merito 501DJacob dicit: Quia vere Dominus est in loco isto (Gen. XXVIII), quia extra catholicam Ecclesiam nullus potest salvari, nec placere Deo. Praeterea si istam scalam juxta moralitatem interpretari volumus, non incongrue per duo ligna ipsius, fidem et spem intelligimus, quae quasi in terra nos accipiunt, et usque ad coeli secreta nos sublevatos intromittunt. In hoc siquidem saeculo fides et spes nobis necessariae sunt, quae in coelis nullum opus habebunt (Rom. VIII), quia, sicut dicit Scriptura: Deum in quem credimus et speramus, et quem per patientiam exspectamus, in futuro saeculo sicuti est videbimus (I Joan. III), et ideo ulterius fide et spe opus non habemus. Scaliones qui duobus lignis hujus scalae inserti sunt, reliquae virtutes intelligi possunt, sine quibus fides et 502Aspes prorsus inutiles existunt. Sicut enim corpus nostrum sine anima moritur, sic fides sine operibus, testante Jacobo, mortua appellatur (Jacob. II). Et ideo quidem bene post fidem et spem in ordine charitas sequitur, quae in duo, hoc est dilectionem Dei et proximi dividitur. Qui enim solummodo in Deum credit vel sperat, et nec tamen ipsum Deum diligit, nec proximum suum pro Deo amat, et insuper quod bonum est operari, renuit; patet profecto quia daemonibus similis existit. De quibus Jacobus apostolus dicit: Daemones credunt et contremiscunt (ibid.). Ponamus ergo hujus scalae quae ad coelum ducit primum scalionem, humilitatem Dei et proximi dilectionem, bonas cogitationes, meliores locutiones, optimas operationes, corporis et animae castitatem, cordis 502Bmunditiam, pacem fraternam, concordiam, confessionem, poenitentiam, corporalem disciplinam, jejunia, vigilias, orationes, corporis pro Deo exercitia, et in lege Dei spirituales meditationes, contemptum mundi, despectum corporis nostri, spiritualia desideria, et in summitate hujus scalae ponamus obedientiam, quia ipsa est quae nos ducit ad supernae majestatis contemplationem. Sic enim dicit divina Scriptura de obedientia: Per obedientiam perveniunt ad occultorum Dei notitiam (II Cor. II). Ascendat ergo noster spiritus per istam scalam: De virtute in virtutem, donec videat Deum deorsum in Sion regnantem (Psal. LXXXIII). Propterea denique et Dominus, obedientiae, quae in summitate hujus scalae posita est, videtur esse innixus, quia per obedientiam 502Cdisponebat genus humanum sibi reconciliare, et juxta Paulum apostolum (Coloss. I), quae in coelo sunt et quae in terra, spirituali concordia pacificare, unde idem Paulus dicit: Christus factus est obediens Patri usque ad mortem (Philipp. II). Et alio in loco: Ex his quae passus est, cum esset Filius Dei didicit obedientiam (Hebr. V). Quod autem Jacob evigilans ait: Vere Dominus est in loco isto (Gen. XXVIII), liquido denuntiat quia tunc Deus vere habitat in corde nostro, si praefatam scalam spiritus noster in se perfecte possederit, et mens nostra spretis saeculi vanitatibus in amorem Dei dulci sopore obdormierit. Enimvero lapis unctus esse moraliter possumus, si unctionem a Spiritu sancto susceperimus, sicut illi acceperunt quibus Joannes 502Dapostolus dicebat: Et vos unctionem a Spiritu sancto accepistis, et, ut aliquis doceat vos, necesse non habetis, quia unctio ejus docet vos de omnibus (II Joan. II). Ager autem, in quo a Jacob puteus reperitur, non incongrue spiritus vel anima nostra intelligitur, si tamen odorifera suavitate spiritualium florum fuerit refertus, sicut fuerat ille qui dicebat: Christi bonus odor sumus (II Cor. II). Puteus vero in hoc loco debet intelligi Spiritus sanctus, de quo in Evangelio Dominus Jesus dicebat: Si quis sitit, veniat ad me et bibat, et de ventre ejus fluent aquae vivae. Hoc autem dicebat de Spiritu sancto quem accepturi erant credentes in eum (Joan. VII).

Ipse, inquam, Spiritus sanctus est fons hortorum 503Aputeus aquarum viventium quae fluunt impetu de Libano (Cant. IV), id est sanctitas, refectio, purificatio, mundatio, gaudium et jubilatio fidelium animarum, quae desiderant esse cum Christo. Laboret ergo anima nostra ut possideat in se puteum istum, hoc est satagat bonis operibus fieri Spiritus sancti habitaculum. Verumtamen aquae hujus putei quae fluere de Libano dicuntur, hoc designant, quod Spiritus sanctus non nisi corpore casto et corde purificato possidetur. Unde et idem mons Libanus candidatio interpretatur. Quod autem puteus ille grandi lapide refertur fuisse clausus, eleganti sermone declarat quod in corpore subdito peccatis nullatenus habitat (Sap. I) Spiritus sanctus. Lapis enim ingens est moles nostrorum criminum, quae procul 503Bdubio ne videatur, abscondit a nobis Spiritum sanctum. Unde et propheta dicit: Non est invalida 504Amanus Domini ad salvandum, sed peccata nostra fecerunt murum inter vos et Deum (Isa. LIX). Sed et hoc sub negligentia non est praetermittendum quod Sapientia dicit: Quia Spiritus sanctus disciplinae effugiet fictum (Sap. I). Removeatur igitur paries nostrorum criminum, sive lapis molaris, et ambiatur ac teneatur a nobis disciplina spiritualis, id est confiteamur peccata 294 nostra, et subdamus nos castigationi spirituali, ut queamus esse sacrosancta habitatio Spiritus sancti. Sit, inquam, spiritus noster ager omnigeno virtutum flore vernans; reperiatur in eo a Jacob puteus salutaria fluenta emanans, id est a Christo Spiritus sanctus inveniatur habitator existens. Cujus praesentia liquido sic manifestabitur, si in omnibus mansueti et humiles et pacifici 504Bfuerimus, et fructibus bonae operationis cum castitate et sobrietate abundaverimus.

503Explicit primus tomus, id est primum volumen in Cantica canticorum.

292 HERE BEGINS THE SECOND MORALITY, INTERMINGLED WITH FLOWERS OF ALLEGORY.

Concerning Isaac the patriarch the text of the history has told to our ears how of old he commanded his servants to dig a well. And they, digging deeper, found water abundantly, and therefore called that well Abundance. For what did that well 500Awhich the servants of Isaac dug set forth, save the Old Testament, which, before it was expounded by the doctors, remained shut up by a barrier of earth? Let us therefore dig this well with a spiritual hoe, and without doubt we shall find abounding waters, that is, the spiritual senses. In the preceding treatise we spoke above of Jacob allegorically and morally; but now let us return by a spiritual road to the same Jacob, and let us seek out in him a second morality with a loftier understanding. Which morality is here and there intermingled with certain allegorical flowers, lest it be judged by prudent and vigorous men to be uncultivated. Behold thyself, O man, and, when thou art considered, thou shalt find in thyself, by a figure, the whole world, and — that I may speak more boldly — if thou hast lived well, thy very Creator himself. If, 500Btherefore, man is of so great dignity that he holds in himself, in a certain manner, the likeness not only of the whole world, but even of God himself — for man was made to the image and likeness of God (Gen. I) — it ought not to seem absurd to any if we wish to interpret our spirit or soul, by alternating turns, now as Jacob, now as the Eastern land.

Let us say that our spirit is itself the Eastern land, and meanwhile the spiritual Jacob. For as the historical Jacob wrestled with the Angel and prevailed against him; and as the allegorical Jacob, that is, Christ, triumphing over death and the devil, overcame them on the wood of his cross; so the moral Jacob, that is, our spirit, ought to crucify to itself this world, and to fight against itself, so that, ruling over its own mind, 500Cstronger as the stormer of cities. Crucify to himself the world, I say, must the spiritual man, that he may be able to say with the apostle Paul: The world is crucified to me, and I to the world (Galat. VI). He must, I say, labour beyond his strength, the inward man, that he may overcome vineat himself and the world, quickened by the example of the Lord Jesus, who says: Have confidence, for I have overcome the world (Joan. XVI). Nor let him strive with less than the utmost care, that he may be able to be made the Eastern land, out of which the vices are ceaselessly rooted up, and in which the spiritual virtues arise. And the way of the spiritual Jacob, that is, of our spirit, ought rightly to be understood as Christ, who says: I am the way (Joan. XIV). The way, I say, which leads straight to God, is simple obedience itself. Whence also, according to the history, Jacob was obeying his mother Rebecca 500Dwhen he was going upon that way of which we speak. There is, therefore, no straighter way than obedience; and there is no true and straight obedience save in him who said: I am the truth and the life (ibid.). That place to which Jacob is reported to have come at his first lodging, that is, the house of God, or Luz, is interpreted the nut of Beth-el or the almond. Let our spirit, therefore, intent upon assiduous prayers, be made the house of God, because the Lord says thus: My house shall be called the house of prayer (Matth. XXI). Nevertheless, let him who desires to be made the house of God not refuse to become a nut or an almond. For neither can our spirit be the dwelling-place of God without fortitude and patience, long-suffering and prudence; which the hardest and almost unconquerable shell of these fruits signifies. And that Jacob 501Awilled to rest after the setting of the sun signifies that our spirit then perfectly rests in God, when it mortifies and kills in itself everything that belongs to sin. And the stones which he gathered and put under his head betoken the examples of the saints, which, laying them up in our head, that is, in our mind, and living according to them, we perfectly despise the world. And that the saints are called stones, the apostle Peter testifies, who says: And you, as living stones, be built up into a dwelling-place of the Holy Spirit (I Petr. II). [Vulg. I Petr. II, 5 superaedificamini domus spiritualis, "be built up a spiritual house"] Nor is it any wonder, indeed, since the Lord Jesus himself is called in David (Psal. CXVII) the corner stone, and in Daniel: A stone cut out of the mountain without hands (Dan. II). Jacob's falling asleep sets forth that we ought perfectly to renounce 501Bthe world and to cleave to Christ; or it signifies the contemplation of the highest Godhead and the forgetting of all worldly vanity. The ladder standing upon two pieces of wood, which Jacob saw in the heights, whose top pierced the secret places of heaven, is the two Testaments, which teach and instruct us, and lead us through to the knowledge of the divine majesty. Whence the history says well, that Jacob saw the Lord leaning upon the top of the ladder. For by no reasoning can we come to the Lord himself, unless, after our little measure, we have spiritually fulfilled the commandments of the two Testaments. O how great is the clemency of our Redeemer What then did God's leaning upon the top of the ladder prefigure, save his own coming down for the redemption of the human race, which even then from that 501Ctime he was already in some manner preparing?
