That stone which Jacob, when his sleep was done, anointed before the other stones, signified Christ, filled before all the saints with the charisms of the Holy Spirit.

293 Of which spiritual anointing and incomprehensible pouring-in of the Holy Spirit, David the prophet speaks to the Son of God: Because thou hast loved justice and hast held iniquity in hatred, therefore God thy Father hath anointed thee with the oil of jubilation above all thy saints (Psal. XLIV). [Vulg. Psal. XLIV, 8 unxit te Deus, Deus tuus, oleo laetitiae prae consortibus tuis, "God, thy God, hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows"] And the stone which Jacob anointed and set up for a title signifies that Christ, through the mystery of the Incarnation, was to obtain in holy Church the place of the pontifical dignity, and to possess throughout the whole world the sceptres of royal authority. Whence deservedly 501DJacob says: That truly the Lord is in this place (Gen. XXVIII), because outside the catholic Church no one can be saved, nor please God. Moreover, if we wish to interpret this ladder according to morality, not unfittingly do we understand by its two pieces of wood faith and hope, which as it were take us up upon the earth and, lifting us up, bring us in even to the secret places of heaven. For in this world faith and hope are necessary to us, which in the heavens shall have no work (Rom. VIII); because, as the Scripture says, God, in whom we believe and hope, and whom we await through patience, in the world to come as he is we shall see (I Joan. III), and therefore we have no further need of faith and hope. The rungs which are set into the two pieces of wood of this ladder can be understood as the remaining virtues, without which faith and 502Ahope stand utterly useless. For as our body dies without the soul, so faith without works, James bearing witness, is called dead (Jacob. II). And therefore rightly indeed after faith and hope charity follows in the order, which is divided into two, that is, into the love of God and of neighbour. For he who only believes in God or hopes in him, and yet neither loves God himself, nor loves his neighbour for God's sake, and moreover refuses to work that which is good, it plainly appears that he stands like the demons. Of whom the apostle James says: The demons believe and tremble (ibid.). Let us set, therefore, as the first rung of this ladder which leads to heaven, humility, the love of God and of neighbour, good thoughts, better speech, best works, chastity of body and of soul, cleanness 502Bof heart, brotherly peace, concord, confession, penance, bodily discipline, fasts, vigils, prayers, exercises of the body for God's sake, and spiritual meditations in the law of God, contempt of the world, disdain of our body, spiritual desires; and at the top of this ladder let us set obedience, because it is she who leads us to the contemplation of the majesty on high. For thus the Holy Scripture says of obedience: Through obedience they come to the knowledge of the hidden things of God (II Cor. II). Let our spirit ascend, therefore, by this ladder: From virtue to virtue, until it see God, downward in Sion, reigning (Psal. LXXXIII). [Vulg. Psal. LXXXIII, 8 videbitur Deus deorum in Sion, "the God of gods shall be seen in Sion"] For this cause, finally, the Lord also seems to be leaning upon obedience, which is set at the top of this ladder, because through obedience 502Che was disposing to reconcile the human race to himself, and, according to the apostle Paul (Coloss. I), to make peace by a spiritual concord between the things that are in heaven and the things that are on earth; whence the same Paul says: Christ was made obedient to the Father unto death (Philipp. II). [Vulg. Philipp. II, 8 factus obediens usque ad mortem, without Patri] And in another place: From those things which he suffered, though he was the Son of God, he learned obedience (Hebr. V). And that Jacob, awaking, says: Truly the Lord is in this place (Gen. XXVIII), plainly declares that God then truly dwells in our heart, if our spirit shall have perfectly possessed the aforesaid ladder in itself, and our mind, the vanities of the world being spurned, shall have fallen asleep in the love of God with a sweet slumber. Indeed we can morally be the anointed stone, if we have received the anointing from the Holy Spirit, as those received it to whom John 502Dthe apostle was saying: And you have received the anointing from the Holy Spirit, and you have no need that any man teach you, because his anointing teacheth you concerning all things (II Joan. II). [Vulg. I Joan. II, 27 Et vos unctionem, quam accepistis ab eo, maneat in vobis, "and as for you, let the unction which you have received from him abide in you"] And the field in which the well is found by Jacob is not unfittingly understood as our spirit or soul, if only it shall have been filled with the fragrant sweetness of spiritual flowers, as was he who said: We are the good odour of Christ (II Cor. II). But the well in this place ought to be understood as the Holy Spirit, of whom in the Gospel the Lord Jesus was saying: If any man thirst, let him come to me and drink, and out of his belly shall flow living waters. Now this he said of the Holy Spirit, whom they that believed in him were to receive (Joan. VII). [Vulg. Joan. VII, 38 Qui credit in me, sicut dicit Scriptura, flumina de ventre eius fluent aquae vivae, "He that believeth in me, as the Scripture saith, out of his belly shall flow rivers of living water"]

He himself, I say, the Holy Spirit, is the fountain of gardens, 503Athe well of living waters which flow with a rush from Libanus (Cant. IV) — that is, the holiness, the refreshment, the purification, the cleansing, the joy and the jubilation of the faithful souls which desire to be with Christ. Let our soul labour, therefore, that it may possess this well within itself, that is, let it be busy by good works to become the dwelling-place of the Holy Spirit. Nevertheless the waters of this well, which are said to flow from Libanus, betoken this: that the Holy Spirit is not possessed save with a chaste body and a purified heart. Whence also the same mount Libanus is interpreted a whitening. And that the well is reported to have been closed with a great stone declares in elegant speech that in a body subject to sins the Holy Spirit in no wise dwelleth (Sap. I). For the stone is the huge mass of our crimes, which without 503Bdoubt hides the Holy Spirit from us, that he be not seen. Whence also the prophet says: The hand of the Lord is not powerless 504Ato save, but our sins have made a wall between you and God (Isa. LIX). [Vulg. Isa. LIX, 1–2 Ecce non est abbreviata manus Domini ut salvare nequeat... sed iniquitates vestrae diviserunt inter vos et Deum vestrum, "your iniquities have divided between you and your God"] But neither is this to be passed over through negligence, which Wisdom says: For the Holy Spirit of discipline shall flee what is feigned (Sap. I). Let the wall of our crimes, therefore, or the millstone, be removed, and let spiritual discipline be sought and held fast by us; that is, let us confess our sins 294, and let us put ourselves under spiritual chastisement, that we may be able to be the most holy dwelling of the Holy Spirit. Let our spirit be, I say, a field blooming with every kind of flower of the virtues; let there be found in it by Jacob a well pouring forth healthful streams, that is, let the Holy Spirit be found by Christ dwelling there. Whose presence will be clearly made manifest thus, if in all things we shall be meek and humble and peaceable 504B, and shall abound in the fruits of good working with chastity and sobriety.

503Here ends the first Tome, that is, the first volume upon the Song of Songs.

INCIPIT SECUNDUS TOMUS Super hoc quod dicitur: Meliora sunt ubera tua vino, fragrantia unguentis optimis.

503BUt autem illa quae sequuntur in hoc libro clarius nobis eluceant, necesse est ut a nostra memoria praecedentia hujus libri non recedant. Sic enim ait 503Cbeatus Hieronymus: Haeret namque sibi invicem sermo divinus, et sequentia pendent ex praecedentibus. Sicut enim manna quondam quod de coelo ad terram descendebat aliud quidem erat, et aliud significabat, sic nimirum divina eloquia aliud exterius ostentant et aliud interius occultant. Manna enim illud quod pluit Deus filiis Israel, corporalis cibus fuit, sed tamen spiritualiter carnem Christi significavit qui in Evangelio dicit: Caro mea vere est cibus, et sanguis meus vere est potus (Joan. VI). Cujus equidem sanguinis figuram ros tenebat, qui cum manna pariter de coelo descendebat. Quemadmodum ergo Israelitae manna colligentes (Num. XI), illud mola conterebant, et inde panem habentes, omne delectamentum et omnem suavitatem sibi praeparabant; 503Dsic filii spiritualis gratiae et inimici occidentis litterae debent discutere et ruminare Scripturas divinas spiritualibus sensibus, et inde spiritualem cibum propinare suis auditoribus. Non igitur simus de pueris Abraham, qui ad radicem montis remanentes custodiunt asinum, sed ascendamus cum Abraham et Isaac in montem excelsum quatenus Angelum videre valeamus, et arietem inter vepres haerentem cornibus (Gen. XXII). Qui sunt ergo qui ad pedem montis remanentes asinum custodiunt, nisi illi qui in sacro eloquio spiritualem et altiorem sensum non requirunt? Quasi enim brutum animal est juxta montem custodire, allegoricum et moralem sensum divinis litteris nolle requirere. Angelus vero qui in monte Abrahae apparuit postquam asinum dimiserat, significat allegoriam quae plenius illis se intimat, quos ab ascensu spiritualis intellectus historica 504Bintelligentia non retardat. Et bene idem Angelus qui in figura allegoriae ponitur, de coelo clamasse perhibetur, quia spiritualis allegoria semper 504Cpraedicat ecclesiastica mysteria et sacramenta coelestia. Aries autem qui inter vepres cornibus haerebat, non inconvenienter moralitatem, quae in asperitate litterae dependet, significabat. Unde bene idem aries non defixus, neque ligatus fuisse inter vepres dicitur, quia moralitas inter seriem litterae, aut raro, aut nunquam plenarie experitur, sed quasi aries inter vepres haerens cornibus; sic ipsa moralitas a prudentibus et litteratis viris ab ipsa historia, cui subtiliter inhaeret, prudenter abstrahitur. Consideremus ergo quam rationabiliter Sponsa apostrophen ad Sponsum faciat, id est qualiter sancta Ecclesia ad Christum convertatur et dicat: Ideo optavi adventum tuum. id est osculum tuum, quia meliora sunt ubera tua vino. Possumus hoc in loco, sano intellectu 504Dduo ubera Christi misericordiam ejus intelligere et clementiam, ita duntaxat, ut misericordia attribuatur ejus humanitati, et clementia ipsius divinitati. Unde Jonas propheta dicit (cap. IV) ad Dominum: Scio, Domine, quia clemens et misericors es tu; ac si diceret: Scio pro certo quia tu, qui nunc Deus solummodo omnibus innotescis, futurum est, ut assumpta humanitate nostra, pro nobis homo efficieris. Praeterea per vinum quod austerioris saporis esse dignoscitur, Mosaicae legis severa justitia non immerito intelligitur. Dicat ergo Sponsa ad Sponsum, id est Ecclesia ad Christum: Idcirco desideravi adventum tuum, quia meliora sunt ubera tua vino, quod est dicere: Melior est clementia et misericordia tua, quam antiquae legis severa et immisericors justitia. Denique si spiritualiter per duo ubera Sponsi quae sunt in divina pagina, allegoriam et 505Amoralitatem; et per vinum, historiam intelligimus, nequaquam a vero sensu deviamus. Dicamus et nos cum Sponsa ad Sponsum: Meliora sunt ubera tua vino, id est dulcior est moralitas et allegoria, quam dura plerumque et irrationabilis historiae intelligentia. Aliter: Meliora sunt ubera tua vino. Duo quippe ubera Dei, lex et prophetae fuerunt, quae lactantes suos interfecerunt. Unde Paulus dicit: Lex iram, (id est mortem) operatur, et nihil ad perfectum adduxit lex (Rom. IV). De his uberibus Isaias dicit: Quem docebit 295 scientiam, et quem intelligere faciet auditum? ablactatos a lacte, avulsos ab uberibus (Isa. XXIX); ac si diceret: Illam Christus spiritualem scientiam et auditum sui Evangelii docebit, qui carnales et insipidos sensus legis et prophetarum 505Ba se penitus removebit.

De his uberibus David ait: Ab uberibus matris meae in te projectus sum (Psal. XXI); quod est dicere: Occidentem litteram et Judaicum legis intellectum deserui, et in te, qui es summa legis veritas, illico deveni. Lex ergo et prophetae in hoc loco ubera sunt Dei, vinum vero est Evangelium de quo Christus dicit: Vinum novum in utres novos (Matth. IX), id est apostolos, oportet poni. Christus ipse est botrus, vinum est Evangelium. Sicut de botro vinum procedit; sic Christus Evangelium condidit. Quod bene praefiguratum est in historia quae refert, quia quondam duo viri attulerunt super colla sua in ligno botrum cum palmite de terra repromissionis (Num. XIII). Duo isti viri afferentes in ligno cum palmite 505Cbotrum, duo sunt populi qui interfecerunt in cruce Christum, Judaei clamantes: Crucifige illum (Luc. XXIII; Joan. XIX), et gentiles qui in cruce posuerunt illum. Dicat ergo Sponsa Sponso, Ecclesia Christo: Meliora sunt ubera tua vino, id est legis et prophetarum eloquia evangelicae praedicationis gratia temperata et rorata, valde sunt meliorata, id est dulciora et meliora effecta. Nam lex dicebat: Si quis hoc, aut illud peccatum fecerit, moriatur (Exod. XXI). Evangelium vero clamat: Si quis peccaverit, confiteatur, poenitentiam agat, et non morietur. Valde ergo sunt meliorata, id est dulciora effecta ubera tua. Fragrantia unguentis optimis. Unguenta ex diversis aromatibus suaviter redolentibus conficiuntur. Quaeramus ergo 505Dista unguenta, Joannes apostolus dicit: Vos unctionem habetis a Spiritus sancto, et unctio ejus docet vos de omnibus (I Joan. II). Videamus denique unguentum quod in capite Aaron fuit positum, et in barbam ejus descendit. Aaron Christum significavit et barba ejus apostolos qui velut barba, familiarius caeteris sanctis adhaeserunt suo capiti Christo. Unguentum ergo in capite Aaron positum et descendens in barbam ejus, diversa sunt dona Spiritus sancti descendentia a Christo in apostolos ejus.

His unguentis Paulus repletus dicebat: Bonus odor sumus Christi in omni loco (II Cor. II). Et alias: Gratias agimus Deo qui odorem notitiae suae per nos manifestavit in omni loco (ibid.). Istis ergo unguentis, id est Spiritus sancti donis et apostolicis doctrinis, 506Aubera Sponsi delibuta et delinita, id est prophetae et lex musto Evangelii temperata, valde sunt fragrantiora, id est redolentiora, quam essent prius, effecta. Stude ergo, o auditor qui audis et legis quae sunt ubera Sponsi, stude, inquam, et tu habere ubera propria sicut habebant Petrus et Paulus quorum alter dicebat: Lac vobis potum dedi (I Cor. III). Alter vero aiebat: Quasi modo geniti infantes lac concupiscite (I Petr. II). Si igitur quaeris quae sunt ista ubera, respondeo: Duo sunt praecepta charitatis, amor Dei et proximi, ut super omnia ames Deum, et proximum sicut teipsum. Sed haec ubera non habent qui saeculum diligunt, quia quanto arctius ei conjuncti sunt, tanto longius a Deo recedunt. Isti ergo audiant Jacobum apostolum terribiliter clamantem: 506BQuicunque voluerit amicus esse hujus saeculi inimicus constituetur Dei (Jac. IV).

HERE BEGINS THE SECOND TOME Upon that which is said: Thy breasts are better than wine, fragrant with the best ointments.

503BBut that those things which follow in this book may shine out to us more clearly, it is needful that what precedes in this book should not depart from our memory. For thus says 503Cblessed Jerome: For the divine discourse cleaves to itself in turn, and what follows hangs upon what goes before. For just as the manna which once came down from heaven to earth was one thing and signified another, so without doubt the divine utterances show one thing outwardly and hide another within. For that manna which God rained upon the children of Israel was bodily food, yet spiritually it signified the flesh of Christ, who says in the Gospel: My flesh is meat indeed, and my blood is drink indeed (Joan. VI). Of which blood indeed the dew held the figure, which came down from heaven together with the manna. As therefore the Israelites, gathering the manna (Num. XI), ground it in a mill, and having bread therefrom, prepared for themselves every delight and every sweetness; 503Dso the sons of spiritual grace and the enemies of the killing letter ought to break open and to ruminate Holy Scripture with spiritual senses, and thence to serve out spiritual food to their hearers. Let us not therefore be of the servants of Abraham, who, remaining at the foot of the mountain, keep the ass; but let us go up with Abraham and Isaac into the high mountain, that we may be able to see the Angel, and the ram clinging by its horns among the briers (Gen. XXII). Who then are they who, remaining at the foot of the mountain, keep the ass, but they who in the sacred utterance do not seek the spiritual and higher sense? For it is as it were the part of a brute beast to keep watch beside the mountain — to be unwilling to seek the allegorical and moral sense in the divine letters. But the Angel who appeared to Abraham on the mountain after he had left the ass signifies the allegory which makes itself known more fully to those whom the historical 504Bunderstanding does not hold back from the ascent of spiritual intelligence. And well is the same Angel, who is set for a figure of allegory, reported to have cried out from heaven, because spiritual allegory always 504Cpreaches the mysteries of the Church and the heavenly sacraments. But the ram which clung by its horns among the briers signified, not unfittingly, morality, which hangs upon the roughness of the letter. Whence well is the same ram said not to have been fixed fast, nor bound, among the briers, because morality is either rarely or never fully found by experience within the sequence of the letter, but is as a ram clinging by its horns among briers; and so morality itself is prudently drawn off, by prudent and lettered men, from the history itself to which it subtly cleaves. Let us consider therefore how reasonably the Bride makes an apostrophe to the Bridegroom, that is, how holy Church turns to Christ and says: For this cause have I desired thy coming. that is, thy kiss, because thy breasts are better than wine. We can in this place, with sound understanding, 504Dunderstand the two breasts of Christ to be his mercy and his clemency, in such wise at least that mercy be attributed to his humanity, and clemency to his divinity. Whence the prophet Jonas says (cap. IV) to the Lord: I know, O Lord, that thou art clement and merciful [Vulg. Jon. 4:2 Scio enim quia tu Deus clemens et misericors es, "for I know that thou art a God clement and merciful"]; as if he should say: I know for certain that thou, who now art made known to all as God only, it shall come to pass that, our humanity being taken up, thou shalt be made man for us. Moreover, by the wine, which is known to be of a harsher savour, the severe justice of the Mosaic law is not undeservedly understood. Let the Bride therefore say to the Bridegroom, that is, the Church to Christ: For this cause have I desired thy coming, because thy breasts are better than wine, which is to say: Better is thy clemency and mercy than the severe and unmerciful justice of the ancient law. Finally, if spiritually by the two breasts of the Bridegroom which are in the divine page we understand allegory and 505Amorality, and by the wine, the history, we in no way stray from the true sense. Let us also say with the Bride to the Bridegroom: Thy breasts are better than wine, that is, sweeter is morality and allegory than the for the most part hard and unreasonable understanding of the history. Otherwise: Thy breasts are better than wine. For the two breasts of God were the law and the prophets, which slew those they suckled. Whence Paul says: The law worketh wrath, (that is, death) and the law brought nothing to perfection (Rom. IV) [Vulg. Rom. 4:15 lex enim iram operatur; the second clause, nihil ad perfectum adduxit lex, is Hebr. 7:19]. Of these breasts Isaias says: Whom shall he teach 295 knowledge, and whom shall he make to understand the hearing? them that are weaned from the milk, torn away from the breasts (Isa. XXIX); as if he should say: Christ will teach that spiritual knowledge and the hearing of his Gospel to him who shall utterly remove from himself the carnal and tasteless senses of the law and the prophets. 505B

Of these breasts David says: From the breasts of my mother I was cast upon thee (Psal. XXI) [Vulg. Ps. 21:10–11 divides these words: spes mea ab uberibus matris meae. In te projectus sum ex utero]; which is to say: I forsook the killing letter and the Jewish understanding of the law, and came forthwith to thee, who art the highest truth of the law. The law therefore and the prophets are in this place the breasts of God, but the wine is the Gospel, of which Christ says: New wine into new bottles (Matth. IX), that is, into the apostles, must be put. Christ himself is the cluster, the wine is the Gospel. As from the cluster wine proceeds, so Christ founded the Gospel. Which is well prefigured in the history which relates that once two men carried upon their shoulders, on a staff, a cluster with its branch out of the land of promise (Num. XIII). Those two men bearing on the staff the cluster with the branch 505Care the two peoples who slew Christ upon the cross: the Jews crying, Crucify him (Luc. XXIII; Joan. XIX), and the Gentiles who set him upon the cross. Let the Bride therefore say to the Bridegroom, the Church to Christ: Thy breasts are better than wine, that is, the utterances of the law and the prophets, tempered and bedewed with the grace of evangelical preaching, are greatly bettered, that is, made sweeter and better. For the law said: If any man shall have committed this or that sin, let him die (Exod. XXI). But the Gospel cries: If any man shall have sinned, let him confess, let him do penance, and he shall not die. Greatly therefore are thy breasts bettered, that is, made sweeter. Fragrant with the best ointments. Ointments are compounded of divers spices sweetly smelling. Let us seek therefore 505Dthese ointments. John the apostle says: You have an unction from the Holy Spirit, and his unction teacheth you of all things (I Joan. II) [Vulg. I Joan. 2:20 vos unctionem habetis a Sancto, "from the Holy One"]. Let us see finally the ointment which was set upon the head of Aaron, and ran down upon his beard. Aaron signified Christ, and his beard the apostles, who, as a beard, clung more familiarly than the other saints to their head, Christ. The ointment therefore set upon the head of Aaron and running down upon his beard are the divers gifts of the Holy Spirit coming down from Christ upon his apostles.

Filled with these ointments Paul said: We are the good odour of Christ in every place (II Cor. II) [Vulg. II Cor. 2:15 Christi bonus odor sumus Deo, "unto God"; in omni loco belongs to v. 14]. And elsewhere: We give thanks to God, who hath manifested the odour of his knowledge by us in every place (ibid.) [Vulg. II Cor. 2:14 Deo autem gratias... et odorem notitiae suae manifestat per nos in omni loco, present manifestat]. By these ointments therefore, that is, by the gifts of the Holy Spirit and by the apostolic teachings, 506Athe breasts of the Bridegroom, anointed and smoothed — that is, the prophets and the law, tempered with the new wine of the Gospel — are made far more fragrant, that is, more odorous, than they were before. Strive therefore, O hearer, thou who hearest and readest what the breasts of the Bridegroom are; strive, I say, thou also to have breasts of thine own, such as Peter and Paul had, of whom the one said: I gave you milk to drink (I Cor. III). But the other said: As newborn babes, desire ye milk (I Petr. II). If therefore thou askest what these breasts are, I answer: They are the two precepts of charity, the love of God and of neighbour, that thou love God above all things, and thy neighbour as thyself. But these breasts they have not who love the world, because the more closely they are joined to it, the further they withdraw from God. Let these therefore hear the apostle James crying terribly: 506BWhosoever shall will to be a friend of this world, shall be made an enemy of God (Jac. IV).

RECAPITULATIO A SUPERIORIBUS.

Superius aspirante gratia Spiritus sancti, praefati sumus, et per aures corporis, mentibus audientium infudimus, quia manna illud quod olim filiis Israel Deus de coelo pluit, figuram et imaginem corporis Christi veraciter tenuerit. Sed fortassis nunc dicturi sunt aliqui quibus aut ista sententia non placebit, aut qui spirituali et altiori intelligentia sunt referti: Haec sententia fastidium generabit, quod admodum a divino intellectu exorbitavimus, qui rem corruptibilem, scilicet manna, rei incorruptibili, videlicet corpori Christi aequiparavimus. Sane si nostris verbis fidem accommodare dedignantur, videant et considerent 506Cquid in Exodo textus historiae nobis etiam tacentibus eloquatur: Apparuit, inquit, in solitudine minutum et quasi pilo tusum in similitudinem pruinae super terram. Quod cum vidissent filii Israel, dixerunt ad invicem: Manhu, quod significat. Quid est hoc? Ignorabant enim quid esset. Quibus ait Moyses: Iste est panis quem Dominus dedit vobis ad vescendum (Exod. XVI). Videamus ergo si forte alicubi reperire poterimus Christum panem appellatum. Dominus ipse in Evangelio dicit: Ego sum panis vivus qui de coelo descendi, si quis manducaverit ex hoc pane vivet in aeternum, et panis quem ego dabo, caro mea est pro mundi vita (Joan. VI). Ecce quam idonee conveniunt sibi invicem Vetus et Novum Testamentum. 506DQuod ergo Moyses manna et panem coelestem vocat, hoc Dominus in Evangelio panem de coelo descendentem, et carnem suam appellat. Verumtamen consideremus remoto a nobis paulisper tumultu saecularium cogitationum, quare Spiritus sanctus per eos Moysi prophetae dicat apparuisse minutum. Deinde cur idem manna pilo tusum fuisse describatur, et, utquid etiam in similitudinem pruinae super terra jacuisse perhibeatur. Si enim secundum grammaticae 296 artis naturam, partem istam scilicet, minutum, subtiliter inspicimus, sine dubio a verbo quod est minuo, minuis, minutum dictum reperimus. Non ergo casu, sed certo rationis sacramento manna fuisse minutum describitur, quia figuram corporis Christi gerebat, de quo David propheta ad Deum 507APatrem loquitur dicens: Minuisti eum paulo minus ab angelis, gloria et honore coronasti eum, et constituisti eum super opera manuum tuarum (Psal. VIII). Non ergo immerito refertur quod manna de supernis apparens, minutum super terram reperitur, quia valde illa superna Majestas Unigeniti Filii se minoravit, quando ad perferendas nostrae mortalitatis injurias descendit, et formam servi suscipiens, secundum Pauli apostoli vocem se ipsum exinanivit (Philip. II). Multum, inquam, manna istud munitum erat, quando Creator coeli et terrae et omnium rerum, inter homines sine existimatione alicujus dignitatis latebat. Valde, inquam, apparuit munitum istud manna, quando a creaturis suis, Creator generis humani, tot sustinuit opprobria. Unde in Exodi 507Blibro sequitur, quia idem coeleste manna quasi pilo tusum esse videatur. Quod utique humanitati Christi apertissime congruit, quae sicut jam praefati sumus, tot dehonestamenta, et tot convicia a perficis hominibus pertulit. Multum siquidem hoc spirituale manna existit pilo tusum quando corpus Christi fuit sputis illitum, flagellis graviter attrectatum, corona irrisorii honoris dehonestatum, insuper ludibrio ab omnibus habitum. Pilo, inquam, dirae passionis spiritale manna diverberatum fuit, quando latus Christi lancea militis perforavit, et clavorum duritia manus pedesque illius terebravit. Fatendum est igitur, omni ambiguitate remota, quod Christus verus Deus et verus homo erat, cujus imaginem vel figuram illud antiquum manna tenebat. Homo sine dubio 507Cfuit, qui dolorem passionis sustinens, obiit, et in manu Dei Patris spiritum suum commendavit, dicens: Pater, in manus tuas commendo spiritum meum (Luc. XXIII). Deus namque verus, et Dei Filius non adoptivus, sed consubstantialis et coaeternus erat, qui in Evangelio dicebat: Potestatem habeo ponendi animam meam, et iterum sumendi eam (Joan. X). Quod autem similitudinem pruinae idem manna habuerit, nullatenus nos a divino et mystico intellectu repellit, sed potius ad intelligendam tantae profunditatis veritatem subtiliter introducit. Omnibus fere hominibus liquet qui utuntur ratione humana, quod pruina, nihil est aliud, nisi aqua gelu constricta, quae eo tempore maxime apparet, quando sol a fervore sui 507Dardoris aliquantulum tepescit, et brumalis aura aquam quam super terram reperit asperitate sui frigoris congelascit. Ideo ergo, ut arbitramur, in similitudinem pruinae manna apparuit super terram, ut insinuaret nobis Judaicae plebis duritiam sive infidelitatem. Unde in Evangelio scriptum est: Facta sunt encaenia in Hierosolymis, et hiems erat (ibid). Circumdederunt ergo Judaei Christum dicentes: Quousque animam nostram tollis: Si tu es Christus, dic nobis palam (ibid.). Luce igitur clarius constat, quia frigus et torpor infidelitatis corda illorum obtinebat, qui Christum insidiis circumvenire satagebant, et in eum qui ob salutem ipsorum venerat, quasi ignorassent quis esset, credere recusabant. Ad duritiam quippe cordium Judaeorum insinuandam 508Aevangelista dicit quia hiems erat, quando Judaei Domino Jesu dixerunt: Si tu es Christus, dic nobis palam. Unde bene in Exodo sequitur, quia cum vidissent manna filii Israel dixerunt: Manhu, quod significat. Quid est hoc? Ignorabant enim quid esset. Ecce enim patres in deserto, viso manna dicunt: Quid est hoc? Filii autem illorum videntes illum cujus figuram illud manna tenebat, dicunt: Si tu es Christus, dic nobis palam. Quis igitur tanta admiratione perfusus merito non obstupescat, quando antiqua lex et nova Evangelii praedicatio unum atque idem clamat? De Judaeis autem in veteri lege textus historiae narrat, quia manna quid esset penitus ignorabant. De filiis vero illorum Paulus apostolus, novae legis praedicator egregius dicit: Si enim Jesum 508BDominum cognovissent, nunquam Regem gloriae crucifixissent (I Cor. II). Manet ergo usque in hodiernum diem apud perfidos Judaeos ignorantia Manhu, sed manet apud fideles Christianos intemerata fides et cognitio Domini Jesu. Interrogent denique Judaei Moysen Manhu quid sit; respondeat illis Moyses: Hic est panis quem ad vescendum Dominus de coelo vobis dedit.

Nos autem sopita nostra interrogatione, intenta aure cordis percipiamus quid Dominus in Evangelio dixerit Judaeis: Patres vestri manducaverunt manna in deserto, et mortui sunt (Joan. VI). Sed quare mortui sunt? quia de illo manna nihil aliud credebant, nisi quod corporeis oculis videbant. Similiter vero et filii eorum paterna infidelitate et caecitate perculsi 508CChristum solum hominem esse arbitrabantur, et illum verum Deum et verum Dei Filium credere dedignabantur.

Constat ergo pro rato quoniam quidem patres et filii, simili morte infidelitatis sunt damnati. Quibus bene in Evangelio Dominus dicit: Dixi vobis quia in peccato vestro moriemini (Joan. VIII). Quod utique bene longe ante prophetaverat Ezechiel propheta qui dixerat: Et quare moriemini filii Israel? quia 297 nolo mortem peccatoris, dicit Dominus, sed ut convertatur et vivat (Ezech. XVIII). Qui igitur non de praesenti, sed de futuro prophetat, dicens: Moriemini; manifeste denuntiat perfidiam Judaeorum, et quia in ipsum, qui pro ipsis nasciturus erat, non 508Dessent credituri.

RECAPITULATION FROM THE FOREGOING.

Above, by the inbreathing grace of the Holy Spirit, we said beforehand, and through the ears of the body poured into the minds of our hearers, that that manna which God once rained from heaven upon the children of Israel truly held the figure and image of the body of Christ. But perhaps there are now some who will say — either such as this opinion will not please, or such as are filled with a spiritual and higher intelligence: This opinion will breed loathing, in that we have strayed exceedingly from the divine understanding, we who have made a corruptible thing, namely the manna, equal to an incorruptible thing, namely the body of Christ. Assuredly, if they disdain to give credit to our words, let them see and consider 506Cwhat in Exodus the text of the history utters to us even when we are silent: It appeared, he says, in the wilderness small, and as it were beaten with a pestle, like unto the hoar frost upon the earth. And when the children of Israel had seen it, they said one to another: Manhu, which signifieth. What is this? For they knew not what it was. And Moses said to them: This is the bread which the Lord hath given you to eat (Exod. XVI). Let us see therefore whether perchance we may anywhere find Christ called bread. The Lord himself says in the Gospel: I am the living bread which came down from heaven; if any man eat of this bread, he shall live for ever; and the bread which I will give is my flesh for the life of the world (Joan. VI). Behold how fitly the Old and the New Testament agree together. 506DWhat therefore Moses calls manna and heavenly bread, this the Lord in the Gospel calls bread coming down from heaven, and his own flesh. Nevertheless, the tumult of secular thoughts being for a little while removed from us, let us consider why the Holy Spirit says through them to Moses the prophet that it appeared small. Then, why the same manna is described to have been beaten with a pestle; and for what cause also it is reported to have lain upon the earth like unto the hoar frost. For if, according to the nature of the grammarian's 296 art, we look subtly upon this part, namely minutum, we find without doubt that minutum is said from the verb which is minuo, minuis. Not therefore by chance, but by a sure sacrament of reason, is the manna described to have been minutum, because it bore the figure of the body of Christ, of whom the prophet David speaks to God 507Athe Father, saying: Thou hast made him a little less than the angels, thou hast crowned him with glory and honour, and hast set him over the works of thy hands (Psal. VIII). Not undeservedly therefore is it related that the manna, appearing from above, is found small upon the earth, because that supernal Majesty of the Only-begotten Son made itself exceedingly small when it came down to bear the injuries of our mortality, and, taking the form of a servant, according to the word of Paul the apostle emptied itself (Philip. II). Much, I say, was that manna fortified, when the Creator of heaven and earth and of all things lay hidden among men without the esteem of any dignity. Exceedingly, I say, did that manna appear fortified, when from his own creatures the Creator of the human race endured so many reproaches. Whence in the book of Exodus 507Bit follows that the same heavenly manna is seen as it were beaten with a pestle. Which indeed most plainly agrees with the humanity of Christ, which, as we have already said before, bore so many dishonourings and so many revilings from perficis men. Much indeed was this spiritual manna beaten with a pestle when the body of Christ was smeared with spittle, grievously handled with scourges, dishonoured with a crown of mocking honour, and moreover held by all for a laughing-stock. With the pestle, I say, of the dread passion was the spiritual manna beaten through, when the soldier's lance pierced the side of Christ, and the hardness of the nails bored through his hands and feet. It must therefore be confessed, all ambiguity being removed, that Christ was true God and true man, whose image or figure that ancient manna held. Without doubt he was man 507Cwho, bearing the pain of the passion, died, and commended his spirit into the hand of God the Father, saying: Father, into thy hands I commend my spirit (Luc. XXIII). For he was true God, and the Son of God not adoptive but consubstantial and coeternal, who said in the Gospel: I have power to lay down my soul, and to take it up again (Joan. X). But that the same manna had the likeness of hoar frost in no wise drives us back from the divine and mystical understanding, but rather subtly leads us in to understand a truth of such depth. It is clear to almost all men who use human reason that hoar frost is nothing else but water bound fast by frost, which appears chiefly at that season when the sun grows somewhat cool from the fervour of its own 507Dburning, and the wintry air congeals with the roughness of its cold the water which it finds upon the earth. For this cause therefore, as we judge, did the manna appear upon the earth like unto hoar frost, that it might intimate to us the hardness or the unbelief of the Jewish people. Whence in the Gospel it is written: It was the feast of the dedication at Jerusalem, and it was winter (ibid). The Jews therefore surrounded Christ, saying: How long dost thou take away our soul: If thou be the Christ, tell us plainly (ibid.). It stands therefore clearer than light that the cold and torpor of unbelief held their hearts, who busied themselves to entrap Christ with snares, and refused to believe in him who had come for their own salvation, as though they knew not who he was. Indeed, to intimate the hardness of the hearts of the Jews 508Athe evangelist says that it was winter when the Jews said to the Lord Jesus: If thou be the Christ, tell us plainly. Whence well does it follow in Exodus that when the children of Israel had seen the manna they said: Manhu, which signifieth. What is this? For they knew not what it was. For behold, the fathers in the wilderness, seeing the manna, say: What is this? But their sons, seeing him whose figure that manna held, say: If thou be the Christ, tell us plainly. Who therefore, filled with so great wonder, would not deservedly be astounded, when the ancient law and the new preaching of the Gospel cry one and the same thing? But concerning the Jews under the old law the text of the history relates that they were utterly ignorant what the manna was. And concerning their sons Paul the apostle, that excellent preacher of the new law, says: For if they had known Jesus 508Bthe Lord, they would never have crucified the King of glory (I Cor. II) [Vulg. I Cor. 2:8 si enim cognovissent, nunquam Dominum gloriae crucifixissent, "the Lord of glory"]. There remains therefore even to this day among the faithless Jews the ignorance of Manhu, but there remains among faithful Christians an unstained faith and knowledge of the Lord Jesus. Let the Jews at last ask Moses what Manhu is; let Moses answer them: This is the bread which the Lord hath given you from heaven to eat.

But let us, our own questioning laid to rest, receive with the intent ear of the heart what the Lord said in the Gospel to the Jews: Your fathers did eat manna in the desert, and are dead (Joan. VI). But why are they dead? Because of that manna they believed nothing else than what they saw with bodily eyes. And in like manner their sons also, stricken with their fathers' unbelief and blindness, 508Cjudged Christ to be man only, and disdained to believe him true God and true Son of God.

It stands therefore for certain that both the fathers and the sons were condemned by a like death of unbelief. To whom well does the Lord say in the Gospel: I said to you that you shall die in your sin (Joan. VIII). Which indeed the prophet Ezechiel had well prophesied long before, who had said: And why will you die, ye children of Israel? because 297 I will not the death of the sinner, saith the Lord, but that he be converted and live (Ezech. XVIII) [Vulg. Ezech. 18:31–32 quare moriemini domus Israel... quia nolo mortem morientis, "house of Israel," "of him that dieth"; sed ut convertatur et vivat answers rather to Ezech. 33:11]. He therefore who prophesies not of the present but of the future, saying: You shall die, manifestly declares the perfidy of the Jews, and that in him who was to be born for their sakes they would not 508Dbelieve.

RECAPITULATIO A SUPERIORIBUS, DE UBERIBUS SPONSI.

In anteriore tractatu hujus libri, cum juxta modulum nostrum disputaremus de uberibus Sponsi, ipsa ejus ubera secundum spiritualem intelligentiam interpretati sumus clementiam et misericordiam; ita duntaxat, ut ad ejus divinitatem referatur clementia, et humanitati illius attribuatur misericordia. Horum igitur spiritualium uberum Sponsi, quae sunt clementia et misericordia, plerisque in locis figura reperitur per sacra eloquia. Unde est illud in tertio libro Regum (cap. VI): Fecit, inquit, Salomon in ingressu oraculi duo ostia de lignis olivarum et sculpsit in eis victuram cherubim, et palmarum 509Aspecies, et anaglypta valde prominentia, et operuit tam cherubim quam palmas et caetera auro. Horum, inquam, spiritualium uberum Sponsi, scilicet clementiae et misericordiae invenitur figura in eodem libro Regum (cap. X) sub manuum appellatione, sicut scriptum est: Fecit, inquit, rex Salomon thronum de ebore grandem, et vestivit eum auro fulvo nimis qui habebat sex gradus, et summitas throni rotunda erat in parte posteriori, et duae manus hinc atque inde tenentes sedile, et duo leones stabant juxta manus singulas, et duodecim leunculi stantes super sex gradus hinc atque inde. Postea autem subinfert Regum historia, quia non est factum tale opus in universis regnis (ibid.). Haec, inquam, sunt duo ubera spiritualia Sponsi, quae Zacharias propheta 509Bduas olivas in libro suo (cap. IV) appellat. Quemadmodum ibi scriptum est: Vidi et ecce candelabrum aureum totum, et lampas ejus super caput ipsius, et septem lucernae ejus super illud, et septem infusoria lucernis quae erant super caput illius, et duae olivae super illud, una a dextris lampadis, et una a sinistris ejus, et in sequentibus cum idem propheta ab Angelo quaereret, qui ei loquebatur, quid essent duae olivae, sive duae species olivarum quae sunt juxta duo rostra aurea, in quibus sunt suffusoria ex auro; responsum est ei ab Angelo sancto: Isti sunt duo filii olei splendoris qui assistunt dominatori universae terrae (ibid.). Si jam nunc ad nostrae narrationis ordinem redeamus, et quomodo duo ostia de lignis olivarum in ingressu 509Coraculi posita, duo ubera Sponsi clementiam et misericordiam significent, ostendamus; verumtamen remoto velamine litterae occidentis, prius dicendum est amicis gratiae spiritalis, quia qui Sponsus appellatur in Canticis canticorum, ipse oraculum templi, et thronus eburneus vocatur in libro Regum. Hunc denique et Zacharias propheta in Spiritu sancto candelabrum aureum nominavit. Sicut igitur in corpore humano corporaliter ubera sunt infixa, sic Christo naturaliter insitae sunt istae duae virtutes, clementia scilicet et misericordia. Unde merito ejus ubera appellantur, quia dulci affluentia harum virtutum orbis terrarum impletur, nutritur et sustentatur. De qua nimirum affluentia David propheta 509Ddicit: Misericordia Domini plena est terra (Psal. XXXII). Quomodo enim mundus iste posset subsistere qui tot iniquitatibus praegravatur, aut qualiter genus mortalium salutem acquireret, si remota Dei clementia et misericordia judicaretur? Manifestum est, quia nec mundus, nec homo, sola diei hora valeret subsistere, si Deus ad mala quae fiunt ab hominibus in hoc mundo, et non ad suam clementiam vellet respicere. Propterea et David propheta dicit: Domine, non secundum peccata nostra facias nobis, neque secundum iniquitates nostras retribuas nobis (Psal. CII). Bene itaque per duo ostia oraculi, accipiuntur ubera Sponsi, quia sicut per illa ostia, qui ingressuri erant oraculum, ingrediebantur, sic qui regnum coelorum intraturi sunt, non suis meritis 510Avel operibus, sed clementia et misericordia Dei intrabunt. Verumtamen quid esse arbitramur, quod in ostiis oraculi cherubim, et palmae, anaglyta quoque sculpta esse dicuntur? Manifeste patet, quia cherubim, multitudo scientiae vel sapientiae interpretatur, et multis notum est quoniam quidem antiquis temporibus victorum manus ob insigne laudis et victoriae, palmis adornabantur. Anaglyptum vero quantum intelligi datur quoddam genus sculpturae esse dicitur, cujus pars posterior intrinsecus latet; sed anterior humani ingenii opere sculptorio deformata, ultra modum prominet. Non igitur immerito in ostiis oraculi cherubin sculpta esse reperiuntur, quae multitudo scientiae vel sapientiae interpretantur, quia nimirum justum esse reor, ut hi qui de clementia 510Bet misericordia Dei sperant, a vera scientia vel sapientia nullatenus recedant. Non enim est aliqua major sapientia, quam ut homo a malo funditus recedat, et bonum, quo Deo placeat, sine intervallo faciat. Quam utique sapientiam Psalmista paucis verbis comprehendere curavit, dicens: Declina a malo et fac bonum (Psal. XXXVI). Palmarum vero species recto ordine 298 post cherubim sequuntur, quia illorum manus victrici palma juste decorantur, qui bonis operibus intenta devotione insudant, et a malis, quantum fas mortalibus est, sollicita intentione sibi praecavent. Unde bene ecclesiastica auctoritas egregio usu jampridem obtinuit, ut martyrum Christi et virginum dextrae palmis laude dignis debeant insigniri, quae saeculum praesens despiciendo 510Cvicerunt, et virginitatem et castitatem suam servando, corpora sua pro amore coelestis regni martyrio tradere non dubitaverunt. Ingens siquidem victoria est, integritatem mentis et corporis in hoc saeculo custodire, et seipsum cum praesenti mundo usque ad mortem vincendo contemnere. Eapropter ipse mundi Creator, qui saeculum nequam despiciendo calcaverat, militibus suis exhortatoria voce dicebat: Confidite, quia ego vici mundum (Joan. XVI). Nullatenus denique melius mundum vincere valemus, quam ut ipsi et eis rebus, quae ejus juris sunt, penitus renuntiemus. Qui autem jam a malo se temperat, et bene operatur, qui virginitatem seu castitatem servat, et mundo perfecte renuntiasse 510Dvidetur, expedit illi ut ab appetitu caeterarum virtutum habenas sui cursus non retrahat, quarum scilicet virtutum figuram spiritualiter tenere dignoscuntur anaglypta quae in ostiis oraculi sculpta reperiuntur. Anaglypta quippe, ut superius praefati sumus, sculpturae sunt quae intrinsecus posteriori parte absconduntur, sed anterior earum pars cunctis cernentibus liquido manifestatur.

Qui ergo visceribus charitatis intus affluit, et ejusdem charitatis opera foris proximis largiter impendit, qui obedientiam servat in mente, et ejusdem obedientiae opera exhibet quandoque in operatione, qui humilis et mitis est in corde, et ejusdem humilitatis et mansuetudinis habet indicia in exteriori exhibitione, qui reliqua virtutum in se exprimit 511Avestigia, ex hujus procul dubio mente et corpore prodeunt spiritualiter anaglypta valde prominentia. Sic denique de reliquis virtutibus sentiendum est. Si quis autem castitatem amat intus, si carnem foris macerat, si jejunat, vigilat, laborat et orat; iste, remota omni dubietate, anaglypta in carne sua exsculpta esse manifestat. Quoniam igitur sine fide impossibile est salvari, ideo merito historia subinfert, quod Salomon operuit auro cherubin et palmas et anaglypta. Qui enim supradictis virtutibus, quae per cherubin et palmas et anaglypta significantur, abundaverit, si tamen Catholica fide caruerit, nequaquam salvari poterit. Quod autem per aurum in Scripturis sacris fides Catholica intelligitur, beatus Petrus edocet qui ait: Ut fides vestra multo pretiosior 511Bsit auro quod per ignem probatur (I Petr. I). Ecce ostendimus quod per duo ostia oraculi, duo ubera Sponsi, scilicet clementia et misericordia Dei debeant spiritualiter intelligi.

Ipse enim Christus est verum et spirituale oraculum, quia per ipsum orationes nostrae exaudiuntur apud Deum Patrem. Unde beatus Joannes dicit: Advocatum habemus apud Patrem, Jesum Christum justum, et ipse est propitiatio pro peccatis nostris (I Joan. II. ,

RECAPITULATION FROM THE FOREGOING, ON THE BREASTS OF THE BRIDEGROOM.

In the earlier treatise of this book, when according to our small measure we were disputing of the breasts of the Bridegroom, we interpreted those breasts of his, according to the spiritual intelligence, as clemency and mercy; in such wise at least that clemency be referred to his divinity, and mercy be attributed to his humanity. Of these spiritual breasts of the Bridegroom therefore, which are clemency and mercy, the figure is found in very many places throughout the sacred utterances. Whence is that passage in the third book of Kings (cap. VI): Solomon made, he says, in the entrance of the oracle two doors of olive wood, and graved upon them a victuram of cherubim, and likenesses of palm trees, 509Aand anaglypta standing out very far; and he covered with gold both the cherubim and the palms and the rest. Of these same spiritual breasts of the Bridegroom, I say, namely of clemency and mercy, a figure is found in the same book of Kings (cap. X) under the name of hands, as it is written: King Solomon made, he says, a great throne of ivory, and clothed it with the finest gold, which had six steps; and the top of the throne was round in the hinder part, and two hands on this side and on that holding the seat, and two lions stood beside each several hand, and twelve little lions standing upon the six steps on this side and on that. But afterwards the history of Kings adds that there was no such work made in any kingdoms (ibid.). These, I say, are the two spiritual breasts of the Bridegroom, which the prophet Zacharias 509Bin his book (cap. IV) calls two olive trees. Even as it is written there: I saw, and behold a candlestick all of gold, and its lamp upon the top of it, and its seven lights upon it, and seven funnels for the lights that were upon the top thereof, and two olive trees over it, one upon the right of the lamp, and one upon the left thereof, and in what follows, when the same prophet asked of the Angel who was speaking to him what the two olive trees were, or the two branches of the olive trees which are beside the two golden beaks, in which are the funnels of gold, it was answered him by the holy Angel: These are two sons of the oil of splendour, who stand before the Ruler of the whole earth (ibid.) [Vulg. Zach. 4:14 isti sunt duo filii olei, without splendoris]. If now at length we return to the order of our narration, and show how the two doors of olive wood set in the entrance 509Cof the oracle signify the two breasts of the Bridegroom, clemency and mercy — nevertheless, the veil of the killing letter being removed, it must first be said to the friends of spiritual grace that he who in the Canticle of Canticles is called the Bridegroom, he himself is called the oracle of the temple and the ivory throne in the book of Kings. Him finally the prophet Zacharias also named, in the Holy Spirit, a golden candlestick. As therefore in the human body the breasts are bodily set, so in Christ these two virtues are naturally implanted, namely clemency and mercy. Whence they are deservedly called his breasts, because by the sweet affluence of these virtues the world is filled, nourished, and sustained. Of which affluence indeed the prophet David 509Dsays: The earth is full of the mercy of the Lord (Psal. XXXII). For how could this world subsist, which is weighed down with so many iniquities, or how would the race of mortals obtain salvation, if it were judged with the clemency and mercy of God removed? It is manifest that neither the world nor man could subsist for a single hour of the day, if God were willing to look upon the evils which are done by men in this world, and not upon his own clemency. Wherefore the prophet David also says: O Lord, do not unto us according to our sins, neither reward us according to our iniquities (Psal. CII) [Vulg. Ps. 102:10 is indicative: non secundum peccata nostra fecit nobis, neque secundum iniquitates nostras retribuit nobis, "he hath not dealt with us according to our sins"]. And so well are the breasts of the Bridegroom taken by the two doors of the oracle, because as through those doors they entered who were to enter the oracle, so they who are to enter the kingdom of heaven shall enter not by their own merits 510Aor works, but by the clemency and mercy of God. Nevertheless, what do we judge it to be, that upon the doors of the oracle cherubim and palms, and anaglyta also, are said to have been graved? It is manifestly plain that cherubim is interpreted a multitude of knowledge or of wisdom, and it is known to many that in ancient times the hands of the victorious were adorned with palms for a token of praise and of victory. But an anaglyptum, so far as it is given us to understand, is said to be a certain kind of sculpture whose hinder part lies hidden within, but whose front part, shaped by the sculptor's work of human skill, stands out beyond measure. Not undeservedly therefore are cherubim found to have been graven upon the doors of the oracle, which are interpreted a multitude of knowledge or of wisdom, because indeed I reckon it just that they who hope in the clemency 510Band mercy of God should in no wise depart from true knowledge or wisdom. For there is no greater wisdom than that a man should withdraw utterly from evil, and without interval do the good whereby he may please God. Which wisdom the Psalmist took care to comprise in few words, saying: Turn away from evil, and do good (Psal. XXXVI). But the likenesses of palm trees follow in right order 298 after the cherubim, because their hands are justly adorned with the palm of victory who sweat at good works with intent devotion, and, so far as is lawful to mortals, guard themselves from evils with anxious purpose. Whence well has ecclesiastical authority long since obtained, by excellent usage, that the right hands of the martyrs of Christ and of virgins ought to be marked with palms worthy of praise, who by despising 510Covercame the present world, and by keeping their virginity and chastity did not hesitate to give over their bodies to martyrdom for love of the heavenly kingdom. It is indeed a mighty victory to keep the integrity of mind and body in this world, and to despise oneself along with the present world by overcoming it even unto death. Wherefore the very Creator of the world, who by despising had trodden down the wicked world, said to his soldiers with a voice of exhortation: Have confidence, for I have overcome the world (Joan. XVI). And finally we can in no way better overcome the world than by renouncing utterly both it and those things which are of its right. But he who already restrains himself from evil and works well, who keeps virginity or chastity, and is seen to have perfectly renounced 510Dthe world, it is expedient for him that he draw not back the reins of his course from the appetite of the other virtues; of which virtues, namely, the anaglypta which are found graven upon the doors of the oracle are known spiritually to hold the figure. For anaglypta, as we said above, are sculptures which are hidden within in their hinder part, but whose front part is clearly manifest to all beholders.

He therefore who within abounds in the bowels of charity, and without bestows the works of that same charity bountifully upon his neighbours; who keeps obedience in mind, and shows forth the works of that same obedience at times in operation; who is humble and meek in heart, and has the tokens of that same humility and meekness in his outward showing; who expresses in himself the remaining traces of the virtues 511A— from his mind and body, beyond all doubt, there come forth spiritually anaglypta standing out very far. And so at last must we think concerning the remaining virtues. But if any man loves chastity within, if he mortifies the flesh without, if he fasts, watches, labours and prays, he, all doubtfulness removed, shows that anaglypta have been carved in his own flesh. Since therefore without faith it is impossible to be saved, therefore does the history deservedly add that Solomon covered with gold the cherubim and the palms and the anaglypta. For he who shall have abounded in the aforesaid virtues, which are signified by the cherubim and the palms and the anaglypta, if nevertheless he shall have lacked the Catholic faith, will in no wise be able to be saved. But that by gold in Holy Scripture the Catholic faith is understood, blessed Peter teaches, who says: That your faith may be much more precious 511Bthan gold which is tried by the fire (I Petr. I) [Vulg. I Petr. 1:7 ut probatio fidei vestrae multo pretiosior auro quod per ignem probatur, "the trial of your faith"]. Behold, we have shown that by the two doors of the oracle the two breasts of the Bridegroom, namely the clemency and the mercy of God, ought spiritually to be understood.

For Christ himself is the true and spiritual oracle, because through him our prayers are heard with God the Father. Whence blessed John says: We have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the just, and he is the propitiation for our sins (I Joan. II. ,

RECAPITULATIO A SUPERIORIBUS DE THRONO EBURNEO QUEM FECIT SALOMON, QUOMODO REFERRI DEBEAT AD PERSONAM CHRISTI.

Superius praefati sumus quod Dominus Jesus 511Cqui in Canticis canticorum appellatur Sponsus, ipse nimirum in libro Regum vocatur oraculum templi, et thronus eburneus. Praeterea hoc addidimus, quod per duo ostia oraculi clementia et misericordia Dei, videlicet ubera Sponsi, debeant intelligi. Subjunximus denique sufficientem rationem, quia sicut ingredientes oraculum per illa ostia ingrediebantur, sic utique per clementiam et misericordiam Dei, qui salvandi sunt, salvabuntur. Superest igitur ut ad thronum ex ebore conditum accedamus, et quemadmodum idem thronus in figura Christi a Salomone sit factus, pro modulo nostro disseramus. Tunc demum necesse erit ut allegorizando spirituali sensu perscrutemur, qualiter illae duae manus de quibus in praecedenti loco loquebamur 511Dquae in throno eburneo sedile tenent, figuram clementiae et misericordiae, scilicet uberum Sponsi, spiritualiter teneant. Verumtamen pius considerandum nobis est, utrum in divinis eloquiis alicubi de throno aliquid dicatur, quatenus hoc quod dicere volumus, ex auctoritate sacrorum eloquiorum corroboretur. In Apocalypsi Joannes dicit: De throno procedunt fulgura, et voces, et tonitrua (Apoc. IV). Ac si dicat: De Christo, qui est thronus aureus, in quo requievit plenitudo divinitatis, prodeunt fulgura, id est, miraculorum insignia, et voces evangelicae praedicationis, tonitrua videlicet terrores futuri judicii et extremae damnationis.

Praeterea hoc neminem lateat quoniam quidem thronus, de quo loqui satagimus, et quem ad personam 512AChristi spiritualiter referri disponimus, non solum de ebore, verum etiam de auro clarissimo exstitisse describitur. Patet igitur et sub nulla ex hoc redigimur ambiguitate, quia per aurum, divinitatem 299 et per ebur, ejus humanitatem debeamus intelligere. Quod vero aurum significationem divinitatis Christi obtinet, difficili interpretatione non indiget, quia sicut aurum ob pretiositatem sui, omnia metalla antecedit, sic nimirum divinitas Christi, non solum humanitatem ejus, sed etiam omnes creaturas ob excellentiam suae dignitatis praecellit. Unde eleganter Spiritus sanctus, postquam dixit per os historiographi, quod fecerit Salomon thronum de ebore grandem, subsequenter adjunxit: Et vestitvit eum auro fulvo nimis, ut manifestaretur 512Bquantum praerogativa divinitatis excelleret assumptam materiam humanitatis. Certum est enim, et nullus ambigit, quia res quae aliam rem vestit, ipsi rei superponitur quam supervestire videtur. Sicut ergo aurum splendidissimum suo splendore et sua claritate thronum, quem vestiebat, decorabat, adornabat et clarificabat; sic procul dubio divinitatis hominem illum quem assumpsit, ad hoc usque suam glorificationem provexit, ut ipse homo participatione divinitatis Deus effectus sit. Multum igitur nostra humanitas profecit, quae ad participationem supernae deitatis in assumptione assumentis divinitatis glorificata ascendit. Denique quanto in primo Adam deterior exstitit ad inferos 512Cejus descensio, tanto in secundo Adam gloriosior et excellentior fuit ejus ad coelos ascensio. Et sicut non erat quo infelicius a diabolo seducta de paradiso praecipitaretur, sic non fuerat quo felicius et gloriosius a Christo sublimaretur.

Exaltata est igitur caro nostra in Christo super omnem creaturam et supra se ipsam; supra se ipsam dico, quia nequaquam promeruit illam dignitatem ad quam pervenit. Et hoc est nimirum quod propheta longe, antequam fieret, prophetaverat. Consilium tuum, Domine, antiquum verum fiat (Isa. XXV). Istud sane consilium ab aeterno, et ante aeternum (si dici fas est!) erat in dispositione summae deitatis, ut nostra caro a Christo assumenda et glorificanda celsificaretur usque ad contemplationem 512Ddivinae majestatis. De hac utique glorificatione nostrae humanitatis vel assumptione, propheta Ezechiel (cap. XVII) spirituali refectus contemplatione mihi videtur dicere: Aquila magna magno alarum praesidio fulta, et multa varietate plumarum decorata venit ad cedrum quae erat in Libano, et tulit de medulla cedri et asportavit eam, et collocavit eam in monte sancto et excelso. Quae est ergo ista aquila, nisi illa de qua dicit propheta: Sicut aquila provocat ad volandum pullos suos et super eos volitans expandit alas suas, et assumpsit eos, atque portavit in humeris suis? (Deut. XXXII) Et tanquam si aliquis quaereret ab eo, de qua aquila loqueretur, illico quia haec de Deo dixerit manifeste subjunxit; Dominus solus dux ejus fuit (ibid.). Ista igitur aquila 513Avenit ad cedrum quae erat in Libano, et de medulla ejus sibi assumpsit; quando Verbum de corde Patris eructatum venit ad beatam Mariam, et de virginea ejus carne sacrosanctum et intemeratum sibi corpus accepit. Et sicut de illa aquila dicit propheta, quod medullam illam quam de cedro tulerat, in monte sancto et excelso collocavit, sic nimirum Dei Filius, videlicet Verbum Patris, carnem nostram quam de beata Virgine Maria assumpsit, in sede majestatis suae secum ad dexteram sui Patris sublimavit. Quod autem beata Maria cedrus Libani appelletur, ex ecclesiastico usu potius quam ex aliqua difficili interpretatione patenter dignoscitur. Sic enim sancta Ecclesia dicit in ejus quotidiano servitio: Quasi cedrus exaltata sum in Libano (Eccli. 513BXXIV). Et quia Libanus, candidatio interpretatur, non immerito virginitati beatae Mariae comparatur. Sicut enim materialis cedrus in Libano fuit exaltata, sic beata Maria ob singulare privilegium suae virginitatis apud Deum est glorificata et praeelecta. Sed jam nunc ad thronum aureum et eburneum superius revertamur, et qualiter figuram Dominici corporis tenuerit, spiritualibus oculis intueamur. Fecit, inquit, Salomon thronum de ebore grandem (III Reg. X). Vere thronus iste admirabili celsitudine exstitit grandis, quia ut Psalmista ait: Magnus Dominus et laudabilis nimis, et magnitudinis ejus non est finis (Psal. CXLIV). Praeterea paulo ante declaravimus ut quid thronus iste auro pretiosissimo 513Cfuerit vestitus, nunc superest ut mens nostra in lucem proferat quare idem thronus sit eburneus, qui Dominici corporis figuram gerebat. Sicut enim ante nos dixerunt qui de naturis rerum prudenti solertia disputaverunt, ebur tam admirabilis virtutis, et frigiditatis est, ut si pannus lineus super ipsum abur mittatur et ipsi panno lineo carbo vivus desuper imponatur, sua virtute ebur pannum qui illi adhaeret illaesum custodit, ne a supraposito carbone vivo consumi penitus aut cremari possit. Qui ergo manifeste non clarescit vel tenuiter scienti, quomodo ista comparatio aptari debeat corpori Christi? Sicut enim ebur naturali virtute superpositum pannum ab ardore ignis inviolatum conservat, sic divinitas quae in corpore Christi adumbrata 513Derat, ipsum corpus ab omni naevo peccati immune custodiebat. Eapropter et beatus Petrus apostolus de Domino Jesu loquens aiebat: Qui peccatum, inquit, non fecit, nec inventus est dolus in ore ejus (I Petr. II). Quod autem sex gradus in throno fuisse referuntur quibus ad sedile throni gradatim ascendebatur, manifesta ratione Historia sacra nos perdocuit quia 300 per sex saeculi labentis aetates Deus Filius se venturum ad suam incarnationem mysticis et occultis modis nobis repromisit. In prima siquidem aetate quae Adam usque Noe exstitit, se venturum spopondit quando mulieri denuntiavit, dicens: Semen tuum conteret caput serpentis (Gen. III). In secunda vero aetate quae a 514ANoe usque ad Abraham fuit, venturum se indicavit, quando filiis Noe dixit: Crescite et multiplicamini (Gen. VIII). In tertia autem aetate quae ab Abraham usque ad Moysen decurrit, adfuturum se praefiguravit, quando ejusdem prophetae proli benedixit, dicens: In semine tuo benedicentur omnes gentes (Gen. XXII). In quarta vero aetate quae erat a Moyse usque ad David, in multiplici sacrificiorum religione adfore se insinuavit. In quinta autem aetate quae erat a David usque ad nativitatem beatae Mariae floruit, quia ipse est Rex regum et sacerdos in aeternum secundum ordinem Melchisedech (Psal. CIX), in regibus et sacerdotibus figuraliter se venturum esse denuntiavit. Sexta aetas, ipsa est quae nunc agitur ab ortu beatae Mariae Virginis usque ad diem 514Bjudicii, in qua ad incarnationem suam clementer dignatus est venire Filius Dei, sicut sexto gradu perveniebatur ad illud gloriosum sedile quod erat in medio throni aurei et eburnei (III Reg. X). Quod autem dicitur quia summitatis throni rotunda sit in posteriori parte, nequaquam cum ignavia a prudenti lectore praetereundum est. Nullus enim fere ignorat qui aliquanto sensu abundat, quia quidquid est rotundum, et omni parte sui, est perfectum. De Christo namque cujus figuram thronus ille tenuit, sancta Ecclesia filiis suis praedicat, dicens: Perfectus Deus, perfectus homo, ex anima rationali et humana carne subsistens. Duae autem manus quae in praefato throno, hinc atque inde sedile tenent, non incongrue clementia et misericordia nostri 514CSalvatoris intelligi debent; quae hoc egerunt, ut pro redemptione generis humani carnem Christus acciperet, et mortem quam penitus ignorabat, ad tempus degustaret. Unde et David propheta dicit: De torrente in via bibet, propterea exaltabit caput (Psal. CIX). Haec, inquam, sunt spiritualia ubera Sponsi in Canticis canticorum, duae manus quae tenent sedile throni aurei et eburnei in libro Regum, scilicet clementia et misericordia, quae fecerunt ut Deus homo fieret, et nos ad gaudia paradisi, quae per Adam perdidimus, mirabiliter reduceret.

Videamus denique quid sit quod superius textus historiae nobis recitavit: Juxta, inquit, singulas manus 514Dduo leones stabant (III Reg. X). Manifesta quippe ratione ex praecedenti sermone intelligimus, quia non amplius ibi erant quam duae manus, et si singulis manibus duo leones deputati erant, pro certo tenemus quia non nisi quatuor leones tantummodo inibi assistebant. Quid ergo quatuor leones, qui juxta singulas manus positi sunt, insinuant, nisi quatuor evangelia quae ubique clementiam et misericordiam Dei praedicant? Sic enim habes in Evangelio scriptum: Qui crediderit et baptizatus fuerit, salvus erit (Marc. XVI). Et alio in loco: Poenitentiam agite, appropinquavit regnum coelorum (Matth. IV). Duodecim leunculi quos sacra historia refert super sex gradus exstitisse, plenitudinem et perfectionem 515Adonorum spiritualium significant; quae nullus dubitat super Christum requievisse, et mansisse. In Christo, ut Apostolus ait: Habitat omnis plenitudo divinitatis corporaliter (Coloss. II). Et de plenitudine ejus, inquit Joannes, nos omnes accepimus (Joan. I). Interim si per septem dona Spiritus sancti, quae Isaias in Christo requievisse narrat, et per virtutes quas sancta Ecclesia praecipue commendat, istos duodecim leunculos volumus intelligere, noster intellectus etiam apud inimicos veritatis, dignus digna judicabitur laude. Sic enim dicit Isaias: Egredietur virga de radice Jesse, et flos de radice ejus ascendet, et requiescet super eum spiritus sapientiae et intellectus, spiritus consilii et fortitudinis, spiritus scientiae et pietatis, et replebit eum spiritus timoris Domini 515B(Isa. XI). His igitur septem Spiritus sancti donis, si quinque virtutes, scilicet fidem, spem, charitatem, bonum opus, et castitatem adjunxeris, procul dubio duodenarium numerum adimplebis. Septem quippe, et quinque, quando in unum conveniunt, duodenarium numerum indubitanter efficiunt. Isti igitur spirituales leunculi, ut arbitror, melioris et dignioris sunt pretii, quam illi quos Salomon stare fecit super sex gradus in medio throni aurei et eburnei.

Post haec, historia quasi sub quadam admiratione refert, quia non est factum tale opus in universis regnis (III Reg. X). Et bene quidem justeque satis hoc historia retulit. Non est enim factum tale opus in universis regnis, quale est illud quod 515CVerbum caro factum est, et habitavit in nobis (Joan. I). Non est, inquam, tale opus in universis regnis factum, ut Deus, homo efficeretur, et redimeret suo sanguine genus humanum quod perierat. Vere, inquam, non est factum tam gloriosum opus in universis regnis, ut natura nostra, prorsus jam de salute desperata, abstraheretur ab inferis, et in coelo collocaretur ad dexteram Dei Patris.

RECAPITULATION FROM THE FOREGOING, ON THE IVORY THRONE WHICH SOLOMON MADE, HOW IT OUGHT TO BE REFERRED TO THE PERSON OF CHRIST.

Above we said beforehand that the Lord Jesus, 511Cwho in the Canticle of Canticles is called the Bridegroom, he himself indeed is called in the book of Kings the oracle of the temple and the ivory throne. Moreover we added this, that by the two doors of the oracle the clemency and mercy of God, namely the breasts of the Bridegroom, ought to be understood. We subjoined finally a sufficient reason, namely that as those entering the oracle entered through those doors, so assuredly through the clemency and mercy of God shall they be saved who are to be saved. It remains therefore that we approach the throne built of ivory, and set forth, according to our small measure, in what manner the same throne was made by Solomon in a figure of Christ. Then at length it will be necessary that we search out, allegorizing in the spiritual sense, how those two hands of which we spoke in the preceding place, 511Dwhich hold the seat in the ivory throne, spiritually hold the figure of clemency and mercy, that is, of the breasts of the Bridegroom. Nevertheless it must be piously considered by us whether anywhere in the divine utterances anything be said of a throne, so that what we mean to say may be strengthened by the authority of the sacred utterances. In the Apocalypse John says: From the throne proceed lightnings, and voices, and thunders (Apoc. IV). As if he should say: From Christ, who is the golden throne in which the fulness of the divinity rested, there go forth lightnings, that is, the tokens of miracles, and the voices of evangelical preaching, and the thunders, namely the terrors of the judgment to come and of the last damnation.

Moreover let this be hidden from no one, that the throne of which we are busy to speak, and which we purpose spiritually to refer to the person 512Aof Christ, is described to have been not only of ivory, but also of the brightest gold. It is plain therefore, and we are brought by this into no ambiguity, that by the gold we ought to understand the divinity 299 and by the ivory his humanity. But that gold holds the signification of the divinity of Christ needs no difficult interpretation, because as gold, for its own preciousness, goes before all metals, so without doubt the divinity of Christ, for the excellence of its dignity, surpasses not only his humanity but also all creatures. Whence elegantly the Holy Spirit, after he had said through the mouth of the historian that Solomon made a great throne of ivory, thereupon added: And he overlaid vestitvit it with the finest gold, that it might be made manifest 512Bhow far the prerogative of the divinity excelled the assumed matter of the humanity. For it is certain, and no one doubts, that a thing which clothes another thing is set above that very thing which it is seen to overclothe. As therefore the most splendid gold with its splendour and its brightness adorned, bedecked and made bright the throne which it clothed, so without doubt the divinity carried that man whom it assumed forward even unto this its own glorification, that the man himself, by participation of the divinity, was made God. Much therefore did our humanity profit, which ascended, glorified unto the participation of the supernal deity in the assumption of the assuming divinity. Finally, by how much in the first Adam his descent to the lower regions was the worse, 512Cby so much in the second Adam his ascent to the heavens was the more glorious and the more excellent. And as there was nothing whereby it could be cast down more unhappily from paradise, seduced by the devil, so there had been nothing whereby it could be lifted up more happily and more gloriously by Christ.

Our flesh therefore has been exalted in Christ above every creature and above itself; above itself, I say, because it in no wise deserved that dignity to which it has come. And this indeed is what the prophet had prophesied long before it came to pass: Let thy ancient, true counsel be done, O Lord (Isa. XXV) [Vulg. Isa. 25:1 cogitationes antiquas fideles amen]. This counsel assuredly was from eternity, and before eternity (if it be lawful so to say!), in the disposition of the highest deity, that our flesh, to be assumed and glorified by Christ, should be raised on high even unto the contemplation 512Dof the divine majesty. Concerning this glorification indeed of our humanity, or its assumption, the prophet Ezechiel (cap. XVII), refreshed by spiritual contemplation, seems to me to say: A great eagle, upheld by the great defence of her wings and adorned with much variety of feathers, came to the cedar which was in Libanus, and took of the marrow of the cedar and carried it away, and set it upon a holy and lofty mountain. [Vulg. Ezech. 17:3–4 aquila grandis magnarum alarum, longo membrorum ductu, plena plumis et varietate, venit ad Libanum et tulit medullam cedri; the setting in monte sancto et excelso answers rather to Ezech. 17:22–23] Who then is this eagle, but that one of which the prophet says: As an eagle provoketh her young to fly, and fluttering over them spreadeth her wings, and hath taken them up and carried them on her shoulders? (Deut. XXXII) And as though some one should ask of him of what eagle he was speaking, he straightway subjoined manifestly that he had said this of God: The Lord alone was his leader (ibid.). This eagle therefore 513Acame to the cedar which was in Libanus, and took to herself of its marrow, when the Word, brought forth from the heart of the Father, came to blessed Mary, and from her virgin flesh received to himself a most holy and undefiled body. And as the prophet says of that eagle, that she set upon a holy and lofty mountain the marrow which she had taken from the cedar, so indeed the Son of God, namely the Word of the Father, raised on high with himself, in the seat of his majesty at the right hand of his Father, our flesh which he assumed from the blessed Virgin Mary. But that blessed Mary is called the cedar of Libanus is plainly known from ecclesiastical usage rather than from any difficult interpretation. For thus holy Church says in her daily office: As a cedar I was exalted in Libanus (Eccli. 513BXXIV). And because Libanus is interpreted a whitening, not undeservedly is it compared to the virginity of blessed Mary. For as the material cedar was exalted in Libanus, so blessed Mary, for the singular privilege of her virginity, is glorified and chosen out before God. But let us now at length return to the golden and ivory throne treated above, and behold with spiritual eyes in what manner it held the figure of the Lord's body. Solomon made, he says, a great throne of ivory (III Reg. X). Truly that throne was great with a wonderful loftiness, because, as the Psalmist says: Great is the Lord, and exceedingly to be praised, and of his greatness there is no end (Psal. CXLIV). Moreover we declared a little before for what cause that throne was clothed with most precious gold; 513Cnow it remains that our mind bring forth into the light why the same throne, which bore the figure of the Lord's body, is of ivory. For as they have said before us who with prudent skill have disputed of the natures of things, ivory is of so wonderful a power and coldness that if a linen cloth be laid upon that same abur, and upon that same linen cloth a live coal be set above, by its own power the ivory keeps unharmed the cloth which cleaves to it, lest it be able to be utterly consumed or burnt by the live coal set above. Who therefore, even to one who knows but slightly, does not manifestly grow clear, how this comparison ought to be fitted to the body of Christ? For as ivory by its natural power keeps the cloth laid upon it unviolated from the burning of fire, so the divinity which in the body of Christ was overshadowed 513Dkept that same body immune from every blemish of sin. Wherefore also the blessed apostle Peter, speaking of the Lord Jesus, said: Who did no sin, he says, neither was guile found in his mouth (I Petr. II). But as to the six steps which are related to have been in the throne, by which one ascended step by step to the seat of the throne, sacred History has taught us with manifest reason that 300 through the six ages of the passing world God the Son promised us in mystical and hidden ways that he would come to his incarnation. For in the first age, which was from Adam unto Noe, he pledged that he would come when he declared to the woman, saying: Thy seed shall crush the head of the serpent (Gen. III) [Vulg. Gen. 3:15 ipsa conteret caput tuum, "she shall crush thy head"]. But in the second age, which was from 514ANoe unto Abraham, he showed that he would come, when he said to the sons of Noe: Increase and multiply (Gen. VIII). But in the third age, which ran from Abraham unto Moses, he prefigured that he would be present, when he blessed the offspring of that same prophet, saying: In thy seed shall all nations be blessed (Gen. XXII). But in the fourth age, which was from Moses unto David, he intimated in the manifold religion of sacrifices that he would be at hand. But in the fifth age, which was from David unto the nativity of blessed Mary [and] flourished, because he himself is King of kings and priest for ever according to the order of Melchisedech (Psal. CIX), he declared figuratively in kings and priests that he would come. The sixth age is that which is now passing, from the birth of the blessed Virgin Mary unto the day 514Bof judgment, in which the Son of God clemently deigned to come to his incarnation, just as by the sixth step one came to that glorious seat which was in the midst of the golden and ivory throne (III Reg. X). But that it is said that the top of the throne is round in the hinder part is by no means to be passed over slothfully by the prudent reader. For scarcely anyone is ignorant who abounds in any sense, that whatever is round is also perfect in every part of itself. For of Christ, whose figure that throne held, holy Church preaches to her sons, saying: Perfect God, perfect man, subsisting of a rational soul and human flesh.
