For thus it is written in the Canticle of the same prophet: « Horns are in his hands (ibid.), » namely Christ's, and in what follows: « There, he says, is his strength hidden, there shall death fall down in his sight (ibid.). » [Vulg. Hab. 3:5 ante faciem ejus ibit mors, "before his face shall go death"] Moreover, if we were minded now to bring into the midst whatever the divine eloquences are wont to utter concerning these spiritual horns, the day would more easily fail 314 us than the abundance of matter for speaking. But neither will we be silent altogether. « The horns of the just, says the Psalmist, shall be exalted (Psal. LXXIV). » Who is so dull and so stupid as to dare say that a just man has horns? But the virtues of the just man are his horns, wherewith 538Dhe himself fights against the devil, and whereby he is lifted up and exalted before God. Let Isaias then say that the vineyard was made most dear to the beloved son of oil, that is, that our soul, on account of the virtues which it has, is loved and held in great honour by the Only-begotten Son of God; and this is what is said through the prophet: « If thou shalt separate the precious from the vile, thou shalt be as my mouth (Jer. XV). » For to separate the precious from the vile is to discern virtues from vices. But who is the son of oil? Since it has now been said very often, to repeat the same thing so many times is exceedingly tedious. But as to what the Prophet says, that the Lord fenced the vineyard, it befits us to understand that fencing assuredly of our own soul, which is the spiritual vineyard of God. O how glorious and how illustrious in virtues is that soul, of which God has so great 539Acare, that it is fenced by him with loving diligence! For thus the Lord himself says through the prophet: « I will hedge up thy ways, and thou shalt go after thy lovers, and shalt not overtake them, and then thou shalt say: I will return to my former husband, because it was better with me than it now is (Osee II). » [Vulg. Osee 2:6–7 runs in the third person, sepiam viam tuam spinis … et sequetur amatores suos et non apprehendet eos … et dicet: vadam et revertar ad virum meum priorem] Moreover, tarrying a little upon this matter, it is pleasing to consider in what manner they that enclose gardens make their fences. For a most blessed soul is called by God, morally, an enclosed garden. It is indeed known to all that fences are made of stakes and of rods, in such wise namely that the stakes, fixed below, cleave to the earth on the one part, but on the other part look upward into heaven with heads erect. Afterwards, moreover, rods are interwoven crosswise, and in such fashion the fence guarding God's garden is completed. Nevertheless, 539Bwhat can we more rightly call the stakes of this spiritual fence but the saints of God, who, although they still tarry on earth for the infirmity of the flesh, are yet assiduously with Christ in heaven, and cleave to him with spiritual desires? whence one of such spiritual stakes, dwelling on earth, said: « But our conversation is in the heavens (Philipp. III). » And what are the rods, but their examples and good works, and their catholic doctrines? With these stakes therefore and with rods of this sort God fences his vineyard, because by the examples and teachings of his saints he daily tills, teaches, instructs and admonishes the souls of his faithful. Hence it is that David said: « The Lord shall send an Angel round about them that fear him, and shall keep them (Psal. XXXIII). » [Vulg. Ps. 33:8 immittet angelus Domini in circuitu timentium eum et eripiet eos, "the angel of the Lord shall encamp round about them that fear him, and shall deliver them"] Another prophet likewise no less testifies in 539Cthe person of God, saying: « He that toucheth you, toucheth the apple of my eye (Zach. II), » it plainly stands that among all the members which a man has, the apple of the eye appears the more tender and the more delicate. If therefore we are of such authority that we are made equal to the apple of God's eye, we ought not to be ungrateful for his benefit, but it is needful that we so live that we may deserve from him to be kept and fenced like a good vineyard. That also which the prophet Isaias so greatly expresses, so as to assert that God chose out stones from his vineyard. Let us not doubt that it was said of our soul, if indeed, as we have already proved above, we shall have been the vineyard of God by living spiritually. For the stones that are in God's vineyard can be understood as vices and concupiscences and unclean desires also, which, as 539Dstones make a vineyard barren, so all these make our soul barren. But now God is to be entreated, that he himself may deign to choose out and cast forth the stones from that vineyard, that is, to root out of our soul all the vices that displease him; that so our soul, as a good branch abiding in him who is the true vine, may hear with the apostles: « Without me ye can do nothing, and as the branch is not able to bring forth fruit unless it abide in the vine, so neither can ye unless ye abide in me (Joan. XV). » But as to what Isaias says in the sequel of his narration, that « God planted his chosen vineyard, » let us know that these things pertain morally to our body and to our soul. « For man was made to the image of God, and to 540Ahis likeness (Gen. I). » That planting was assuredly choice enough, which was created to the likeness and image of God. Moreover in the book of Wisdom it is found written: « God made man simple and upright, but he himself mingled himself with infinite questions (Eccle. VII). After these things, says Isaias, God built a tower in the midst of his vineyard. » [Vulg. Eccle. 7:30 quod fecerit Deus hominem rectum, without simplicem] If therefore our soul desires to be God's vineyard, it behoves it without doubt to have a tower built by God in the midst of itself. For a tower, when it is constructed, is raised up from the bottom in quadrangular form. Build therefore, O Lord God, in my vineyard a quadrangular tower, that is, found and cause to be rooted in our soul those four virtues, namely prudence, fortitude, justice, temperance, 540Band in our body no less abstinence, sobriety, chastity, true penitence. But let the remaining virtues be the stones of this spiritual building. And let the cement which binds and embraces the stones be charity itself, without which no work is said to be good, and in which every good work is made solid and founded; for thus says Paul: « Be ye rooted and founded in charity (Ephes. III). » « A winepress, says Isaias, the Lord built in his vineyard. » Known to all is the work that is done in a winepress. For in the winepress the grapes are pressed out, and being pressed out they melt, that they may be turned into most sweet wine. Although therefore the Lord says of his passion through the prophet: « I have trodden the winepress alone, and of the Gentiles there is not a man with me (Isa. 540CLXIII), » yet there is still another spiritual winepress, 315 which would that the Lord Jesus might deign to build in the vineyard of our body. For holy compunction, and the pouring out of blessed tears for the love of Christ, ought not unfittingly to be called the winepress of the soul. This spiritual winepress that most dear bride amid delights had built within her, who in the Song of Songs, sighing of her Bridegroom, said: « My soul melted, as my Beloved spoke to me (Cant. V). » There is shown therefore in this sentence the inestimable and incomparable affection of the Bride toward her bridegroom, who loved him with so sincere a love that she was even wholly melted at his word. Nevertheless with a far ampler affection and 540Da loftier charity does that same bridegroom love that same bride of his, who for love of her not only came down from heaven a little lower than the angels, but even came as far as the punishment of an ignominious death. Which indeed the prophet Jeremias attests in his lamentations, saying: « The Lord hath trodden the winepress for the virgin daughter of Juda (Thren. I). » For that most blessed soul, which in the Song of Songs is called the Bride, is in Jeremias named the virgin daughter of Juda. Now the soul wedded to God is called a virgin because of the integrity of faith, and the chastity of the body wherewith she is clothed. But daughter of Juda, for the humble confession of sins and the glorious perseverance in the praises of God. For Juda is interpreted confession, which is referred to the confession of offences and to the praise of God. 541AAlthough many things still remain that might be said of the winepress, yet I judge that these can suffice for modern hearers, who, listening willingly, are studious of brevity, and lest the volume, drawn out at greater length, weigh down the minds of tedious hearers. Truly, ought we so far to fear to offend the slothful and the tedious, that we should leave undone that which can help and instruct and edify strenuous men, exercised in the meditation of God's law? By no means. Let us see then what it is that follows in the prophet Isaias. And the Lord, says Isaias, looked for his vineyard to bring forth grapes, and behold, alas the grief! it brought forth wild grapes. Which sentence assuredly is rather to be feared than expounded. « For behold, says the apostle James, the husbandman waiteth for the precious fruit 541Bof the earth, patiently bearing till he receive the early and the latter. Be ye also patient, and strengthen your hearts in the service of God until the Lord himself come (Jacob. V). » [Vulg. Jac. 5:8 confirmate corda vestra quoniam adventus Domini appropinquavit, "for the coming of the Lord is at hand"] Moreover, that we may not leave wholly undiscussed what was said above, that the Lord looked for his vineyard to bring forth grapes, and it brought forth wild grapes, it must be said in few words that woe indeed to those who are waited for by their Creator throughout their whole life that they may bear fruit, and at last, the course of the present life being finished, appear fruitless. For generally all Christians who have received the faith, and are imbued with the works of faith, if they live ill, if they close their last day without the working of faith, beyond doubt bring forth wild grapes instead of grapes. But 541Cthou also, whosoever art called a convert, though thou seem for a time to be within the compass of a monastery or of a cloister, if before thy last day comes upon thee thou withdrawest from 542Aholy conversion and from the service of God, without doubt (if it can be said) thou makest — more than, alas the grief! — wild grapes for the Lord thy God instead of grapes. Nevertheless those are blessed and happy who, by an opposite turn, bring precious grapes instead of wild grapes to God who awaits them unto salvation. These are assuredly they who once clove to this deceitful world with excessive love, but afterwards, perceiving that they had erred, were converted with the whole affection of the heart to holy religion, and, the vanity of the world despised, delivered themselves over with all their strength to the service of God. Of whom the prophet Zacharias seems to me to say: « These are the sons of the oil of splendour who stand before the Ruler of the whole earth (Zach. IV). But since we have very often already premised above that oil signifies mercy, and mercy designates him of whom 542Bthe Psalmist says: My God, my mercy (Psal. LVIII), » [Vulg. Zach. 4:14 isti sunt duo filii olei, "these are the two sons of oil", without splendoris] this we avoid repeating again, lest we beget loathing in our hearers. But this stands for certain, that if God is called oil, they who are the sons of oil can equally be called also the sons of God. To which sentence the blessed apostle John accords with a consonant voice, saying: « Brethren, we are the sons of God, and it doth not yet appear what we shall be. We know that when he shall appear we shall be like to him, because we shall see him as he is (Joan. III). » [Vulg. I Joan. 3:2 carissimi, "dearly beloved", and the perfect nondum apparuit, "it hath not yet appeared"] Let us consider then who he is that speaks. He was a convert indeed, he was an apostle and an Evangelist, he had renounced the world, and therefore he said boldly: « We are the sons of God. » It is manifest therefore that he who more perfectly 542Crenounces the world, and more familiarly, after the manner of the apostles, cleaves to God, is a son of God, and can truly be so called.

541Here ends the third Tome.

INCIPIT QUARTUS TOMUS Super hoc quod dicitur: Trahe me post te, curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum.

541CAd quartum tomum exponendum hujus libri accedentes, ipsum qui quarto die faciendo luminaria coeli mundum illuminavit pura mente et summa intentione 316 invocare nos convenit, quatenus velamen litterae Veteris Testamenti dignetur nobis inferre, ut secundum voluntatem ejus sensum spiritualem in litteris peplo obscuriori coopertis, valeamus reperire. Et enim velamen magnum super istam sententiam positum, scilicet: « Trahe me post te. Sicut in octonario opere Dei in libro Genesis, et in octo sententiis Amos prophetae, ubi dicitur: Super tribus sceleribus Damasci et super quatuor non convertam (Amos I), » et octonaria visione Zachariae prophetae. Verumtamen istis sententiis paululum praelibatis, ad ea quae superius praefati sumus stylum nostrum dirigamus, et cujus sit haec vox quae dicit: Trahe me post te, vel cui loquatur spirituali indagine perquiramus. Sanctae Ecclesiae hanc vocem esse, quae dicit: Trahe me post te, nullus sapiens dubitat: ipsum vero sponsum ejus esse 542CChristum Filium Dei, cui ipsa loquitur, omnis auctoritas tam Novi quam Veteris Testamenti consona voce praedicat. Sed mirandum valde est, qua ratione sancta Ecclesia a Filio Dei, id est a Sponso suo trahi se postulat, quandoquidem ista spiritalis attractio ad Patrem maxime ex auctoritate Filii ejus pertineat. Sic enim idem Filius Dei de eodem charissimo Patre suo dicit in Evangelio: « Nemo venit ad me, nisi Pater meus attraxerit eum (Joan. VI). » Denique idem piissimus Pater de eadem praeclara et praeelecta Filii sui Sponsa, id est sancta Ecclesia, per Jeremiam prophetam ait: « In charitate perpetua dilexi te, ideo attraxi te miserans tui (Jere. XXXI). » Fatendum est igitur, quia Deus Pater abundantia suavitatis suae, in invisibili amore charitatis internae, ad hoc attraxit mentem Sponsae Filii sui, ut ipsa eadem charissima Sponsa postularet attrahi ab eodem Sponso suo, videlicet Filio Dei. Sed certe, o tu auditor, noli credere istam tractionem violentam, quam exoptat sibi fieri Sponsa a Sponso suo. 543ANon enim est violenta, sed est omnibus modis charitativa et voluntaria. Hinc est namque quod Psalmista spirituali desiderio inflammatus, ut laudes Dei digne pronuntiare valeat, attrahit gratiam Spiritus sancti, sicut scriptum est: « Os meum aperui, et attraxi Spiritum (Psal. CXVIII), » subauditur sanctum, et in quid attraxisset eum manifeste subjunxit, dicens: « quia mandata tua desiderabam (ibid.). » Datur ergo nobis intelligi, quoniam quidem in desiderio mandatorum Dei attrahitur gratia Spiritus sancti. Est praeterea alia valde laudabilis et approbabilis tractio cum quemlibet in aliqua anxietate positum constat, cui compatiens et condolens amicus ejus eamdem anxietatem cum illo condolendo supportat, quatenus illum ab eadem 543Banxietate liberare valeat. Tali quoque modo fit, ut ille qui ope et compassione amici sui ab illa anxietate liberatur, amplius solito eumdem amicum suum diligere cogatur, et miro atque inaestimabili charitatis affectu ad dilectionem trahitur illius. Hoc igitur piissimo modo sancta Ecclesia divino charitatis amore succensa, ineffabilis pietatis compassione considerata, quam Christus de ea habuit, quando pro ea mortem subiit, dicit: Trahe me post te; ac si dicat: Sicut tu mortuus es pro me, sic da mihi ut ego valeam mori pro te. Vel certe trahe me post te, id est fac me ad tuae majestatis cognitionem pervenire, ut non jam per speculum et in aenigmate, sed facie ad faciem (I Cor. XIII) possim videre quomodo tu es in Patre, et Pater in te, et quomodo 543CSpiritus sanctus procedit a te et a Patre. Curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum, haec vox videtur esse adolescentularum, id est fidelium animarum dicentium ad Sponsum. Mater nostra sancta Ecclesia de excellentia meritorum suorum in membris fortioribus, id est apostolis et martyribus confisa, te deprecatur, ut ad tuae majestatis notitiam pervenire mereatur, sed nos adolescentulae, id est infirmiores et debiliores tamen fideles animae nondum pro te tantum laboravimus, ut ad lucem inaccessibilem in qua tu habitas suspirare audeamus, sed curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum, hoc est in hoc saeculo bene vivendo sequemur exempla sanctorum tuorum, quatenus post mortem pervenire valeamus ad consortium ipsorum. Vel certe curremus in odorem 543Dunguentorum tuorum, id est gradiemus viam mandatorum tuorum, quatenus charitate tua dilatati recipere valeamus infra armaliolum cordis nostri, charismata Spiritus sancti. Curramus ergo, fratres charissimi in odorem unguentorum Dei, ut dicatur de nobis: « Beati immaculati in via qui ambulant in lege Domini (Psal. CXVIII). » Curramus, inquam, cum apostolo Paulo (Philip. III) sic ut comprehendamus cum ipso scilicet perpetuam vitam; ille enim bene currit, qui pie et juste vivendo, ultimum diem vitae suae religioso fine concludit. Denique qui currit, locum in quo erat deserit, et ad locum in quo non erat tendit. Sic ergo nos deseramus gloriam hujus mundi, fugiamus concupiscentias et voluptates 544Ahujus saeculi, aemulemur cum Paulo (I Cor. XII) charismata meliora, quae sunt unguenta redolentia, id est dona Spiritus sancti, quatenus de lacu miseriae, et de luto faecis abstracti, pervenire valeamus ad gaudia perpetua paradisi.

HERE BEGINS THE FOURTH TOME Upon that which is said: Draw me after thee, we will run in the odour of thy ointments.

541CAs we approach the exposition of the fourth Tome of this book, it befits us to invoke with pure mind and utmost intention 316 him who on the fourth day, by making the lights of heaven, gave light to the world, that he may deign to bring in upon us the veil of the letter of the Old Testament, so that according to his will we may be able to find the spiritual sense in letters covered with a darker robe. For indeed a great veil set over that sentence, namely: « Draw me after thee. As in the eightfold work of God in the book of Genesis, and in the eight sentences of Amos the prophet, where it is said: For three crimes of Damascus, and for four, I will not turn (Amos I), » and in the eightfold vision of Zacharias the prophet. Nevertheless, these sentences being a little tasted beforehand, let us direct our stylus to those things we premised above, and let us search out by spiritual investigation whose is this voice that says: Draw me after thee, or to whom it speaks. That this voice is holy Church's, which says: Draw me after thee, no wise man doubts: but that her bridegroom is 542CChrist the Son of God, to whom she herself speaks, all the authority of the New as well as of the Old Testament proclaims with a consonant voice. But it is greatly to be wondered at, for what reason holy Church asks to be drawn by the Son of God, that is, by her Bridegroom, since indeed that spiritual attraction belongs chiefly to the Father by the authority of his Son. For thus the same Son of God says of the same most dear Father of his in the Gospel: « No man cometh to me, unless my Father hath drawn him (Joan. VI). » [Vulg. Joan. 6:44 nemo potest venire ad me nisi Pater qui misit me traxerit eum, "no man can come to me, except the Father, who hath sent me, draw him"] Finally that same most loving Father says of the same illustrious and fore-chosen Bride of his Son, that is, of holy Church, through Jeremias the prophet: « I have loved thee with a perpetual charity, therefore have I drawn thee, having mercy on thee (Jere. XXXI). » It must be confessed therefore that God the Father, by the abundance of his sweetness, in the invisible love of inward charity, has drawn the mind of his Son's Bride to this, that she herself, that same most dear Bride, should ask to be drawn by that same Bridegroom of hers, namely the Son of God. But surely, O thou hearer, do not believe that drawing to be violent which the Bride longs to have done to her by her Bridegroom. 543AFor it is not violent, but is in all ways charitable and voluntary. Hence it is indeed that the Psalmist, inflamed with spiritual desire, that he may be able worthily to utter the praises of God, draws in the grace of the Holy Spirit, as it is written: « I opened my mouth, and drew in the Spirit (Psal. CXVIII), » — the holy is understood; and for what he had drawn him in he manifestly subjoined, saying: « because I desired thy commandments (ibid.). » It is given us therefore to understand that the grace of the Holy Spirit is drawn in by the desire of God's commandments. There is besides another drawing, greatly to be praised and approved, when someone is known to be set in some anguish, and his friend, having compassion and grieving with him, bears up that same anguish with him by grieving together, that he may be able to free him from that same 543Banguish. In such wise also it comes about that he who by the help and compassion of his friend is freed from that anguish is constrained to love that same friend of his more than he was wont, and is drawn by a wondrous and inestimable affection of charity to the love of him. In this most loving manner therefore holy Church, kindled with the divine love of charity, having considered the compassion of unspeakable tenderness which Christ had of her when he underwent death for her, says: Draw me after thee; as if she said: As thou hast died for me, so grant me that I may be able to die for thee. Or else draw me after thee, that is, make me to come to the knowledge of thy majesty, so that no longer through a glass and in a dark manner, but face to face (I Cor. XIII) I may be able to see how thou art in the Father, and the Father in thee, and how 543Cthe Holy Spirit proceeds from thee and from the Father. We will run in the odour of thy ointments, this voice seems to be that of the maidens, that is, of the faithful souls, speaking to the Bridegroom. Our mother holy Church, confident in the excellence of her merits in her stronger members, that is, the apostles and martyrs, beseeches thee that she may deserve to come to the knowledge of thy majesty; but we maidens, that is, the weaker and feebler yet faithful souls, have not yet laboured so much for thee that we dare sigh after the inaccessible light in which thou dwellest, but we will run in the odour of thy ointments, that is, by living well in this world we shall follow the examples of thy saints, that after death we may be able to come to their fellowship. Or else we will run in the odour 543Dof thy ointments, that is, we shall walk the way of thy commandments, that, enlarged by thy charity, we may be able to receive within the armaliolum of our heart the charisms of the Holy Spirit. Let us run therefore, most dear brethren, in the odour of God's ointments, that it may be said of us: « Blessed are the undefiled in the way, who walk in the law of the Lord (Psal. CXVIII). » Let us run, I say, with the apostle Paul (Philip. III) so that we may lay hold with him, namely, of everlasting life; for he runs well who, living piously and justly, closes the last day of his life with a religious end. Lastly, he who runs forsakes the place in which he was, and stretches toward the place in which he was not. So therefore let us forsake the glory of this world, let us flee the concupiscences and pleasures 544Aof this age, let us with Paul be emulous (I Cor. XII) of the better charisms, which are the ointments giving forth odour, that is, the gifts of the Holy Spirit, that, drawn out of the pit of misery and out of the mire of dregs, we may be able to come to the perpetual joys of paradise.

RECAPITULATIO A SUPERIORIBUS SECUNDUM ALLEGORICUM ET MYSTICUM SENSUM.

317 Ipso auxiliante qui ait: « Si exaltatus fuero a terra, omnia traham ad me (Joan. XII), » superius locuti sumus de illa sententia quae est in Canticis canticorum, scilicet trahe me post te. Super est modo ut eodem opitulante de septenario vel octonario opere Dei, ad laudem ipsius eloquamur, et qua ratione octo aetatibus hujus saeculi unumquodque opus congruat, in lucem proferamus, verumtamen 544Bprius necesse est, ut saeculum octo per aetates distinguamus sic demum singulis aetatum distinctionibus unumquodque opus sigillatim rectius assignabimus. Prima igitur aetas hujus saeculi exstitit ab Adam usque ad Noe. Secunda vero a Noe usque ad Abraham. Tertia autem ab Abraham usque ad Moysen. Quarta exstitit a Moyse usque ad David. Quinta a David usque ad adventum Christi. In sexta aetate venit Dominus Jesus Christus, quae aetas duravit usque ad tempus illud quo persecutio in sancta Ecclesia cessavit. Septima aetas ipsa est quae nunc agitur usque ad diem judicii. Octava erit aetas peracto judicii die, quae carebit fine. Sane illud admodum est mirabile, quia quae legitur Deus fecisse in illis septem diebus, ea nimirum eleganti ordine et 544Csapientissima ratione congruunt istis septem vel octo aetatibus praefatis. Igitur ad opus primi diei disserendum properemus, et freti sacrosancta aspiratione ipsius qui primum diem fecit, quid sacramenti ipsum opus in se contineat, salva sanctorum Patrum omnimoda expositione, mens nostra in lucem proferre non pertimescat. « In principio, inquit Moyses, fecit Deus coelum et terram (Gen. I). » O Moyses Dei inquilinus. O secretorum divinorum reserator arcanus! Quis tibi intimavit quod Deus in principio fecit coelum et terram, nisi ipse idem qui haec omnia ad laudem et honorem, et gloriam nominis sui condidit? Sed in quo principio? In eo nimirum qui ait in Evangelio: « Ego principium qui loquor vobis (Joan. VIII). » In eo, inquam, de quo 544DJoannes evangelista testatur: « In principio erat Verbum et omnia per ipsum facta sunt (Joan. I). » In eo, inquam, de quo Psalmista ad Patrem dicit: « Et tu in principio, Domine Deus Pater, hoc in tuo Filio terram fundasti et opera manuum tuarum sunt coeli (Psal. CI). » Considera, o benignissime auditor, et eloquiorum spiritualium placidissime amator, quoniam quidem duae res duntaxat in opere primi diei resplendent: Creator scilicet qui creaturam condidit, et creatura quae a Deo suo facta est, quae modo illi servit. Nihil ergo aliud in ipso opere prorsus reperire poteris si ipsum opus primi diei mille per annos scrutatus fueris. Creatura nempe a Deo Patre per Filium facta ibi declaratur, 545ASpiritus sanctus super aquas, ut eas nobis sanctificaret ad gratiam baptismi sui deferri similiter inibi praedicatur. Et sicut sancta Trinitas in Patre et Filio et Spiritu sancto ostenditur per effectum operis, sic ipsum opus quod factum est quodammodo in se repraesentat similitudinem sacrosanctae et individuae Trinitatis. Omne enim quod a Deo creatum est, ex quo creatum exstitit esse habuit, quantitati subjacuit, qualitati illam subesse oportuit. Dilucidemus ergo quod diximus, ne forte simplicis auditoris sensum obscuritate obsessum relinquamus. Manifestum est quia quidquid est, aut per se, aut cum alio, aut per alium, aut nullo modo habet esse. Deus solus per se solummodo est, sed quod ab eo creatum est, ut per ipsum consistat qui 545Bcreavit, necesse est per se esse, et sine alio esse, unum dignoscitur esse, quod ad Deum proprie pertinet. Per alium esse et sine alio esse non posse, creaturae proprium comprobatur esse, quae absque Creatore suo non valet esse, etiam si aliquo modo videatur esse. Quantitati vero subjacet quod creatum est, quia aut parvum, aut magnum, aut mediocre est. Qualitatis legibus necessario subest creatura, quoniam, aut est alba, aut nigra, aut medio colore colorata. Ecce paucis verbis Deo inspirante noster stylus elucidat quam rationabiliter omnis creatura sanctae individuae Trinitatis imaginem in se repraesentat. Praeterea si adhuc subtilius internis oculis ipsam superficiem hujus sacrae paginae 545Cperscrutati fuerimus, omnes leges illarum septem artium, quae secundum philosophiam liberales vocantur, reperiemus. Ut enim ad rhetoricam artem accedamus, juxta locutionis modos qui flores dicuntur, prima oratio scilicet: « In principio creavit Deus coelum et terram, » unimembris esse dignoscitur. Secundam vero orationem videlicet « terra erat inanis et vacua, et tenebrae erant super faciem abyssi et spiritus Dei ferebatur super aquas, » trimembrem esse clara voce fatebuntur, qui in illius artis peritia vel praerogativa gloriantur. Sane hoc nullatenus sub silentio praeterire debemus, quod illa prima oratio quae est unimembris, unum Deum nobis praedicat; secunda autem quae est trimembris, ipsum Deum verum esse, qui ex una substantia et 545Dex tribus personis constat, nihilominus nobis confirmat atque denuntiat. Ad nostrae fidei firmamentum confirmandum, quae de Patre et Filio et Spiritu sancto procedit, quae non solum in hoc libro, 318 verum etiam in omnibus divinis voluminibus repetitur, ista dixisse sufficiat. Beatus Augustinus super coelo et terra talem profert interpretationem: « Fecit, inquit, Deus in principio coelum et terram, » id est angelicam vel coelestem et terrenam creaturam. Nos quoque possumus salvo sensu ecclesiasticae regulae, coelum et terram ad personam primi patris nostri Adae allegorice referre. Ipse enim antequam peccaret velut coelum exstitit, sed post peccatum a Deo audivit: « Terra es et in terram ibis (Gen. III). » Et bene quidem Adam ante perpetrationem 546Apeccati coelum potest appellari, quia sine dubio, ut ait sanctus Augustinus, nisi illum miserabilem reatum incurrisset, ad coelestem gloriam sine gustu mortis pervenisset. Potest adhuc Adam alio modo coelum et terra nuncupari, quia ipse animam de coelesti natura habuit, et corpus ejus de terra originem duxit. Sic enim Scriptura dicit: « Fecit Deus hominem de limo terrae, et inspiravit in faciem ejus spiraculum vitae (Gen. II). » Est praeterea et alia rationabilis intelligentia, qua primus parens noster debeat vocari coelum et terra. De eo namque Eva facta est; sicut scriptum est: « Immisit Deus soporem in Adam et unam de costis ejus tulit, et aedificavit costam illam in mulierem (ibid.). » Si igitur per coelum accipimus Adam, et per terram 546Baccipimus Evam, noster intellectus directo tramite incedit per ecclesiasticam regulam. Dicamus aliter: Non immerito coelum et terram in Adam possumus interpretari, quia de ejus propagine sancta Ecclesia debebat egredi, quae in electos, coelum, et in reprobos quos continet, terra potest appellari. Vel certe ideo Adam coelum et terra non incongrue dici valet, quia futurum erat ut ille de ejus stirpe per lineas generationum originem duceret, qui coelestia simul et terrena per sanguinem suum pacificando conjungeret; et damnum quod illa coelestis curia in lapsu Sathaelis sociorumque ejus pertulerat, ex filiis ejusdem Adae, licet salutari fonte regeneratis, redintegraret. Quod autem dicitur quia terra inanis et vacua erat, sic intelligi oportet, quoniam quidem 546CAdam et Eva postquam diabolo magis quam Deo peccando consenserunt, statim inanes et vacui a gratia Spiritus sancti et reliquis virtutibus, quibus antea refulgebant, remanserunt. Pari quoque sententia accipiendum est quod sequitur: « Tenebrae erant super abyssum (Gen. I). » Abyssus, ut ait sanctus Augustinus, est quaedam profunditas quae fundo caret; quae nimirum quanto est profundior, tanto remota ambiguitate est tenebrosior. Nonne satis tibi videntur esse tenebrosi, qui putabant se posse latere a facie Dei? Sic nempe post transgressionem ipse ait pro se et pro sua conjuge. « Audivi, Domine, vocem tuam et abscondi me (Gen. III). » Tenebrae ergo erant super faciem abyssi, quia obscuritas peccati, et defensionis audacia, et involucrum mendacii obsidebat 546Dcorda eorum, qui illum fallere verbis nitebantur, cui Salomon ait: « Tu solus nosti corda filiorum hominum (II Par. II). » Illud vero quod Spiritus Dei super aquas ferebatur, sic nos oportet interpretari, ut intelligamus jam nunc ex eo tempore gratiam baptismi per virtutem Spiritus sancti consecrari, quo baptismo progenies Adae ab hac transgressione debebat purificari. Sed et illud quod Deus dixit: « Fiat lux et facta est lux (Gen. I), » satis decenter ipsi Adae aptari valet. Ipse enim quasi lux prae caeteris creaturis, quibus praelatus erat, emicuit, ideoque participatione sapientissimae lucis, quae in eo latebat, omnibus rebus nomina imposuit. « Et vidit Deus lucem quod esset bona (ibid.) » 547APlacuisset Deo utique si Adam permansisset in eodem statu, in quo ab eo conditus fuerat. Sed quia ipse peccavit, magna divisio fuit inter illum statum, quem ante lapsum habuit, et illum in quo post libamen peccati remansit, et haec est divisio « lucis a tenebris. Appellavitque lucem diem, et tenebras noctem (ibid.), » quod est dicere: Ante transgressionem Adam justus, id est lux et dies exstitit; post transgressionis reatum, nox et tenebrae, id est peccator permansit. « Et factum est vespere et mane dies unus (ibid.). » Si per mane animam Adae quae coelestis naturae erat, et per corpus ejus vesperam volumus intelligere, potest talis solutio haberi hujus sententiae: « Vespere et mane factum est dies unus, » 548Aid est ex anima et corpore factus est homo unus. Vel certe si per mane quod pertinet ad lucem, volumus interpretari illud esse gloriosum, in quo Deus creavit Adam, et si per vesperam quae tenebras respicit interpretamur infelicem reatum quem Adam incurrit, possumus dicere, quoniam quidem Adam, quem Deus justum condidit, licet ipse peccatorem se fecerit, tamen idem ipse permansit, excepto quod peccati naevo non caruit. Nostra igitur intentio ad hoc tendit; quatenus eo suppeditante qui primum diem fecit, illud quod actum est in ipso primo die, referamus per allegoriam ad ea quae gesta sunt in prima saeculi aetate.

547Explicit quartus tomus in Cantica canticorum.

RECAPITULATION FROM THE FOREGOING ACCORDING TO THE ALLEGORICAL AND MYSTICAL SENSE.

317 By the help of him who said: « If I be lifted up from the earth, I will draw all things to myself (Joan. XII), » we have spoken above of that sentence which is in the Song of Songs, namely draw me after thee. It remains now that, by the same one's aid, we speak concerning the sevenfold or eightfold work of God, to his praise, and bring to light for what reason each several work agrees with the eight ages of this world; nevertheless 544Bit is first needful that we distinguish the world by eight ages, and so at length we shall the more rightly assign each several work, one by one, to the several distinctions of the ages. The first age therefore of this world was from Adam unto Noe. The second, from Noe unto Abraham. The third, from Abraham unto Moses. The fourth was from Moses unto David. The fifth, from David unto the coming of Christ. In the sixth age came the Lord Jesus Christ, which age lasted until that time in which persecution ceased in holy Church. The seventh age is that very one which is now being passed, until the day of judgment. The eighth will be the age after the day of judgment is done, which shall lack an end. Truly this is exceedingly marvellous, that the things which God is read to have done in those seven days agree, in an elegant order and 544Cmost wise reason, with those seven or eight aforesaid ages. Let us hasten therefore to the discussing of the work of the first day, and, relying on the most holy inspiration of him who made the first day, let not our mind be afraid to bring to light what of sacrament that work contains in itself, saving the exposition of the holy Fathers in every kind. « In the beginning, says Moses, God made heaven and earth (Gen. I). » [Vulg. Gen. 1:1 creavit, "created" — as the same verse is printed below in this very chunk] O Moses, God's lodger! O secret unsealer of the divine mysteries! Who made known to thee that God in the beginning made heaven and earth, unless that same one who founded all these things to the praise and honour and glory of his name? But in what beginning? In that one assuredly who says in the Gospel: « I am the beginning, who speak to you (Joan. VIII). » In him, I say, of whom 544DJohn the evangelist testifies: « In the beginning was the Word, and all things were made by him (Joan. I). » In him, I say, of whom the Psalmist says to the Father: « And thou in the beginning, O Lord God the Father, in thy Son didst found the earth, and the heavens are the works of thy hands (Psal. CI). » [Vulg. Ps. 101:26 initio tu Domine terram fundasti et opera manuum tuarum sunt caeli, without Deus Pater and without hoc in tuo Filio] Consider, O most kindly hearer, and most placid lover of spiritual eloquences, that only two things shine forth in the work of the first day: namely the Creator who founded the creature, and the creature which was made by her God, and which now serves him. Nothing else therefore wilt thou be able to find at all in that work, though thou search that work of the first day for a thousand years. For the creature is there declared to have been made by God the Father through the Son; 545Athe Holy Spirit over the waters, that he might sanctify them for us unto the grace of his baptism, is likewise proclaimed to be borne there. And as the holy Trinity is shown in Father and Son and Holy Spirit by the effect of the work, so the work itself which was made in a certain manner represents in itself the likeness of the most holy and undivided Trinity. For everything that was created by God, from the moment it was created, had being, lay subject to quantity, and it behoved it to lie under quality. Let us make clear then what we have said, lest perchance we leave the sense of a simple hearer beset with obscurity. It is manifest that whatever is, has being either through itself, or with another, or through another, or in no way. God alone is only through himself, but that which was created by him, so that it may subsist through him who 545Bcreated it, must needs be through itself; and to be without another is discerned to be one, which properly pertains to God. To be through another, and not to be able to be without another, is proved to be proper to a creature, which apart from its Creator is not able to be, even if in some manner it seem to be. But that which was created lies subject to quantity, because it is either small, or great, or middling. And a creature is necessarily under the laws of quality, since it is either white, or black, or coloured with a middle colour. Behold, in a few words, God inspiring, our stylus makes plain how reasonably every creature represents in itself the image of the holy undivided Trinity. Moreover, if we shall have searched out still more subtly with inward eyes the very surface of this sacred page, 545Cwe shall find all the laws of those seven arts which according to philosophy are called liberal. For, that we may come to the art of rhetoric, according to the modes of speech which are called flowers, the first sentence, namely: « In the beginning God created heaven and earth, » is discerned to be of one member. But the second sentence, to wit « the earth was void and empty, and darkness was upon the face of the deep, and the Spirit of God was borne over the waters, » they will confess with a clear voice to be of three members, who glory in the skill or the prerogative of that art. Truly this we ought by no means to pass over in silence, that that first sentence which is of one member preaches to us one God; but the second, which is of three members, no less confirms and declares to us that the same God is true, who consists of one substance and 545Dof three persons. For the confirming of the firmament of our faith, which proceeds from the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, and which is repeated not only in this book, 318 but also in all the divine volumes, let it suffice to have said these things. The blessed Augustine puts forth such an interpretation upon heaven and earth: « God, says he, made in the beginning heaven and earth, » that is, the angelic or heavenly and the earthly creature. We also can, saving the sense of the ecclesiastical rule, refer heaven and earth allegorically to the person of our first father Adam. For he himself, before he sinned, stood as it were heaven, but after sin he heard from God: « Thou art earth, and into earth shalt thou go (Gen. III). » [Vulg. Gen. 3:19 pulvis es et in pulverem reverteris, "dust thou art, and into dust shalt thou return"] And well indeed can Adam before the perpetrating 546Aof sin be called heaven, because without doubt, as saint Augustine says, had he not run into that wretched guilt, he would have come to the heavenly glory without the taste of death. Adam can further in another manner be named heaven and earth, because he himself had a soul of heavenly nature, and his body drew its origin from the earth. For thus Scripture says: « God made man of the slime of the earth, and breathed into his face the breath of life (Gen. II). » And there is besides another reasonable understanding whereby our first parent ought to be called heaven and earth. For of him Eve was made; as it is written: « God sent a deep sleep upon Adam, and took one of his ribs, and built that rib into a woman (ibid.). » If therefore by heaven we take Adam, and by earth 546Bwe take Eve, our understanding walks by a straight path along the ecclesiastical rule. Let us say otherwise: Not undeservedly can we interpret heaven and earth in Adam, because of his offspring holy Church was to go forth, which in the elect can be called heaven, and in the reprobate whom she contains, earth. Or else Adam may not unfittingly be called heaven and earth for this cause, that it was to come to pass that from his stock, through the lines of the generations, he should draw his origin who by his blood should join together, by making peace, things heavenly and earthly at once; and should restore, out of the sons of the same Adam, though regenerated by the saving font, the loss which that heavenly court had borne in the fall of Sathael and of his companions. But as to what is said, that the earth was void and empty, it must be so understood, that indeed 546CAdam and Eve, after they consented by sinning to the devil rather than to God, remained at once void and empty of the grace of the Holy Spirit and of the remaining virtues wherewith they before shone. With a like sentence also is that to be taken which follows: « Darkness was upon the deep (Gen. I). » The deep, as saint Augustine says, is a certain depth which lacks a bottom; which assuredly, the deeper it is, so much the more, ambiguity removed, is it dark. Do they not seem to thee to be dark enough, who thought they could lie hid from the face of God? For thus after the transgression he himself said for himself and for his wife: « I heard, O Lord, thy voice, and I hid myself (Gen. III). » Darkness therefore was upon the face of the deep, because the obscurity of sin, and the boldness of self-defence, and the wrapping of lying beset 546Dthe hearts of those who strove to deceive with words him to whom Solomon says: « Thou alone knowest the hearts of the sons of men (II Par. II). » But as to that, that the Spirit of God was borne over the waters, it behoves us so to interpret it, that we understand that already from that time the grace of baptism was consecrated by the power of the Holy Spirit, by which baptism the offspring of Adam was to be purified from this transgression. And that also which God said: « Let there be light, and light was made (Gen. I), » can fittingly enough be applied to Adam himself. For he shone forth as it were a light before the other creatures over which he was set, and therefore by participation of the most wise light which lay hid in him he imposed names upon all things. « And God saw the light, that it was good (ibid.) » 547AIt would surely have pleased God if Adam had remained in that same state in which he had been founded by him. But because he himself sinned, there was a great division between that state which he had before the fall, and that in which he remained after the tasting of sin; and this is the division « of the light from the darkness. And he called the light day, and the darkness night (ibid.), » which is to say: Before the transgression Adam stood just, that is, light and day; after the guilt of transgression he remained night and darkness, that is, a sinner. « And there was evening and morning, one day (ibid.). » If by the morning we would understand the soul of Adam, which was of heavenly nature, and by his body the evening, such a solution of this sentence may be had: « Evening and morning were made one day, » 548Athat is, of soul and body one man was made. Or else, if by the morning, which pertains to light, we would interpret that glorious estate in which God created Adam, and if by the evening, which regards the darkness, we interpret the unhappy guilt which Adam ran into, we can say that indeed Adam, whom God founded just, although he made himself a sinner, yet remained the very same, save that he lacked not the blemish of sin. Our intention therefore tends to this: that, by the furnishing of him who made the first day, we may refer that which was done in that first day, by allegory, to the things that were done in the first age of the world.

547Here ends the fourth Tome upon the Song of Songs.

INCIPIT QUINTUS TOMUS Super hoc quod dicitur: Introduxit me rex in cellaria sua, exsultabimus et laetabimur in te memores uberum tuorum: super vinum recti diligunt te (Cant. I).

547B319 Sicut sacra historia Veteris Testamenti cum legeretur mentibus audientium spirituali modulatione intimavit, Isaac sanctus patriarcha filius illius nobilissimi et egregii patriarchae Abrahae olim advesperascente jam die exierat ad meditandum in agrum (Gen. XXIV): et factum dum deambularet meditans levavit oculos suos, et vidit Rebeccam sponsam suam venientem, et occurrit ei obviam, et duxit eam in tabernaculo matris suae Sarae, et in tantum dilexit illam, ut etiam dolorem qui ex morte ejusdem matris suae sibi acciderat, maxima ex parte temperaret. Quia enim Isaac interpretatur risus vel gaudium, non immerito per illum spiritualiter intelligimus Dominum nostrum Jesum Christum qui est gaudium et laetitia, jubilatio et exsultatio, et jucunditas 547Cindeficiens sanctorum suorum. Quod autem ipse Dominus Jesus filius sit Abrahae secundum carnem, testatur Matthaeus evangelista cum dicit: « Liber generationis Jesu Christi filii David, filii Abraham (Matth. I). » Illud vero quod sanctus Isaac advesperascente jam die egressus fuerat ad meditandum in agrum, significat quod Dominus Jesus Christus appropinquante hujus saeculi fine, venit in hunc mundum ad hoc, ut impleret sacrosanctae suae incarnationis mysterium. Deambulatio quippe sancti Isaac in agrum insinuat nobis adventum Domini nostri Jesu Christi in hunc mundum. Unde et Sponsa sua charissima eidem Domino Jesu dicit in Canticis canticorum: « Veni, dilecte mi, commoremur in silvis, egrediamur in agrum (Cant. VII), » licet beatus 547DAmbrosius ipsum agrum interpretetur castellum. Praeterea hoc quod Isaac in tabernaculum matris suae introduxit Rebeccam, et pro amore ejus maternae mortis temperavit dolorem, significat quod Dominus Jesus Christus spiritualibus nuptiis et epithalamicis Canticis sibi sociavit sanctam Ecclesiam. Quam nimirum adeo dilexit amore spirituali, ut etiam 548BSynagogam, scilicet matrem suam carnalem, traderet oblivioni. Denique ipsa Rebecca interpretatur patientia, quam Isaac in suum tabernaculum introduxit, quod utique mysteriis sanctae Ecclesiae eleganter alludit; quam Dominus noster Jesus Christus finito certamine hujus saeculi introducet ad coelestis gloriae mansiones aeternas, cujus equidem Ecclesiae fundatoribus, scilicet sanctis apostolis, ipse dicit: « In patientia vestra possidebitis animas vestras (Luc. XXI). » Hanc igitur beatissimam ac felicissimam introductionem in hoc libello considerans Christi Sponsa, et quasi jam facta sit quae adhuc post diem judicii est futura, gaudio inenarrabili et jubilatione incomprehensibili exsultans adolescentulas sic alloquitur, dicens: Introduxit me rex in cellaria sua 548C(Cant. I). Sane quis est iste rex a quo ista Sponsa se dicit introductam esse in cellaria? Ille nimirum de quo propheta David ait: « Deus, judicium tuum regi da et justitiam tuam filio regis (Psal. LXXI). » Quod utique idem rex summi regis filius in Evangelio manifestat clarius cum dicit: « Pater non judicat quemquam, sed omne judicium dedit Filio (Joan. V). » Quae vero sunt ista cellaria, nisi illa tabernacula vel atria de quibus David propheta cecinit: « Quam dilecta tabernacula tua, Domine virtutum, concupiscit et desiderat anima mea in atria Domini? » (Psal. LXXXIII.) Haec siquidem tabernacula vel atria Dominus Jesus in Evangelio mansiones appellat, ubi ait: « In domo Patris mei mansiones multae sunt (Joan. XIV). » Sancta igitur Ecclesia, 548DChristi Sponsa, istas mansiones sive tabernacula vel atria spiritualiter considerans, et ad ea postposito saeculi amore toto desiderio animi anhelans, quasi jam ea praesentialiter possideat, quae peracto die judicii possessuram se esse non dubitat, dicit: Introduxit me rex in cellaria sua. Vere, inquit sancta Ecclesia, introduxit me rex in cellaria sua, quia « quod 549Aoculus non vidit, nec auris audivit, nec in cor hominis ascendit (I Cor. II), » sicut mihi promiserat, fideliter donavit. Unde Isaias propheta ait: « Oculus non vidit, Deus, absque te quae praeparasti diligentibus te (Isa. LXIV). » Hujus igitur ineffabilis gaudii charitate, jubilationis incomprehensibilis affectu, contemplatione summae et inenarrabilis Trinitatis, adolescentulae, id est fideles animae accensae dicunt: Exsultabimus et laetabimur in te. Tunc siquidem completo resurrectionis et judicii generalis sacramento, quando fidelibus suis dicet Filius Dei: « Venite, benedicti Patris mei, percipite regnum quod vobis paratum est ab origine mundi (Matth. XXV), » et quando corpus quod corrumpitur ultra non aggravabit animam, nec terrena inhabitatio deprimet 549Bsensum multa cogitantem, sed anima videns Deum sicuti est facie ad faciem (I Cor. XIII), participatione visionis summae et incomprehensibilis Trinitatis, carnem suam faciet ex toto spiritualem; tunc, inquam, 320 verius dicent istae adolescentulae, id est fideles animae, exsultabimus et laetabimur in te. In terra enim nobis degentibus et praeceptis tuis fideliter obedientibus, magna quidem promisisti, sed in coelo majora reddidisti. Idcirco exsultabimus et laetabimur in te. Memores uberum tuorum, id est praeceptorum tuorum, per quorum observantiam et Spiritus sancti gratiam, pervenimus ad contemplandam tuae majestatis claritatem; vel certe exsultabimus et laetabimur in te, memores uberum tuorum super vinum, id est eloquiorum tuorum, quae eloquia dulcia et melliflua 549Cfacta sunt nobis super vinum, hoc est plus quam doctrina Mosaicae legis, quae merito propter austeritatem praeceptorum vino comparatur: quod vinum parvulos necare dicitur, quia, sicut dicit Paulus, « Lex iram (id est mortem) operatur (Rom. IV). » Ergo, rex Christe, quia tot beneficia contulisti Sponsae tuae, ideo filii ejusdem Sponsae, id est, recti diligunt te. Recti cogitatione, recti locutione, recti operatione diligunt te. Recti merito appellantur, qui post confessionem et poenitentiam ad peccatum non revertuntur. Possumus sane istam sententiam referre ad Christi personam, ita ut dicat Christus: Introduxit me rex in cellaria sua, quod est dicere: Pater meus, qui est Rex regum, praecepit mihi ut carnem sumerem intra viscera beatae Mariae semper 549Dvirginis. Quae viscera sacrosanctae Virginis merito appellantur cellaria Dei Patris, quia ab ipso ab aeterno sunt praedestinata, et ab eodem in fine saeculorum nativitati Filii sui praeparata et consecrata. Quam nativitatem adolescentulae cognoscentes, id est angelicae et coelestes virtutes, gloriam Deo canunt in excelsis et in terra pacem annuntiant hominibus bonae voluntatis (Luc. II), quasi dicant ad Patrem: Merito exsultabimus et laetabimur in te, quia numerum nostrum qui minoratus fuerat diabolo cadente, per incarnationem Filii tui disponis reparare. Memores uberum tuorum super vinum, ac si dicat: Recordamur misericordiae tuae et pietatis tuae admirabilis, quibus modo tu temperas austeram sententiam 550Ajuste damnationis, quam primo homini olim intulisti, quando illum iniquitate sua exigente a paradisi gaudiis exclusisti, et in hanc convallem lacrymarum et miseriarum expulisti. Consideratis igitur istis beneficiis a te sibi clementer praerogatis, recti diligunt te, quia nec in coelo, nec in terra inveniunt quidquam charius vel dignius te. Tu enim totum mundum, cum non esset, de nihilo creasti, hominemque de limo terrae admirabili potentia plasmasti, eumdemque quem diabolus sua fraude seduxit et perdidit, tua pietas benignissima ad paradisi gaudia reduxit et salvavit; et ideo recti diligunt te, quia aliquid quod melius et justius ament, non reperiunt quam te. Introducamus ergo et nos, fratres, Christum in cellaria nostra. Cellaria vero nostra sunt corpus 550Bet anima nostra, in quibus intraturum se Dominus repromittit, si exclusis omnibus malis ea sibi parata et munda invenerit sicut ipse ait: « Ecce ego sto ad ostium et pulso, si quis aperuerit mihi januam, introibo ad illum, et coenabo cum illo, et ipse mecum (Apoc. III). » Sed quid putamus, quid gaudii, quid jubilationis, quid laetitiae vel exsultationis, adolescentulae, id est omnes virtutes animae nostrae, tunc possidebunt, quando Christum in cellaria sua adesse videbunt? Tunc praeterea dicet Sponsus adolescentulis istis: « Gaudium vestrum nemo tollet a vobis (Joan. XVI) »

HERE BEGINS THE FIFTH TOME Upon that which is said: The king hath brought me into his storerooms, we will be glad and rejoice in thee, mindful of thy breasts: above wine the upright love thee (Cant. I).

547B319 As the sacred history of the Old Testament, when it was read, intimated to the minds of the hearers with spiritual modulation, Isaac the holy patriarch, son of that most noble and excellent patriarch Abraham, had once, when the day was already drawing on toward evening, gone out to meditate in the field (Gen. XXIV): and it came to pass, while he walked meditating, that he lifted up his eyes and saw Rebecca his bride coming, and ran to meet her, and led her into the tent of Sara his mother, and loved her so greatly that he even tempered, for the greatest part, the grief which had befallen him from the death of that same mother of his. For because Isaac is interpreted laughter or joy, not undeservedly do we spiritually understand by him our Lord Jesus Christ, who is the joy and gladness, the jubilation and exultation, and the unfailing delight 547Cof his saints. But that the Lord Jesus himself is the son of Abraham according to the flesh, Matthew the evangelist attests when he says: « The book of the generation of Jesus Christ, the son of David, the son of Abraham (Matth. I). » But that saint Isaac, the day now drawing on toward evening, had gone out to meditate in the field, signifies that the Lord Jesus Christ, as the end of this world drew near, came into this world for this, that he might fulfil the mystery of his most holy incarnation. For the walking of holy Isaac into the field insinuates to us the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ into this world. Whence also his most dear Bride says to the same Lord Jesus in the Song of Songs: « Come, my beloved, let us abide in the woods, let us go forth into the field (Cant. VII), » although the blessed 547DAmbrose interprets that field as a stronghold. [Vulg. Cant. 7:11 egrediamur in agrum, commoremur in villis, "let us go forth into the field, let us abide in the villages" — and in that order] Moreover, that Isaac brought Rebecca into his mother's tent, and for love of her tempered the grief of his mother's death, signifies that the Lord Jesus Christ joined holy Church to himself by spiritual nuptials and epithalamic Songs. Whom assuredly he so loved with spiritual love, that he even 548Bhanded over the Synagogue, namely his carnal mother, to oblivion. Lastly, Rebecca herself is interpreted patience, whom Isaac brought into his tent, which surely alludes elegantly to the mysteries of holy Church; whom our Lord Jesus Christ, the contest of this world being finished, shall bring in to the eternal mansions of heavenly glory — to the founders of which Church, namely the holy apostles, he himself says: « In your patience you shall possess your souls (Luc. XXI). » Considering therefore in this little book this most blessed and most happy bringing-in, and as though that were already done which is still to come after the day of judgment, the Bride of Christ, exulting with unspeakable joy and incomprehensible jubilation, thus addresses the maidens, saying: The king hath brought me into his storerooms 548C(Cant. I). Truly, who is this king by whom this Bride says she has been brought into the storerooms? He assuredly of whom the prophet David says: « O God, give thy judgment to the king, and thy justice to the king's son (Psal. LXXI). » Which indeed the same king, son of the highest king, makes plainer in the Gospel when he says: « The Father judgeth no man, but hath given all judgment to the Son (Joan. V). » But what are these storerooms, if not those tabernacles or courts of which David the prophet sang: « How lovely are thy tabernacles, O Lord of hosts, my soul coveteth and desireth to be in the courts of the Lord? » (Psal. LXXXIII.) [Vulg. Ps. 83:3 concupiscit et deficit anima mea in atria Domini, "my soul coveteth and fainteth for the courts of the Lord"] These tabernacles indeed, or courts, the Lord Jesus in the Gospel calls mansions, where he says: « In my Father's house there are many mansions (Joan. XIV). » Holy Church therefore, 548Dthe Bride of Christ, considering spiritually those mansions or tabernacles or courts, and panting after them with the whole desire of her mind, the love of the world set behind her, as though she already possessed in present those things which she doubts not she shall possess when the day of judgment is done, says: The king hath brought me into his storerooms. Truly, says holy Church, the king hath brought me into his storerooms, because « what 549Aeye hath not seen, nor ear heard, neither hath it ascended into the heart of man (I Cor. II), » as he had promised me, he has faithfully bestowed. Whence Isaias the prophet says: « The eye hath not seen, O God, besides thee, what things thou hast prepared for them that love thee (Isa. LXIV). » [Vulg. Isa. 64:4 exspectantibus te, "for them that wait for thee"] By the charity therefore of this unspeakable joy, by the affection of incomprehensible jubilation, by the contemplation of the highest and unutterable Trinity, the maidens, that is, the faithful souls, being kindled, say: We will be glad and rejoice in thee. For then, when the sacrament of the resurrection and of the general judgment is completed, when the Son of God shall say to his faithful: « Come, ye blessed of my Father, receive the kingdom which was prepared for you from the origin of the world (Matth. XXV), » [Vulg. Matth. 25:34 possidete paratum vobis regnum a constitutione mundi, "possess the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world"] and when the body which is corrupted shall no longer weigh down the soul, nor the earthly habitation press down 549Bthe sense that thinketh many things, but the soul, seeing God as he is, face to face (I Cor. XIII), by participation of the vision of the highest and incomprehensible Trinity, shall make its flesh wholly spiritual; then, I say, 320 shall these maidens, that is, the faithful souls, more truly say, we will be glad and rejoice in thee. For to us dwelling on earth and faithfully obeying thy precepts thou hast promised great things indeed, but in heaven thou hast rendered greater. Therefore we will be glad and rejoice in thee. Mindful of thy breasts, that is, of thy precepts, by the keeping of which and by the grace of the Holy Spirit we come to the contemplating of the brightness of thy majesty; or else we will be glad and rejoice in thee, mindful of thy breasts above wine, that is, of thy eloquences, which eloquences have been made sweet and honey-flowing 549Cto us above wine, that is, more than the doctrine of the Mosaic law, which is deservedly compared to wine for the austerity of its precepts: which wine is said to kill little ones, because, as Paul says, « The law worketh wrath (that is, death) (Rom. IV). » Therefore, King Christ, because thou hast conferred so many benefits on thy Bride, therefore the sons of that same Bride, that is, the upright love thee. Upright in thought, upright in speech, upright in work, they love thee. They are deservedly called upright, who after confession and penitence return not to sin. We may indeed refer that sentence to the person of Christ, so that Christ says: The king hath brought me into his storerooms, which is to say: My Father, who is the King of kings, commanded me to take flesh within the bowels of the blessed Mary, ever 549Da virgin. Which bowels of the most holy Virgin are deservedly called the storerooms of God the Father, because by him they were predestined from eternity, and by the same prepared and consecrated in the end of the ages for the nativity of his Son. Which nativity the maidens knowing, that is, the angelic and heavenly powers, sing glory to God in the highest and announce peace on earth to men of good will (Luc. II), as if they said to the Father: Deservedly we will be glad and rejoice in thee, because thou dost dispose to repair, through the incarnation of thy Son, our number which had been diminished by the devil's fall. Mindful of thy breasts above wine, as if it said: We remember thy mercy and thy admirable tenderness, whereby thou now temperest the austere sentence 550Aof just damnation, which thou didst once bring upon the first man, when, his iniquity requiring it, thou didst shut him out from the joys of paradise and drive him forth into this valley of tears and of miseries. Considering therefore these benefits mercifully bestowed upon her by thee, the upright love thee, because neither in heaven nor on earth do they find anything dearer or worthier than thee. For thou didst create the whole world out of nothing when it was not, and didst fashion man of the slime of the earth with admirable power, and the same man whom the devil by his fraud seduced and destroyed, thy most kindly tenderness led back to the joys of paradise and saved; and therefore the upright love thee, because they find nothing which they may love better and more justly than thee. Let us also therefore, brethren, bring Christ into our storerooms. Now our storerooms are our body 550Band our soul, into which the Lord promises that he will enter, if, all evils being shut out, he shall find them prepared and clean for him, as he himself says: « Behold, I stand at the door and knock; if any man shall open to me the gate, I will come in to him, and will sup with him, and he with me (Apoc. III). » But what do we think — what of joy, what of jubilation, what of gladness or of exultation — will the maidens, that is, all the virtues of our soul, then possess, when they shall see Christ present in his storerooms? Then moreover shall the Bridegroom say to these maidens: « Your joy no man shall take from you (Joan. XVI) »

RECAPITULATIO A SUPERIORIBUS SECUNDUM MYSTICUM ET ALLEGORICUM SENSUM IN ILLA SENTENTIA: « INTRODUXIT ME REX IN CELLARIA SUA. »

550CHomines pigmentarii, qui redolentia aromata ex diversis speciebus conficiunt, et qui balsama vel caetera hujusmodi aromatizantia saepius contingunt: ipsi quidem prius quasi pro munere sui laboris eorumdem aromatum sive balsami miro odore potiuntur, deinde supervenientes et propius accedentes gratissima fragrantia perfunduntur et perfruuntur. Porro hoc certissime constat, quia quanto amplius supradicti pigmentarii aromata sua comminuunt, vel minutatim conterunt seu balsama effundunt, et de vase in vas transponunt, tanto procul dubio suis naribus inferunt recentiorem et eminus assistentibus propinant gratiorem odorem. Quae sunt igitur ista aromata sive balsama pretiosa, nisi divinorum librorum 550Dsacratissima eloquia? Unde Sponsa in Canticis canticorum dicit: « Dilectus meus descendit ad areolam aromatum ut lilia colligat (Cant. VI). » Quaenam est ergo areola aromatum, nisi Ecclesia catholica, in qua continetur ingens copia divinorum librorum? Pigmentarios vero nullos alios arbitror rectius posse intelligi, quam rectores et praedicatores Ecclesiae Dei, qui more aromatum Scripturas divinitus inspiratas exponendo conterunt et per minutias dividunt, et sicut balsama cum de vase in vas transfunduntur, suavissimum spirant odorem: sic ipsi rationabiles et gratos sensus proferunt, dum de historia ad allegoriam, et de allegoria transeunt ad moralitatem. 321 Si igitur, sicut diximus, aromata sunt sacra eloquia, et si eo amplius redolent quanto amplius 551Afuerint contrita, videamus quid redoleat vel quid fragrantiae in se contineat ista sententia. Introduxit me rex in cellaria sua. Cellaria siquidem secundum allegoriam istius regis sunt omnes libri generaliter divinae legis, in quibus tunc spiritualiter et mystice a Domino introducimur, cum ad intelligendos occultos et mysticos sensus ab ipso instruimur et docemur. Sic denique beati illi discipuli a Domino Jesu infra cellaria ista introducebantur de quibus in Evangelio dicitur: « Tunc aperuit illis sensum ut intelligerent Scripturas (Luc. XXIV). » Cellarium nempe quoddam secretum, et omnibus divinis charismatibus et divitiis spiritualibus affluens est Evangelium. Unde Sponsa proclamat exsultans in Canticis canticorum: « Introduxit me rex in cellam 551Bvinariam, et ordinavit in me charitatem (Cant. II). » Evangelium quippe docendo declarat quomodo charitas inter Christianos haberi et possideri debeat, ut scilicet principaliter prae omnibus creaturis, et super omnes creaturas, et ante omnia diligatur Deus; deinde secundo loco, sincero charitatis affectu ametur proximus. Haec est namque beatissima et sacratissima purae charitatis ordinatio, quam reperire potest unusquisque fidelis in Evangelii Christi cellario. Sunt praeterea et alia cellaria istius magni et ditissimi regis: liber scilicet Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, liber Numeri liberque Deuteronomii, Isaias, Jeremias, Ezechiel, Daniel, et duodecim prophetae aliique perplures divinae legis libri, cellaria sunt Christi; sed quid dicam de praecipuo et 551Ceximio prophetarum David, qui cujus cellarium sit in prima parte sui libri luce clarius manifestat cum dicit: « Beatus vir qui non abiit in consilio impiorum, et in via peccatorum non stetit, et in cathedra pestilentiae non sedit? » (Psal. I.) Non itaque arbitramur modo tempus esse, quo per omnes libros sigillatim discurramus, et qualiter unusquisque liber cellarium sit istius summi regis exponamus, quia ad id peragendum nequaquam obtinemus sensus idoneos, et hoc plenius peregisse cognoscimus antiquitus sanctos Patres nostros. Verumtamen ne quis sub silentio lenti corporis velle latere nos autumet, ingrediamur illud cellarium de quo praefati sumus superius, et quid in se absconditum contineat 551Dquidve redoleat, ipso largiente cujus est cellarium, in palam proferamus. « Beatus vir qui non abiit in consilio impiorum, etc. » In divina pagina, o spiritualis legis perscrutator, tria subjacent inquisitioni tuae, scilicet historia, allegoria, et moralitas, quae semper obambulat et inseparabiliter adhaeret nostrae conditioni. Sane in rerum natura sunt ista tria, et non facile reperiuntur, nisi a prudenti lectore sollicitius requirantur. Historica igitur intelligentia hujus versus, scilicet, « Beatus vir, » referatur ad personam terreni regis David, allegoria Domino Jesu Christo ascribatur, moralitas nostris actibus corrigendis assignetur. Sed quomodo possumus ad David historice istum versum videlicet, « Beatus vir, » etc., referre, quem cognoscimus cogitatione, locutione et 552Aoperatione peccasse? Certum est enim quia David cogitando peccavit, quando de stratu suo incaute respiciens Bethsabee uxorem Uriae Ethei vidit lavantem et concupivit. Verum quia cogitationem illicitam in se minime repressit, usque ad locutionis reatum descendit, cum per internuntios quaenam esset illa mulier inquisivit. Et quis nesciat illum miserabili lapsu in opere corruisse, quando cum eadem Bethsabee adulterium perpetravit (II Reg. II), et postea virum ejus ne adulterium omnibus pateret gladiis filiorum Ammon occidendum tradidit? Qua ergo beatitudine David donabitur, qui in cogitatione, locutione et opere reus esse comprobatur? Et quis audebit David beatum virum dicere, qui adulterium tam leviter commisit, et eidem adulterio, 552Bquod pejus est, homicidium copulavit? Sed secundum apostoli Pauli (I Cor. VI) vocem ablutus est, justificatus est, sanctificatus est in nomine Domini nostri et in Spiritu Dei nostri. Justificatus est, inquam, per confessionem quando ait ad Nathan prophetam: « Peccavi Domino, ora pro me; » et Nathan propheta ad eum: « Noli, inquit, timere, nequaquam morte morieris, quia Dominus transtulit peccatum tuum a te (II Reg. XII). Ecce quam velociter iste homo per confessionem ablutus est, justificatus est, sanctificatus est. Praeterea audiamus quid Dominus de eo: « Inveni, inquit, David servum meum filium Jesse, qui facit omnes voluntates meas (Act. XIII). » Et in alio loco: « Inveni David servum meum, oleo sancto meo unxi eum (Psal. LXXXVIII). » 552CMea, inquit, manus auxiliabitur ei (ibid.) » , et caetera quae sequuntur in eodem psalmo, quae ad laudem ipsius David dicta manifesta comprobat oratio. Quis igitur istum condemnare audeat quem Deus verbis hujuscemodi abluit, justificat et sanctificat? Sed certe quaeramus si forte nobis occurrerit, quod alicubi David in consilio impiorum non abierit, et in via peccatorum non steterit, et in cathedra iniquitatis non sederit. Neque enim decet Dei majestatem, ut impium justificet, sicut nec hoc ut justum condemnet. Nequaquam vero Deus justum David justificaret, vel laudaret, nisi aliquod bonum opus in eo inveniretur quod ipsi Deo placeret. Nota est siquidem historia libri Regum, quae refert quod aliquando Saul, impiissimus rex, David injuste persequeretur, 552Det accidit quadam die ut idem 322 Saul quamdam speluncam ad requisita naturae ingrederetur, et ecce in eadem spelunca David cum sociis suis a facie ipsius Saul fugiens, latitabat. Dixerunt ergo socii ejus ad eum: « Ecce dies de qua locutus est Dominus ad te, dicens: Ego tradam tibi inimicum tuum, ut facias in eum quidquid volueris. Surge igitur et interfice eum. Et confregit David verba virorum qui cum eo erant, et non permisit ut interficerent eum, sed dixit: Vivit Dominus, quia nisi Dominus percusserit eum, aut dies ejus venerit ut moriatur, aut descendens in praelio perierit, non mittam manum meam in christum Domini (I Reg. XXIV). » Ecce quomodo David non abiit in consilio impiorum. 553AQuamvis enim lex diceret: « Diliges amicum tuum, et odio habebis inimicum tuum (Levit. XIX); » tamen iste despiciens antiquae legis instituta, jam transitum faciebat ad novae legis sacramenta. In nova enim lege dicitur: « Diligite inimicos vestros, benefacite his qui oderunt vos (Matth. V). » Postquam autem, Deo adjuvante, ostendimus quomodo David in consilio impiorum non abiit, restat ut ipso opitulante demonstremus qualiter in via peccatorum non steterit. In eodem namque libro Regum similiter reperitur, quoniam quidem alia vice idem rex flagitiosissimus Saul sanctum David invidia stimulante persequebatur. Et factum est dum sol ad occasum tenderet, et vespera superveniens diei terminum jam imponere vellet, Saul in loco in quo erat expansis 553Btentoriis et papilionibus suis requiescere disponebat. Cumque omnium membra quietus sopor obtineret, David notato loco in quo Saul dormiebat ait ad socios suos: « Quis descendet mecum ad Saul in castra? » (I Reg. XXVI.) Et descenderunt cum eo nonnulli ex sociis illius. Cum autem venisset ad locum in quo Saul dormiebat, dixit unus quispiam ex his qui cum eo erant: « Ecce dedit Dominus inimicum tuum in manu tua. Nunc ergo dimitte me, et perfodiam eum lancea semel in terra, et secundo opus non erit. Et ait David: Non facias ei quidquam mali, nec extendas manum tuam in eum. Quis enim mittens manum suam in christum Domini innocens erit? » (Ibid.) Hoc sciat Dominus quia nisi ipse Dominus percusserit eum, aut dies ejus venerit 553Cut moriatur, aut descendens in praelio perierit, « non mittam manum meam in christum Domini (ibid.). » Ecce quomodo David in via peccatorum non stetit, quia consilium eorum qui ei homicidium persuadebant audire recusavit. Ostensis igitur quibusdam argumentis ex operibus sancti David, quibus illum manifesta auctoritate comprobavimus aliquando, nec in consilio impiorum abiisse, nec in via peccatorum stetisse; incumbit nobis adhuc vigilanti animo illum ostendere in cathedra pestilentiae non sedisse. Postquam autem Saul vitam istam temporalem cum filiis Israel sub sorte praelii in monte Gelboae finivit (I Reg. XXXI), scriptum est quia domus Juda in Ebron David sibi in regem unxerit, et eum more regio in cathedra regali collocaverit. 553DEcce, inquit historia, venerunt quidam, et annuntiaverunt David quod viri Jabes Galaath honorifice sepelissent Saul inimicum ejus, et mandavit eis, dicens: « Benedicti vos a Domino, qui fecistis hanc misericordiam cum Saule domino vestro. Sed et ego reddam vobis gratiam pro opere quod fecistis in eo (II Reg. II). » Praeterea in sequentibus libri Regum dicitur, quia tulit David ossa Saul et Jonathae, et sepelivit ea valde honorabiliter in terra Benjamin (II Reg. XXI). Sane quibus verbis possimus digne explicare quomodo David fleverit audita morte ejusdem inimici sui Saulis de cujus utique interitu debuisses gaudere exsultantibus animis sicut scriptum est: « Planxit David planctum hujuscemodi 554Asuper Saul, et super Jonathan filium ejus: Filiae Israel, super Saul flete, qui praebebat ornamenta aurea cultui vestro, et vestiebat vos in deliciis. O Saul et Jonatha, quam amabiles et decori fuistis in vita vestra! aquilis eratis velociores, et leonibus fortiores. Montes Gelboe, nec ros nec pluvia veniant super vos, nec sint agri primitiarum in vobis, quia ibi abjectus est clypeus fortium, clypeus Saul quasi non esset unctus oleo. Sagitta Jonathae nunquam abiit retrorsum, et gladius Saul non est reversus inanis. O quomodo ceciderunt robusti et nobiles et egregii viri, et perierunt arma bellica! » (II Reg. I.) Sed et hoc quod David illum interfici jussit qui annuntiabat ei, dicens: « Ego interfeci Saul in montem Gelboe, » valde congruit 554Boperibus pietatis et misericordiae. Ecce sicut diximus, David in cathedra pestilentiae et iniquitatis non sedit, qui pro morte inimici sui non solum planctus lamentabiles effudit, sed etiam alios ad hoc ut secum plangerent, piissimis verbis incitavit. Superius igitur praefati sumus, quod historia istius versus, scilicet « Beatus vir, » debeat referri ad David, allegoria ad Christum, moralitas ad unumquemque nostrum. Verumtamen facile allegoria istius versus potest Domino Jesu cooptari, quoniam quidem qui peccatum non fecit, ipse quidem in consilio impiorum non abiit, et in via peccatorum non stetit, et in cathedra pestilentiae et iniquitatis nullatenus resedit. Consideremus ergo subtili indagatione qualiter ad unumquemque nostrum moralitas hujus versus 554Cpertineat, et quomodo in interiori actione nos undique circumveniat. Tria quippe genera lapsuum humanae fragilitatis, o tu amator vel auditor divini verbi, in hoc versu, videlicet « Beatus vir, » te oportet 323 considerare et diligenti intuitu quibus modis peccato subjaceat nostra natura perpendite. Sicut enim in hoc versu ista tria, videlicet deambulatio, statio, sessio denotantur, sic omne peccatum cui obnoxii sumus tribus modis a nobis perpetratur. Sed certe qui sunt isti tres modi plus experimento quotidiano, quod sine gravi moerore dicere non possumus, quam alicujus sententiae tractatu indicante cognoscimus. Cogitatione quippe, aut locutione, seu operatione difficile est, ut non labatur humana conditio. Quasi enim in consilio impiorum deambulare 554Dvidetur, quando vanis cogitationibus malignorum spirituum mens nostra dedita huc atque illuc evagatur; stat namque in via peccatorum, quando illa quae Deo sunt contraria loquitur. Sedet vero in cathedra pestilentiae, quando retrorsum abjecto Dei timore male operatur.

RECAPITULATION FROM THE FOREGOING ACCORDING TO THE MYSTICAL AND ALLEGORICAL SENSE UPON THAT SENTENCE: « THE KING HATH BROUGHT ME INTO HIS STOREROOMS. »

550CThe men who are perfumers, who make up fragrant spices out of divers kinds, and who rather often handle balsams or the other things of that sort that give out aroma: they indeed first enjoy, as it were for the wage of their labour, the wondrous odour of those same spices or of the balsam; then they that come after and draw nearer are steeped in the most pleasing fragrance and enjoy it. Further, this stands most certainly, that the more the aforesaid perfumers break their spices small, or grind them minutely, or pour out the balsams and shift them from vessel to vessel, so much the more beyond doubt do they bring a fresher odour to their own nostrils, and offer a more pleasing one to them that stand at a distance. What then are these spices or precious balsams, if not the 550Dmost sacred eloquences of the divine books? Whence the Bride in the Song of Songs says: « My beloved is gone down to the little bed of spices, that he may gather the lilies (Cant. VI). » What then is the little bed of spices, if not the catholic Church, in which is contained a mighty abundance of the divine books? But the perfumers I judge can be understood as none other, more rightly, than the rulers and preachers of the Church of God, who after the manner of spices grind and divide into small parts, by expounding, the Scriptures divinely inspired, and, as balsams when they are poured from vessel to vessel breathe out a most sweet odour, so these bring forth reasonable and pleasing senses, while they pass from history to allegory, and from allegory to morality. 321 If therefore, as we have said, the spices are the sacred eloquences, and if they give out so much the more odour the more 551Athey shall have been ground, let us see what odour this sentence gives out, or what of fragrance it contains in itself. The king hath brought me into his storerooms. Indeed the storerooms of this king, according to allegory, are all the books generally of the divine law, into which we are then spiritually and mystically brought in by the Lord, when we are instructed and taught by him to understand the hidden and mystical senses. Thus finally were those blessed disciples brought in by the Lord Jesus within these storerooms, of whom it is said in the Gospel: « Then he opened their understanding, that they might understand the Scriptures (Luc. XXIV). » A certain secret storeroom indeed, abounding in all divine charisms and spiritual riches, is the Gospel. Whence the Bride cries out exulting in the Song of Songs: « The king hath brought me into the wine 551Bcellar, and hath ordered charity in me (Cant. II). » For the Gospel by teaching declares how charity ought to be had and possessed among Christians: namely that principally, before all creatures, and above all creatures, and before all things, God be loved; then in the second place, that the neighbour be loved with the sincere affection of charity.
