For this is the most blessed and most holy ordering of pure charity, which every one of the faithful can find in the storeroom of Christ's Gospel. And there are besides other storerooms of this great and most rich king: namely the book of Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, the book of Numbers and the book of Deuteronomy, Isaias, Jeremias, Ezechiel, Daniel, and the twelve prophets, and very many other books of the divine law — these are the storerooms of Christ; but what shall I say of the chief and 551Cexcellent one among the prophets, David, who makes clearer than light, in the first part of his book, whose storeroom it is, when he says: « Blessed is the man who hath not gone in the counsel of the ungodly, and hath not stood in the way of sinners, and hath not sat in the chair of pestilence? » (Psal. I.) We do not therefore judge it now to be the time in which we should run through all the books one by one, and expound how each several book is a storeroom of this highest king, since for the accomplishing of that we by no means possess fit senses, and we know that our holy Fathers of old accomplished this more fully. Nevertheless, lest anyone suppose that we wish to lie hid under the silence of a sluggish body, let us enter that storeroom of which we spoke above, and let us bring forth into the open what it contains hidden in itself 551Dor what it gives out for odour, by the bounty of him whose the storeroom is. « Blessed is the man who hath not gone in the counsel of the ungodly, etc. » In the divine page, O searcher of the spiritual law, three things lie subject to thy inquiry, namely history, allegory, and morality, which last always walks about and inseparably cleaves to our condition. Truly in the nature of things these three are, and are not easily found, unless they be sought out rather carefully by a prudent reader. Let therefore the historical understanding of this verse, namely, « Blessed is the man, » be referred to the person of the earthly king David; let the allegory be ascribed to the Lord Jesus Christ; let the morality be assigned to the correcting of our own acts. But how can we refer historically to David this verse, namely, « Blessed is the man, » etc., whom we know to have sinned in thought, in speech and in 552Awork? For it is certain that David sinned by thinking, when, looking incautiously from his couch, he saw Bethsabee the wife of Urias the Hethite washing, and coveted her. But because he in no wise repressed within himself the unlawful thought, he came down as far as the guilt of speech, when through go-betweens he inquired who that woman might be. And who knows not that he fell in work by a wretched lapse, when he committed adultery with the same Bethsabee (II Reg. II), and afterwards, lest the adultery should lie open to all, delivered her husband to be slain by the swords of the sons of Ammon? With what blessedness then shall David be endowed, who is proved to be guilty in thought, in speech and in work? And who will dare to call David a blessed man, who committed adultery so lightly, and to the same adultery, 552Bwhich is worse, joined homicide? But according to the word of the apostle Paul (I Cor. VI) he was washed, he was justified, he was sanctified in the name of our Lord and in the Spirit of our God. He was justified, I say, by confession, when he said to Nathan the prophet: « I have sinned against the Lord, pray for me; » and Nathan the prophet to him: « Fear not, said he, thou shalt by no means die the death, because the Lord hath taken away thy sin from thee (II Reg. XII). Behold how swiftly this man was washed by confession, was justified, was sanctified. Moreover let us hear what the Lord says of him: « I have found, said he, David my servant, the son of Jesse, who doeth all my wills (Act. XIII). » [Vulg. Act. 13:22 inveni David filium Jesse virum secundum cor meum qui faciet omnes voluntates meas, "I have found David the son of Jesse, a man according to my own heart, who shall do all my wills"] And in another place: « I have found David my servant, with my holy oil I have anointed him (Psal. LXXXVIII). » 552CMy hand, says he, shall help him (ibid.) » , and the rest which follows in the same psalm, which the plain wording proves to have been said to the praise of that same David. Who then would dare to condemn him whom God with words of this sort washes, justifies and sanctifies? But surely let us seek whether it may perchance occur to us that somewhere David did not go in the counsel of the ungodly, and did not stand in the way of sinners, and did not sit in the chair of iniquity. For neither does it befit the majesty of God to justify the ungodly, as neither does this, to condemn the just. By no means indeed would God justify or praise the just David, unless some good work were found in him which might please God himself. Known indeed is the history of the book of Kings, which relates that at a certain time Saul, that most impious king, was unjustly persecuting David, 552Dand it fell out on a certain day that the same 322 Saul entered a certain cave for the requirements of nature, and behold, in that same cave David with his companions, fleeing from the face of that same Saul, lay hid. His companions therefore said to him: « Behold the day of which the Lord spoke to thee, saying: I will deliver thy enemy to thee, that thou mayest do to him whatsoever thou wilt. Rise up therefore and kill him. And David broke the words of the men who were with him, and did not permit that they should kill him, but said: The Lord liveth, that unless the Lord shall strike him, or his day shall come that he die, or going down into battle he shall perish, I will not put forth my hand upon the anointed of the Lord (I Reg. XXIV). » Behold how David went not in the counsel of the ungodly. 553AFor although the law said: « Thou shalt love thy friend, and thou shalt hold thy enemy in hatred (Levit. XIX); » yet he, despising the institutes of the ancient law, was already making his passage to the sacraments of the new law. For in the new law it is said: « Love your enemies, do good to them that hate you (Matth. V). » [Vulg. Levit. 19:18 diliges amicum tuum sicut temet ipsum, "thou shalt love thy friend as thyself"; the clause et odio habebis inimicum tuum stands at Matth. 5:43, as the saying of them of old] But after we have shown, God helping, how David went not in the counsel of the ungodly, it remains that, by his aid, we demonstrate in what manner he stood not in the way of sinners. For in the same book of Kings it is likewise found that on another occasion that same most shameful king Saul, at the goading of envy, was persecuting holy David. And it came to pass, while the sun drew toward its setting, and the evening coming on was now willing to set a term to the day, that Saul in the place where he was, his 553Btents and pavilions being spread, was disposing himself to rest. And when quiet slumber held the limbs of all, David, having marked the place in which Saul was sleeping, said to his companions: « Who will go down with me to Saul into the camp? » (I Reg. XXVI.) And there went down with him some of his companions. But when he had come to the place in which Saul was sleeping, one of those who were with him said: « Behold, the Lord hath given thy enemy into thy hand. Now therefore let me go, and I will pierce him with the spear once into the earth, and there will be no need of a second time. And David said: Do him no evil at all, nor stretch out thy hand upon him. For who, putting forth his hand upon the anointed of the Lord, shall be innocent? » (Ibid.) Let the Lord know this, that unless the Lord himself shall strike him, or his day shall come 553Cthat he die, or going down into battle he shall perish, « I will not put forth my hand upon the anointed of the Lord (ibid.). » Behold how David stood not in the way of sinners, because he refused to hear the counsel of those who were persuading him to homicide. Certain arguments therefore having been shown from the works of holy David, whereby we have proved him by manifest authority to have at one time neither gone in the counsel of the ungodly nor stood in the way of sinners, it lies upon us still with watchful mind to show that he sat not in the chair of pestilence. Now after Saul ended this temporal life with the sons of Israel under the lot of battle on Mount Gelboe (I Reg. XXXI), it is written that the house of Juda in Hebron anointed David to itself as king, and set him after the royal manner on the royal chair. 553DBehold, says the history, certain men came and announced to David that the men of Jabes Galaad had honourably buried Saul his enemy, and he sent to them, saying: « Blessed be ye of the Lord, who have done this mercy with Saul your lord. But I also will render you thanks for the work which ye have done in him (II Reg. II). » Moreover in what follows of the book of Kings it is said that David took the bones of Saul and of Jonathas, and buried them very honourably in the land of Benjamin (II Reg. XXI). Truly, with what words could we worthily set forth how David wept when he heard of the death of that same enemy of his Saul, at whose destruction surely thou oughtest to have rejoiced with exulting spirits, as it is written: « David made a lamentation of this sort 554Aover Saul, and over Jonathas his son: Ye daughters of Israel, weep over Saul, who furnished ornaments of gold for your attire, and clothed you in delights. O Saul and Jonathas, how lovely and comely were ye in your life! ye were swifter than eagles, and stronger than lions. Ye mountains of Gelboe, let neither dew nor rain come upon you, nor let there be fields of firstfruits in you, because there was cast away the shield of the mighty, the shield of Saul, as though he had not been anointed with oil. The arrow of Jonathas never went back, and the sword of Saul returned not empty. O how are the strong and the noble and the excellent men fallen, and the weapons of war perished! » (II Reg. I.) And this also, that David commanded him to be slain who announced to him, saying: « I have slain Saul on Mount Gelboe, » agrees exceedingly 554Bwith the works of tenderness and mercy. Behold, as we have said, David sat not in the chair of pestilence and of iniquity, who for the death of his enemy not only poured out lamentable wailings, but even stirred up others with most loving words to lament with him. Above therefore we premised that the history of that verse, namely « Blessed is the man, » ought to be referred to David, the allegory to Christ, the morality to each one of us. Nevertheless the allegory of that verse can easily be fitted to the Lord Jesus, since indeed he who did no sin, he assuredly went not in the counsel of the ungodly, and stood not in the way of sinners, and in no wise sat down in the chair of pestilence and of iniquity. Let us consider then with subtle investigation how the morality of this verse 554Cpertains to each one of us, and how in our inward action it besets us on every side. Three kinds indeed of the lapses of human frailty, O thou lover or hearer of the divine word, it behoves thee to consider in this verse, namely « Blessed is the man, » and do ye weigh 323 with diligent regard by what ways our nature lies subject to sin. For as in this verse these three, namely walking, standing, sitting, are denoted, so every sin to which we are liable is perpetrated by us in three manners. But surely what these three manners are we know rather by daily experience, which we cannot speak of without heavy sorrow, than by the discourse of any sentence pointing it out. For in thought, or in speech, or in work, it is hard that the human condition should not slip. For it seems as it were to walk in the counsel of the ungodly 554Dwhen our mind, given over to the vain thoughts of malign spirits, strays hither and thither; and it stands in the way of sinners when it speaks those things which are contrary to God. But it sits in the chair of pestilence when, the fear of God being cast away backward, it works evil.

RECAPITULATIO SECUNDUM ANAGOGEN ET MORALITATEM IN ILLA SENTENTIA: « INTRODUXIT ME REX IN CELLARIA SUA. »

Omnes artes humanae scientiae inter docendum, id est quando docentur vel exercentur, praebent auditoribus suis extra seipsas quaedam delectabilia refugia, ut verbi gratia sunt politores, vel lignorum 555Acaesores, seu hi qui in lapidiciniis versantur, aut aurifices vel structores, seu etiam cujuslibet alterius artis, quae ad humanam pertinere sententiam dignoscitur. Isti enim omnes habent infra operationem suam quasdam vanas consolationes. Dum vero politores marmora poliunt, loquuntur, fabulantur, rident, jucundantur; aliquando etiam quaedam quae ad eamdem artem nihil prorsus pertinere comprobantur. Nostra autem ars, videlicet spiritualium litterarum, in discendo seu docendo totum vult hominem habere sibi intentum. Aut enim homo totum seipsum nostrae arti intentum praebebit, aut ejusdem artis peritiam nullatenus ad liquidum obtinebit. Quis enim unquam dimidiato corde, vel sensibus aversis seu vaga et instabili mente bonus clericus 555Besse potuit? Necesse est ergo ut, non solum ea quae hujus mundi sunt, sed etiam seipsum maxima ex parte, si dici fas est, tradat oblivioni, qui perfecte adipisci praeoptat illa quae in divinis libris humanae subjacent intentioni. Sopitis ergo terrenis voluptatibus, contemptis pravis operibus, quae solent incautos homines persaepe dimergere in voraginis profundum, accedamus non dimidio corde, sed mente integra, affectione sincera ad Sponsum, et deprecemur illum humilibus verbis cum charissima ejus Sponsa quatenus dignetur nos sibi devotos introducere in sua cellaria. Superius ergo me fateor dixisse quoniam quidem cellaria istius Sponsi et regis essent allegorice omnes libri divinae legis. Nunc autem similiter dico quod ejusdem Sponsi et regis moraliter 555Csunt cellaria omnes virtutes quae reperiri queunt ubique sparsim per sacra eloquia. Verumtamen quia sermonem de virtutibus ordiri desideramus, consequens est ut ad caput seu principium omnium virtutum stylum nostrum dirigamus. Caput nempe seu principium de quo omnes virtutes egrediuntur, charitas sine dubio esse dignoscitur. Unde beatus Gregorius dicit: « Sicut ex uno arboris stipite multi rami egrediuntur, sic ex una charitate multae virtutes generantur, nec habet quidquam viriditatis ramus boni operis si non manserit in radice charitatis. » Ipsa ergo charitas ipsa sit exordium nostrae narrationis, quia ipsa cellarium esse creditur istius magni Sponsi et regis. Sed qua praesumptione audebimus de charitate 555Dloqui, qui eamdem charitatem perfecte, sicut deceret, non amamus, nec possidemus; et cum fratribus vestris nullatenus charitativi apparemus? Verumtamen ista praesumptio prosilit ex amore charitatis, quae ad hoc nos incitat ut libenter disputemus de operibus charitatis. « Deus, inquit Joannes, est charitas (I Joan. IV). » Non potuit ista virtus majori rei comparari, quam ut aequipararetur omnipotenti Creatori. Quamvis enim dici liceat Deus est obedientia, humilitas, castitas, pax, patientia et sic de caeteris virtutibus; tamen de nulla tam specialiter quemadmodum de charitate dicitur Deus est illa, vel illa virtus. Deus ergo est charitas. Merito ergo Deus charitas esse dicitur, et juste Deus charitatem habere perhibetur, quia cum solus esset in sua gloria, 556Ain sua divinitate, in sua majestate, noluit solus regnum coelestis beatitudinis obtinere, sed ad felicia et immortalia gaudia perennis jucunditatis secum possidenda socios quaesivit, angelos videlicet et homines, quos ad laudem et gloriam nominis sui creavit. De cujus adhuc charitate inaestimabili, pietate incomparabili, misericordia ineffabili, beatus Augustinus sic loquitur dicens: « O inaestimabilis dilectio charitatis quam habuisti! ut servum redimeres Filium tradidisti. » Considerabat enim quam pia charitate, et quam charitativa pietate, Deus omnipotens homini miserabiliter lapso condoluit, pro cujus erectione Filium suum sibi Spirituique sancto consubstantialem et coaeternum servile carnis cinctorium induere, et mortis suspendia sustinere fecit. Sed et propheta 556B(Isa. LXIII) Dominum Jesum 324 in spiritu prophetiae vidit pallio zeli amictum et solum calcantem in torculari et de gentibus non esse cum illo aliquem virum. Hoc autem neminem lateat, quia quod est servili cinctorio carnis indui, hoc est pallio zeli amiciri, et quod designat dispendia necis immeritae perpeti, hoc nimirum significat calcare solum in torculari. Valde enim Dominus Jesus Christus nos amavit, qui pro nobis in torculari, id est in cruce, sanguinem suum fundens interiit. Unde et Isaias ait: « Ipse autem vulneratus propter iniquitates nostras, attritus est propter scelera nostra, et ejus morte nos salvati sumus (Isa. LIII). » Hinc namque Joannes dicit: « Qui dilexit nos, inquit, et lavit sanguine suo (Apoc. I). » Praeterea ista Domini 556CJesu Christi incomparabilis charitas, amor inaestimabilis, compassio inexplicabilis, bene quidem olim praefigurata est in sancto David. Refert enim de illo historia libri Regum, quod aliquando illum Absalon filius ejus, cupidus paterni regni, crudeliter persequebatur, et ei, nisi difficultas obsisteret, immeritam mortem inferre moliebatur (II Reg. XVIII). Cum autem secundum Dei dispositionem reverteretur, ipsa mors super illatorem suum et quercus densissima inter coelum et terram Absalon detineret suspensum, (neque enim dignus erat, non dicam ut coelum moriens aspiceret, sed nec talis erat quidem quem terra digne sustineret; quippe qui contra universa jura naturae, paternae dulcedini mortis discrimina 556Dnitebatur inferre) ipse David aeque ut piissimus pater, audita nece filii sui et persecutoris sui, si tamen filius dici possit, qui nefandissimus persecutor exstitit, ipse, inquam, David, inconsolabiliter lacrymas fudit. Sic nempe de illo scriptum est: « Rex autem David cooperto capite incedens lugebat filium, dicens: Absalon fili mi, fili mi Absalon, quis mihi det ut ego moriar pro te, fili mi Absalon? » (Ibid.) Ecce quomodo David fuerat introductus in cellaria charitatis, qui lacrymis irremediabilibus flebat pro morte sui persecutoris. Sed potest aliquis dicere: Quia tu vim facis divinae Scripturae; unde igitur tu scis quod luctus ille quem habuit David super interitu filii sui, figura exstiterit passionis et mortis quam sustinuit Dominus Jesus Christus pro salute 557Ageneris humani? Audi apostolum Paulum (I Cor. X), non me, dicentem quia omnia in figura contingebant patribus nostris et scripta sunt ad correptionem nostram. Si ergo David figura exstitit, sine dubio apud Dominum Jesum Christum veritas intemerata permansit. Verumtamen ad liquidum perscrutemur utrum lamentatio David passioni et morti Christi congruat, et hoc perscrutato non relinquetur objectori nostro quid juste nobis objiciat. In Evangelio namque legitur Dominus Jesus Christus in duobus locis flevisse, et postea mortem pro nobis sustinuisse: « Videns quippe Hierusalem civitatem, flevit super illam, dicens: Quia si cognovisses et tu (Luc. XIX), » subauditur quae ventura sunt tibi, fleres; quae quia modo ignoras, ad modicum temporis inepta laetitia 557Bdissolveris et gaudes. Simili quoque modo ad sepulturam Lazari (Joan. XI) utpote verus homo flere dignatus et Dominus Jesus Christus. De quo nimirum fletu dicit sanctus Augustinus, quia non mortuum flebat, quem ipse suscitavit, sed mortem, quam sibi genus humanum peccando acquisivit. Considerabat enim in quanta gloria genus humanum a suo Creatore conditum fuerat, et ad quantam miseriam inobediendo devenerat. Post vero istam binam lacrymationem, accessit Dominus Jesus ut indebitum mortis debitum solveret, quia ideo a Patre missus fuerat in mundum, quatenus moriendo genus humanum redimeret. Videamus ergo qualiter ploratus David bino fletu et simplici morti Domini nostri Jesu Christi decenter conveniat. « Rex, inquit, David 557Ccooperto capite incedens (II Reg. XIX). » Quid vero arbitramur posse significari quod David cooperto capite incedebat, nisi quia assumens divinitas in assumpta humanitate latebat? Caput quippe Christi secundum apostolum Paulum » Deus est (I Cor. XI). Et quasi nimirum David cooperto capite incessit, quando Deus in assumpto homine latuit. Annon tibi videtur quasi cooperto capite Dominus Jesus incessisse, cum humana opinio aestimabat illum, aut Eliam, aut Jeremiam, aut unum ex antiquis prophetis esse? Sed Petrus apostolus caput Domini Jesu discooperuit, quando ad illum veridica voce dixit: « Tu es Christus Filius Dei vivi (Matth. XVI). » Cum ergo Dominus Jesus per virtutem divinitatis 557Dmiracula perpetrabat, tunc David cooperto capite incedebat, quia Deus in homine latens manifestis indiciis apparebat. Cum vero per agrum hujus mundi pergens, humana solummodo pro nobis patiebatur, et faciebat, tunc David quasi cooperto capite incedens filium suum lugebat, dicens: « Absalon fili mi, fili mi Absalon (II Reg. XVIII). » Idcirco denique Dominus Jesus nostrae naturae injurias sustinuit, ut genus humanum quod per Absalon designatur, salvaret, quod ipse ad laudem nominis sui et gloriam creavit. Unde bene et Absalon patris pax, sive diabolus interpretatur, quia ille antiquus hostis, genus nostrum per inobedientiam se substravisse gloriabatur, et quia Dominus Jesus Christus per carnem quam de humano genere assumpsit, idem genus 558Ahumanum Patri suo reconciliavit et repacicavit. Quod autem David incedens et plorans nomen filii sui lacrymabiliter ingeminat, dicens: « Absalon fili mi, fili mi Absalon, » hoc utique significat 325 quod Dominus Jesus bis in Evangelio lacrymas fudit, et mortem veraciter subiit pro salute generis humani. Potest per duplicem et lacrymabilem repetitionem istius nominis compassio et mors intelligi nostri Salvatoris, quam pertulit pro salute animae nostrae simul et corporis; quia, quidquid in primo parente nostro periit, totum secundus parens assumpsit et salvavit. Per mortem quippe suam genus humanum redemit. Mentiamur et velut falsi interpretes divinae legis de mendacio redarguamur, si non hoc ipsum quod dicimus historia 558Bsequens indicat, cum manifesta voce proclamat dicens: « Quis mihi det ut ego moriar pro te, fili mi Absalon? » (Ibid.) Si ergo sicut superius praefati sumus, per David intelligimus Dominum nostrum Jesum Christum, et per Absalon accipimus genus humanum, constat pro certo, quia mors quam desideravit David pro perditione filii sui, mortem nobis insinuat, quam sustinuit Dominus Jesus pro redemptione generis humani. Obsecro te, o auditor hujus lectionis, ut mihi parcas, nec contra me indigneris pro eo quod per Absalon metaphorice dixi debere intelligi genus humanum, per David quoque piissimum et charitativum regem, Dominum nostrum Jesum Christum. Ista enim consuetudo in divinis litteris persaepe reperitur, ut videlicet una persona sub 558Callegorico sensu pro variis intelligentiis accipiatur. Sic denique beatus Gregorius metaphorice per Saul regem impiissimum, Dominum nostrum Jesum Christum; per montes vero Gelboe in quibus idem peremptus est, intelligit Judaicum populum. Ecce autem dum charitatem David et Christi figurative pro capacitate sensus nostri discussimus, in cellaria charitatis paululum longius immorati sumus. Sed contemplemur adhuc subtilius si aliquis antiquorum patrum in haec cellaria charitatis habitaverit, praeter solum David, qui pro persecutoribus suis, videlicet Absalone et Saule, clementer exoravit. O Moyses inquiline, dic nobis quid fecisti, et qualiter cellarium charitatis in persecutione positus introiisti. Aliquando enim filii Israel vitulum aureum ad adorandum 558Dfecerunt, pro quo piaculo Deum majestatis, qui tot beneficia eis contulerat ad iracundiam provocaverunt. Quo facinore comperto, Moyses, qui erat mitissimus super omnes homines qui morabantur in terra, ait ad Dominum: « Obsecro, Domine, peccavit populus iste peccatum maximum, feceruntque sibi deos aureos; aut dimitte eis hanc noxam, aut si non facis, dele me de libro tuo quem scripsisti. Cui respondit Dominus: Qui peccaverit mihi, delebo eum de libro meo. Tu autem vade, et educ populum istum (Exod. XXXII). » Et in sequentibus scriptum est quia volebat populus Moysen lapidare. Et ait idem Moyses ad Dominum: « Obsecro, Domine Deus, dimitte peccatum hujus populi secundum multitudinem 559Amisericordiae tuae. Dixitque Dominus ad Moysen: Dimisi secundum verbum tuum (Num. XIV). » Ecce quemadmodum iste introductus erat in cellaria charitatis; ecce qualiter sanctus Moyses abundabat visceribus charitatis, qui tanto affectu pro idolatris et pro persecutoribus suis, Dei majestatem deprecabatur, ut etiam deleri se posceret de libro vitae aeternae, cui jam ascriptus tenebatur, si non eis haec offensa a Deo remitteretur. Sed ecce dum sermonem facio de charitate sancti Moysis, charitatem David non possum tradere oblivioni. Refert enim historiae Regum liber secundus, quod olim Deus populo Israelitico, cui praeerat David, admodum fuerat iratus: misitque angelum suum, et interfecit de populo Judaeorum usque ad septuaginta millia virorum. Cum autem 559BDavid angelum Domini vidisset caedentem populum motus pietate ait ad Dominum: « Ego sum qui peccavi: ego inique egi; isti qui oves sunt, quid fecerunt? vertatur, oro, manus tua contra me, et contra domum patris mei (II Reg. XXIV). » Videns Deus igitur charitatem David quam habebat, qui pro populo sibi commisso mori exoptabat, dixit angelo percutienti: « Sufficit nunc (ibid.). » Et cohibita est plaga desaeviens ab Israele, et repropitiatus est Dominus terrae suae. Praeterea Samuel propheta quasi reprobus dudum a populo eodem de principatu suo ejiciebatur, et idem tamen populus eumdem prophetam quem de sede sua propellebat, ut pro ipsis Dominum exoraret, deprecabatur. Quid dixerit, quidve responderit, vel quid locutus fuerit inimicis 559Csuis audiant illi qui vix aut fortasse nunquam sincero corde orant pro amicis suis. « Absit hoc peccatum a me in Domino, quo minus cessem orare pro vobis, et ostendam vobis viam bonam per quam incedere debeatis (I Reg. XII). » Item idem propheta in eodem libro invenitur luxisse pro Saule rege impiissimo, ob cujus causam ipse etiam ejectus fuerat de principatu suo. Eapropter dicebat et Dominus ad illum. « Usquequo tu luges Saul, cum ego abjecerim eum? » (I Reg. XVI.) Ecce quomodo isti tres viri ex eo tempore jam tunc observabant illud quod Dominus dicturus erat: « Diligite inimicos vestros, benefacite iis qui oderunt vos (Matth. V). » Eam majorem quippe charitatem nemo habet, quam ut pro persecutoribus 559Dsuis exoret, et animam suam pro eis ad mortem tradat (Joan. XV). Vix tandem poterimus egredi de cellariis charitatis, tot et tanta opera et exempla occurrunt nobis ejusdem charitatis. Sunt adhuc et alia cellaria istius magni et praeclari Sponsi gloriosique Regis, scilicet omnes virtutes, sicut jam praefati sumus, quae inveniuntur in libris divinae legis. De quibus 326 hic aliquas nominatim exprimere curabimus, ne eas illibatas sub negligentia pertransiisse videamur. Charitas semper erit principalis, et prima scilicet quam sequetur humilitas, fides, spes, parcimonia, sobrietas, virginitas, continentia, castitas, misericordia, obedientia, pax, patientia, longanimitas, perseverantia, peccatorum poenitentia, et pura confessio. Mandatorum Dei ex vera dilectione 560Aobservatio, bonitas, concordia, benignitas, mansuetudo, fraternus amor, spiritualis in Deo societas, eleemosynarum largitio, rationabilis morum compositio, contemptus sui, despectus mundi, abjectio gloriae saecularis, abrenuntiatio propriae voluntatis, amor spiritualis, paupertatis, jejunia, vigiliae, orationes, lacrymae, carnis maceratio, in lege Dei assidua meditatio, animae nostrae semper infatigabilis in coelestibus contemplatio, gaudium, exsultatio, jubilatio: non in terrenis rebus, sed in Spiritu sancto, temperantia in prosperis, fortitudo in adversariis. Ecce Deo aspirante quadraginta duas virtutes in hoc catalogo composui, in quibus iter usque ad coelum volentibus ascendere sine obstaculo praeparavi. Per has enim virtutes si quis voluerit gradatim 560Bconscendere, poterit absque dubio usque ad coeli fastigia penetrare. Denique hoc notandum quia principalis virtus ipsa est charitas in hoc catalogo, et ultima ipsa est indeficiens fortitudo, quoniam quidem sicut Salomon in Canticis canticorum dicit: « Fortis est ut mors charitas, sive dilectio (Cant. VIII). » Et nequaquam valet custodiri charitas sine spirituali fortitudine, nec fortitudo absque vera charitate. Si igitur vere credimus quod et nos et sermones nostri in manu Dei sumus, tunc profecto nunc abs re factum esse creditur, quoniam quidem quadraginta duae virtutes in serie hujus catalogi positae inveniuntur. Quadraginta quippe mansiones et duae exstiterunt, per quas filii Israel de Aegypto egredientes ad terram repromissionis venerunt. 560CPorro Dominus Jesus Christus quando ad imperium Dei Patris pro salute generis humani de coelo ad terras descendit, per quadraginta duas generationes usque ad uterum beatae et perpetuae semperque Virginis Mariae pervenit. O si quis esset scriba doctus in regno Dei! O si quis posset nova et vetera proferre de thesauro cordis sui! O, inquam, si quis esset ita eruditus in lege divina, ut sciret digne et eleganter aptare et assignare singulis singula, qualiter scilicet singulae virtutes quas jam praelibavimus rationabili ordine conveniant singulis generationibus et mansionibus. Et rursum quomodo singulae generationes congruant singulis virtutibus et mansionibus; et iterum quam seriatim singulae mansiones aptari queant singulis virtutibus et generationibus. 560DEnim vero quis est tantus ac talis qui audeat dicere: « Os meum aperui, et attraxi Spiritum sanctum (Psal. CXVIII), » vel cui Deus in secreto cordis sui loquatur, et dicat: « Aperi os tuum, et implebo illud? » (Psal. LXXX.) Ille nimirum posset explicare vel enodare profunditatem tantorum sacramentorum, quae latet infra continentiam harum virtutum, generationum simul et mansionum. Sed, licet istud opus excedat nostri ingenioli paupertatem, tamen confisi in eo qui ait discipulis suis: « Non enim vos estis qui loquimini, sed Spiritus Patris vestri qui loquitur in vobis (Matth. X), » tentabimus praelibare duntaxat harum virtutum generationumque et mansionum principia, et sub ipso intellectu 561Aquem proferemus illa quae supererunt nostris auditoribus relinquemus intelligenda. Prima namque mansio filiorum Israel Ramasse dicitur, in exordio vero generationis Jesu Christi, Abraham patriarcha ponitur; in catalogo autem virtutum quem composuimus charitas antefertur. Abraham interpretatur pater multarum gentium, Ramasse commotio tineae, et Deus est charitas secundum Joannem apostolum (I Joan. IV). Quid ergo intelligimus per multas gentes quarum pater dicitur esse Abraham, nisi diversarum virtutum ingentem multitudinem? Quid vero debemus accipere per tineae commotionem, nisi peccatorum et vitiorum insolentem infestationem? Deus igitur qui est charitas ipse dat cui vult per gratiam suam ut possit esse pater multarum virtutum, et ipse 561Beliminat et exstirpat et removet ab eo, cujus miseretur, omnes commotiones et infestationes, et insolentias omnium vitiorum et peccatorum. Sub isto itaque sensu, et sub meliori, si cui Deus revelaverit, intelligi oportet secundam filiorum Israel mansionem secundamque generationem Jesu Christi et secundam nostri catalogi virtutem. Sic namque scriptum est: « Profecti sunt filii Israel de Ramesse in Sochoth, qui locus interpretatur tabernacula (Exod. XII). » In serie quoque generationis Domini nostri Jesu Christi, 562AIsaac filius Abrahae fuisse dignoscitur, et in catalogo supra memorato post charitatem humilitas in ordine subsequitur: Mira res valde, et in aevum digna admirabili et multimoda laude! Ecce jam satis considerare libet qualiter tabernacula conveniunt generationi et generatio virtuti. Isaac namque, sicut dicit sanctus Paulus (Hebr. XI), cum cohaeredibus coelestis regni habitavit in tabernaculis, et in eo resplenduerunt utique insignia incomparabilis humilitatis. Quis digne siquidem explicare valeat quam admirabilem humilitatem Isaac habuit (Gen. XXII), qui patri quando volebat eum Deo immolare nequaquam contradixit? Praeterea idem Isaac interpretatur risus vel gaudium, quia vox 327 exsultationis et salutis est in tabernaculis justorum (Psal. CVII). Porro vera 562Bsalus non nisi in humilitate sacra acquiritur, quoniam, sicut divina Scriptura dicit, « Humilis tantummodo spiritu salvabitur (Psal. XXXIII). » Si quis igitur bene sciret discurrere exponendo per reliquas mansiones generationes et virtutes, fateor veritatem rei, maxima pars temporis illum desereret potius quam copia dicendi. Praebebimus igitur nostris auditoribus materiam cogitandi, quia si omnia exponeremus ipsi remanerent taedio torpentes et otiosi.

561Explicit quintus tomus in Canticis.

RECAPITULATION ACCORDING TO THE ANAGOGE AND THE MORALITY UPON THAT SENTENCE: « THE KING HATH BROUGHT ME INTO HIS STOREROOMS. »

All the arts of human knowledge, in the midst of the teaching — that is, when they are taught or practised — offer to their hearers certain delightful refuges outside themselves, as for example are the polishers, or the hewers of wood 555A, or those who are busied in the stone-quarries, or goldsmiths or builders, or of any other art whatsoever that is discerned to pertain to human judgment. For all these have within their working certain empty consolations. But while the polishers polish marbles, they talk, they tell tales, they laugh, they make merry; and sometimes even certain things which are proved to pertain nothing at all to that same art. But our art, namely that of spiritual letters, in the learning or in the teaching wills to have the whole man intent upon it. For either a man shall offer his whole self intent upon our art, or he shall by no means come to a clear mastery of the skill of that same art. For who ever, with a halved heart, or with the senses turned away, or with a wandering and unstable mind, was able to be a good clerk 555B? It is needful therefore that not only the things which are of this world, but even himself for the greatest part, if it be lawful to say so, he hand over to oblivion, who longs perfectly to attain those things which in the divine books lie subject to human intention. Earthly pleasures therefore being lulled to sleep, and depraved works despised, which are wont very often to plunge incautious men into the depth of the gulf, let us come with no halved heart, but with a whole mind and sincere affection to the Bridegroom, and let us beseech him with humble words together with his most dear Bride, that he may deign to bring us in, devoted to him, into his storerooms. Above therefore I confess I said that the storerooms of this Bridegroom and king were, allegorically, all the books of the divine law. But now I say likewise that of the same Bridegroom and king the storerooms are, morally, 555Call the virtues that can be found scattered here and there throughout the sacred eloquences. Nevertheless, because we desire to begin a discourse concerning the virtues, it follows that we direct our stylus to the head or beginning of all the virtues. Now the head or beginning from which all the virtues go forth is without doubt discerned to be charity. Whence the blessed Gregory says: « As from one stock of a tree many branches go forth, so from one charity many virtues are begotten, nor has the branch of good work anything of greenness if it has not abided in the root of charity. » Let charity itself therefore be itself the opening of our narration, since it is believed to be the storeroom of this great Bridegroom and king. But with what presumption shall we dare to speak of charity 555D, who do not perfectly love that same charity, as would be fitting, nor possess it; and appear in no wise charitable with your brethren? Nevertheless this presumption leaps forth out of the love of charity, which stirs us to this, that we should willingly dispute of the works of charity. « God, says John, is charity (I Joan. IV). » This virtue could not be compared to a greater thing than that it should be made equal to the omnipotent Creator. For although it is lawful to say, God is obedience, humility, chastity, peace, patience, and so of the other virtues; yet of none is it so specially said, as of charity, God is that or that virtue. God therefore is charity. Deservedly then is God said to be charity, and justly is God declared to have charity, because when he was alone in his glory, 556Ain his divinity, in his majesty, he willed not alone to hold the kingdom of heavenly beatitude, but sought companions to possess with him the happy and immortal joys of everlasting delight, namely angels and men, whom he created to the praise and glory of his name. Of whose inestimable charity still, incomparable tenderness, unspeakable mercy, the blessed Augustine speaks thus, saying: « O inestimable love of charity which thou hast had! that thou mightest redeem a servant, thou didst deliver up a Son. » For he was considering with what loving charity, and with what charitable love, God almighty grieved with man wretchedly fallen, for whose raising up he made his own Son, consubstantial and coeternal with himself and with the Holy Spirit, to put on the servile girding of flesh, and to endure the hangings of death. But the prophet also 556B(Isa. LXIII) saw the Lord Jesus 324 in the spirit of prophecy clothed with the cloak of zeal and treading alone in the winepress, and that of the Gentiles there was not any man with him. But let this lie hid from no one, that what it is to be clothed with the servile girding of flesh, this it is to be wrapped in the cloak of zeal; and what betokens the suffering of the losses of an undeserved slaying, this assuredly signifies to tread alone in the winepress. For greatly did the Lord Jesus Christ love us, who for us perished in the winepress, that is, on the cross, pouring out his blood. Whence also Isaias says: « But he was wounded for our iniquities, he was bruised for our wickednesses, and by his death we are saved (Isa. LIII). » [Vulg. Isa. 53:5 et livore ejus sanati sumus, "and by his bruise we are healed"] Hence indeed John says: « Who hath loved us, says he, and washed [us] with his blood (Apoc. I). » Moreover this 556Cincomparable charity of the Lord Jesus Christ, this inestimable love, this inexplicable compassion, was well indeed of old prefigured in holy David. For the history of the book of Kings relates of him that at a certain time Absalom his son, greedy of his father's kingdom, was cruelly persecuting him, and, unless difficulty had stood in the way, was contriving to bring an undeserved death upon him (II Reg. XVIII). But when, according to God's disposition, that same death was turned back upon him who brought it, and a most dense oak held Absalom hanging between heaven and earth (for neither was he worthy — I will not say that he should look upon heaven as he died, but neither indeed was he such as the earth should worthily bear up; seeing that against all the laws of nature he strove to bring the perils of death 556Dupon a father's sweetness) — David himself, even as a most loving father, hearing of the slaying of his son and of his persecutor, if indeed he can be called a son who stood forth a most abominable persecutor, he himself, I say, David, poured out tears inconsolably. For thus it is written of him: « Now king David, walking with covered head, mourned his son, saying: Absalom my son, my son Absalom, who will grant me that I may die for thee, my son Absalom? » (Ibid.) Behold how David had been brought into the storerooms of charity, who wept with irremediable tears for the death of his persecutor. But someone can say: Thou doest violence to Holy Scripture; whence therefore dost thou know that that mourning which David had over the destruction of his son stood forth as a figure of the passion and death which the Lord Jesus Christ endured for the salvation 557Aof the human race? Hear the apostle Paul (I Cor. X), not me, saying that all things happened to our fathers in figure, and were written for our correction. If therefore David stood forth as a figure, without doubt in the Lord Jesus Christ the truth abided undefiled. Nevertheless let us search out clearly whether David's lamentation agrees with the passion and death of Christ, and this being searched out, there will be left to our objector nothing which he may justly object against us. For in the Gospel the Lord Jesus Christ is read to have wept in two places, and afterwards to have endured death for us: « For seeing the city Jerusalem, he wept over it, saying: If thou also hadst known (Luc. XIX), » — there is understood, the things that are to come upon thee, thou wouldst weep; which, because thou now knowest not, for a little space of time thou art 557Bdissolved in unfitting gladness and rejoicest. In a like manner also the Lord Jesus Christ, as being true man, deigned to weep at the burial of Lazarus (Joan. XI). Of which weeping assuredly saint Augustine says that he wept not the dead man, whom he himself raised, but the death which the human race acquired for itself by sinning. For he was considering in how great glory the human race had been founded by its Creator, and to how great misery it had come by disobeying. But after that twofold weeping, the Lord Jesus went forward to pay the undue debt of death, because he had for this been sent by the Father into the world, that by dying he might redeem the human race. Let us see then how the wailing of David fittingly agrees with the twofold weeping and the single death of our Lord Jesus Christ. « King, says he, David 557Cwalking with covered head (II Reg. XIX). » But what do we judge can be signified by David walking with covered head, if not that the assuming divinity lay hid in the assumed humanity? For the head of Christ, according to the apostle Paul » is God (I Cor. XI). And as it were assuredly David walked with covered head, when God lay hid in the man he had assumed. Does it not seem to thee that the Lord Jesus walked as it were with covered head, when human opinion reckoned him to be either Elias, or Jeremias, or one of the ancient prophets? But Peter the apostle uncovered the head of the Lord Jesus, when he said to him with a truth-speaking voice: « Thou art Christ the Son of the living God (Matth. XVI). » When therefore the Lord Jesus by the power of the divinity 557Dwrought miracles, then David walked with covered head, because God lying hid in the man appeared by manifest tokens. But when, passing through the field of this world, he suffered and did for us only human things, then David, as it were walking with covered head, mourned his son, saying: « Absalom my son, my son Absalom (II Reg. XVIII). » For this cause finally did the Lord Jesus endure the injuries of our nature, that he might save the human race which is designated by Absalom, which he himself created to the praise and glory of his name. Whence Absalom is well interpreted the father's peace, or the devil, because that ancient enemy gloried that our race had laid itself low through disobedience, and because the Lord Jesus Christ, through the flesh which he assumed of the human race, reconciled and 558Arepacicavit that same human race with his Father. But that David, walking and weeping, tearfully doubles the name of his son, saying: « Absalom my son, my son Absalom, » this surely signifies 325 that the Lord Jesus twice poured out tears in the Gospel, and truly underwent death for the salvation of the human race. By the twofold and tearful repetition of that name may be understood the compassion and death of our Saviour, which he bore for the salvation of our soul together with our body; because whatever perished in our first parent, the second parent took up wholly and saved. For by his death he redeemed the human race. Let us be lying, and let us be convicted of falsehood as false interpreters of the divine law, if the history that follows does not indicate this very thing which we say, 558Bwhen it proclaims with manifest voice, saying: « Who will grant me that I may die for thee, my son Absalom? » (Ibid.) If therefore, as we premised above, by David we understand our Lord Jesus Christ, and by Absalom we take the human race, it stands for certain that the death which David desired for the destruction of his son insinuates to us the death which the Lord Jesus endured for the redemption of the human race. I beseech thee, O hearer of this lesson, that thou spare me, and be not indignant against me for this, that I have said that by Absalom, metaphorically, the human race ought to be understood, and by David also, that most loving and charitable king, our Lord Jesus Christ. For this usage is very often found in the divine letters, namely that one person is taken under an 558Callegorical sense for various understandings. Thus finally the blessed Gregory, metaphorically, understands by Saul, that most impious king, our Lord Jesus Christ; but by the mountains of Gelboe, on which the same was slain, the Jewish people. But behold, while we have discussed the charity of David and of Christ figuratively according to the capacity of our sense, we have tarried a little too long in the storerooms of charity. But let us contemplate still more subtly whether any of the ancient fathers dwelt in these storerooms of charity, besides David alone, who mercifully prayed for his persecutors, namely Absalom and Saul. O Moses, thou lodger, tell us what thou didst, and how, set in persecution, thou didst enter the storeroom of charity. For at a certain time the sons of Israel made a golden calf 558Dto be adored, by which crime they provoked to wrath the God of majesty, who had bestowed on them so many benefits. Which misdeed being discovered, Moses, who was the meekest above all men that dwelt on the earth, said to the Lord: « I beseech thee, O Lord, this people hath sinned a very great sin, and they have made to themselves gods of gold; either forgive them this trespass, or if thou dost not, blot me out of thy book which thou hast written. To whom the Lord answered: He that shall have sinned against me, him will I blot out of my book. But go thou, and lead forth this people (Exod. XXXII). » And in what follows it is written that the people wished to stone Moses. And the same Moses said to the Lord: « I beseech thee, O Lord God, forgive the sin of this people according to the multitude 559Aof thy mercy. And the Lord said to Moses: I have forgiven according to thy word (Num. XIV). » [Vulg. Num. 14:19 secundum magnitudinem misericordiae tuae, "according to the greatness of thy mercy"] Behold in what manner he had been brought into the storerooms of charity; behold how holy Moses abounded in the bowels of charity, who with so great affection besought the majesty of God for idolaters and for his persecutors, that he even asked to be blotted out of the book of eternal life, in which he was already held enrolled, if this offence were not remitted them by God. But behold, while I make discourse of the charity of holy Moses, I cannot hand over to oblivion the charity of David. For the second book of the history of Kings relates that once God was exceedingly angry with the people of Israel, over whom David was set: and he sent his angel, and slew of the people of the Jews as many as seventy thousand men. But when 559BDavid had seen the angel of the Lord striking the people, moved with tenderness he said to the Lord: « I am he who sinned: I have done wickedly; these who are sheep, what have they done? let thy hand, I pray, be turned against me, and against the house of my father (II Reg. XXIV). » God therefore, seeing the charity of David which he had, who longed to die for the people committed to him, said to the striking angel: « It is enough now (ibid.). » And the raging plague was restrained from Israel, and the Lord was made favourable again to his land. Moreover Samuel the prophet was of old cast out as it were reprobate by that same people from his principality, and yet that same people entreated that same prophet whom they were driving from his seat, that he would pray the Lord for them. What he said, or what he answered, or what he spoke to his enemies, 559Clet those hear who scarcely, or perhaps never, pray with a sincere heart for their friends. « Far be this sin from me in the Lord, that I should not cease to pray for you, and I will show you the good way by which you ought to walk (I Reg. XII). » [Vulg. I Reg. 12:23 absit a me hoc peccatum in Dominum ut cessem orare pro vobis … et docebo vos viam bonam et rectam, "against the Lord … *that I should cease to pray for you … and I will teach you the good and right way"; Migne prints quo minus cessem, which reverses the clause] Likewise the same prophet is found in the same book to have mourned for Saul, that most impious king, on whose account he himself had also been cast out from his principality. Wherefore the Lord also said to him: « How long dost thou mourn Saul, when I have cast him away? » (I Reg. XVI.) Behold how these three men from that time already then observed that which the Lord was to say: « Love your enemies, do good to them that hate you (Matth. V). » For no man has that greater charity, than that he should pray for his persecutors 559Dand deliver up his own soul unto death for them (Joan. XV). Scarcely at last shall we be able to come forth out of the storerooms of charity, so many and so great works and examples of that same charity meet us. There are still other storerooms also of this great and illustrious Bridegroom and glorious King, namely all the virtues, as we have already premised, which are found in the books of the divine law. Of which 326 we shall here take care to express some by name, lest we seem to have passed them over untouched through negligence. Charity will always be the principal and the first, which humility shall follow, faith, hope, thrift, sobriety, virginity, continence, chastity, mercy, obedience, peace, patience, long-suffering, perseverance, penitence for sins, and pure confession. The keeping of God's commandments out of true love 560A, goodness, concord, kindness, meekness, brotherly affection, spiritual fellowship in God, the bestowing of alms, a reasonable ordering of manners, contempt of self, despising of the world, casting away of worldly glory, renunciation of one's own will, spiritual love, poverty, fastings, watchings, prayers, tears, mortification of the flesh, assiduous meditation in the law of God, our soul's ever untiring contemplation of heavenly things, joy, exultation, jubilation: not in earthly things, but in the Holy Spirit, temperance in prosperity, fortitude among adversaries. Behold, God breathing upon me, I have composed in this catalogue forty-two virtues, in which I have prepared without obstacle the way as far as heaven for those willing to ascend. For by these virtues, if anyone shall have willed step by step 560Bto climb, he will be able without doubt to penetrate as far as the summits of heaven. Lastly, this is to be noted, that the principal virtue in this catalogue is charity itself, and the last is unfailing fortitude itself, since indeed, as Solomon says in the Song of Songs: « Charity, or love, is strong as death (Cant. VIII). » And charity can by no means be kept without spiritual fortitude, nor fortitude without true charity. If therefore we truly believe that both we and our discourses are in the hand of God, then assuredly it is believed to have been done now beside the purpose, seeing that forty-two virtues are found set in the series of this catalogue. For forty mansions and two there were, through which the sons of Israel, going forth out of Egypt, came to the land of promise. 560CFurther, the Lord Jesus Christ, when at the command of God the Father he came down from heaven to the lands for the salvation of the human race, came through forty-two generations as far as the womb of the blessed and perpetual and ever Virgin Mary. O if there were one who were a scribe learned in the kingdom of God! O if there were one who could bring forth new things and old out of the treasure of his heart! O, I say, if there were one so instructed in the divine law that he knew worthily and elegantly to fit and assign single things to single ones — namely how the single virtues which we have already tasted beforehand agree in a reasonable order with the single generations and mansions. And again, how the single generations accord with the single virtues and mansions; and yet again, how in order the single mansions may be fitted to the single virtues and generations. 560DFor truly who is so great and such a one as to dare to say: « I opened my mouth, and drew in the Holy Spirit (Psal. CXVIII), » or to whom God may speak in the secret of his heart, and say: « Open thy mouth, and I will fill it? » (Psal. LXXX.) [Vulg. Ps. 80:11 dilata os tuum, "open wide thy mouth"] He assuredly would be able to explain or unravel the profundity of such great sacraments, which lies hid within the compass of these virtues, of the generations and of the mansions together. But, although that work exceed the poverty of our little wit, yet trusting in him who says to his disciples: « For it is not you that speak, but the Spirit of your Father that speaketh in you (Matth. X), » we shall attempt to taste beforehand only the beginnings of these virtues and generations and mansions, and under that understanding 561Awhich we shall bring forth we shall leave to our hearers the things that remain to be understood. For the first mansion of the sons of Israel is called Ramasse, but in the opening of the generation of Jesus Christ the patriarch Abraham is set; and in the catalogue of the virtues which we have composed charity is put first. Abraham is interpreted father of many nations, Ramasse the stirring of the moth, and God is charity according to John the apostle (I Joan. IV). What therefore do we understand by the many nations of which Abraham is said to be the father, if not a vast multitude of divers virtues? And what ought we to take by the stirring of the moth, if not the insolent infestation of sins and of vices? God therefore, who is charity, himself gives to whom he wills by his grace that he may be able to be a father of many virtues, and himself 561Bdrives out and roots up and removes from him on whom he has mercy all the stirrings and infestations, and the insolences of all vices and sins. Under this sense therefore, and under a better, if God shall have revealed it to anyone, it behoves us to understand the second mansion of the sons of Israel and the second generation of Jesus Christ and the second virtue of our catalogue. For thus it is written: « The sons of Israel set forth from Ramesse to Sochoth, which place is interpreted tabernacles (Exod. XII). » In the series also of the generation of our Lord Jesus Christ, 562AIsaac is discerned to have been the son of Abraham, and in the catalogue mentioned above humility follows next in order after charity: a very wondrous thing, and worthy for ever of admirable and manifold praise! Behold, it is now pleasing enough to consider how the tabernacles agree with the generation and the generation with the virtue. For Isaac, as saint Paul says (Hebr. XI), dwelt in tabernacles with the coheirs of the heavenly kingdom, and in him shone forth assuredly the tokens of an incomparable humility. Who indeed could worthily explain how admirable a humility Isaac had (Gen. XXII), who in no wise contradicted his father when he wished to immolate him to God? Moreover the same Isaac is interpreted laughter or joy, because the voice 327 of exultation and of salvation is in the tabernacles of the just (Psal. CVII). Further, true 562Bsalvation is not gained save in holy humility, since, as Holy Scripture says, « Only the humble in spirit shall be saved (Psal. XXXIII). » [Vulg. Ps. 33:19 humiles spiritu salvabit*, "he will save the humble in spirit"] If therefore anyone knew well how to run through, by expounding, the remaining mansions, generations and virtues, I confess the truth of the matter, the greatest part of time would fail him rather than the abundance of matter for speaking. We shall therefore offer our hearers matter for thinking, because if we expounded all things they themselves would remain numb with weariness and idle.

561Here ends the fifth Tome on the Canticles.

INCIPIT SEXTUS TOMUS Ab eo quod dicitur: Nigra sum, sed formosa, filiae Jerusalem, usque ad vineam meam non custodivi (Cant. I).

561CQui aurum quaerunt, et in montibus aureis demorantur, idcirco egesta superiori humo, inferiora et altiora terrae perscrutantur, ut ad diu desideratum pondus auri cum labore et sudore pervenire mereantur. Cum autem diu fodiendo fortassis aurum reperiunt, quamvis rubigine superducta coopertum, et terrae sordibus denigratum conspiciunt, nullatenus tamen illud contemnunt. Sciunt enim quia si igne projectum fuerit, et inter manus malleatorum devenerit, nigredinem accidentalem cum rubigine et sordibus illico deponet, et naturalem auri claritatem cum fulgore flammante sine mora recipiet. Qui sunt ergo qui aurum quaerunt, nisi qui sapientiam et spiritualem intelligentiam divinarum Scripturarum diligunt? Qui vero montes aurei, nisi 561Dsancti doctores, prophetae scilicet et apostoli? De quibus scriptum est: « Levavi oculos meos in montes, unde veniet auxilium mihi (Psal. CXX). » Quid igitur significat massa auri rubigine et sordibus cooperta, nisi scripturam prophetarum quae valde est obscura, et diversis aenigmatibus et figuris involuta? Quam necesse est ut tam diu igne Spiritus sancti decoquamus, donec abjecta scoria litterae occidentis, ad sapientiam et spiritualem intelligentiam perveniamus. Sicut ergo aurum quamvis rubigine et sordibus exterius decoloratum nequaquam despicitur, 562Cquia fulgor et claritas interior consideratur, sic scriptura prophetarum, quamvis duro cortice exterioris litterae sit circumspecta, nullatenus tamen est despicienda, quia intus latet pretiosa et spiritualis intelligentia. Ut igitur clarius quod dicimus jam manifestetur, perpendamus quid sit quod Sponsa, id est sancta Ecclesia, ad adolescentulas loquitur: Nigra sum, inquit, sed formosa, filiae Jerusalem Nigra quidem exterius, sed formosa interius; ac si dicat: Aurum sum nigredine accidentalis rubiginis obscuratum, sed si igne adhibito conflagrata fuero, aurum clarissimum, id est obrysum apparebo. Sic enim ait Scriptura: « Sicut igne probatur aurum, sic justos probat tribulatio tentationum (Prov. XVII). » Jerusalem autem interpretatur visio pacis, filias vero 562DJerusalem fideles animas appellat, quas superius adolescentulas vocaverat. Quarum studium est et summa intentio, ut possint placere soli Deo, qui secundum apostolum Paulum (Ephes. II) pax nostra est, et fecit utraque unum. Dicat ergo sancta Ecclesia: O vos, filiae Jerusalem, id est, o vos, fideles animae, quae Deum, qui est pax nostra, assidue desideratis videre, nolite perturbari, nolite admirari, quia nigra sum in passione martyrum meorum, sed gaudete et laetamini, quia formosa sum in perceptione aeternorum praemiorum. Nigra quidem sum, 563Aet lugeo propter persecutiones praesentes quas mihi inferunt haeretici et mali Christiani, sed formosa sum et gaudeo, sciens quod per multas tribulationes oportet me intrare in regnum Dei. Modo subinfert comparationem suae nigredinis et suae formositatis, dicens: Sicut tabernacula Cedar nigra sum, et sicut pellis Salomonis formosa sum. Cedar filius fuit Ismael filii Abrahae, et interpretatur Cedar tenebrae. De hoc itaque refertur quia maximam persecutionem a vicinis suis sustinuerit, et semper mutando tabernacula de loco ad locum fugerit. Quod utique bene sanctae Ecclesiae congruit, quae inter tenebras angustiarum et persecutiones adversariorum posita dicit: « Non habemus hic manentem civitatem, sed futuram inquirimus (Hebr. XIII). » De his quippe 563Badversariis eadem Ecclesia conqueritur, dicens in Psalmis quia « odio habuerunt me gratis (Psal. XXIV; Joan. XV). » De pelle autem Salomonis non recordor me quidquam reperisse scriptum, nisi in isto loco tantummodo, neque in Novo, neque in Veteri Testamento. Eapropter quaestio magna nobis generatur. Sed sciendum est quia non sine ingenti sacramento in litteris divinis reperitur, quod cum tanta difficultate et obscuritate vix intelligitur. Nisi vero a parte totum intellexerimus, nullam profecto intelligentiam spiritualem in isto loco capiemus. Mos enim est sacrae Scripturae aliquando totum a parte, aliquando a toto partem accipere. Quod utique in isto loco necesse est facere. Legimus siquidem in libro Regum 563C328 quod in tempore suo rex potentissimus Salomon, congregato universo Israel, profectus sit cum magno apparatu in Gabaon, quia ibi erat excelsum majus (III Reg. III), et tabernaculum quod praeceperat Dominus fieri per manum Moysi servi sui, quando egressi sunt filii Israel de terra Aegypti. Quod tabernaculum factum est ad instar et similitudinem cujusdam spiritualis et coelestis tabernaculi, quod ostensum est in monte Moysi famulo Dei. Sic praeterea dicit Dominus ad Moysen: « Vide, inquit, ut tu facias tabernaculum secundum formam illius tabernaculi quod in monte vidisti (Exod. XXV). » Ergo tabernaculum terrenum factum est ad instar tabernaculi coelestis. Quia vero tabernaculum, quod a Moyse constitutum est, de pellibus fuerit, testatur 563DDavid, qui ad Nathan prophetam dixit: « Videsne quod ego habito in domo cedrina, et arca Dei est in medio pellium (I Par. XVII), » hoc est in tabernaculo de pellibus constituto. Quem ergo alium Salomon qui interpretatur pacificus, nisi Christum designavit, de quo Paulus dicit quia per sanguinem suum quae in coelo sunt et quae in terra pacificavit. Quid vero significat quod idem Salomon cum universo Israel in Gabaon perrexit, nisi hoc quod Christus cum his quos ab inferis abstraxit in coelum ascendit. Unde David dicit: « Ascendens Christus in altum, captivam duxit captivitatem (Psal. LXVII). » Quid vero est illud excelsum majus, nisi cognitio summae et incomprehensibilis Trinitatis? Non enim possumus majus conscendere excelsum, quam ut 564Acognoscamus sicuti est ab aeterno Patrem et Filium et Spiritum sanctum, unum et verum esse Deum. Cujus vero rei figuram tabernaculum illud gerebat, quod de pellibus mortuorum animalium constructum erat, nisi praesentis Ecclesiae quae a sanctis Patribus nostris, prophetis scilicet et apostolis, fundata et constituta est? Ergo per pellem Salomonis totum tabernaculum quod de pellibus fuit constructum, id est a parte totum spiritualiter recipere debemus. Alioquin in ista sententia nullum spiritualem intellectum prorsus reperiemus. Dicat ergo Sponsa, id est sancta Ecclesia: Nigra sum sicut tabernacula Cedar, id est quemadmodum Cedar quondam passus est persecutionem a vicinis suis, sic ego quotidie patior infestationem ab haereticis et falsis Christianis, 564Bsed formosa sum sicut pellis Salomonis, id est sicut tabernaculum Salomonis de pellibus constructum, quod propter multiplicia ornamenta aurea et argentea quae intus continebat, inaestimabili modo et incomparabili claritate refulgebat. Nolite me considerare quod fusca sum, hoc est, nolite me despicere propter innumeras oppressiones quas ab adversariis sustineo, quia pro ipsis coelestem gloriam ab aeterno judice in die judicii exspecto. Quia decoloravit me sol, idcirco, inquit, non debetis me despectu habere si inter tribulationes fusca, id est nigra appareo, quia decoloravit me sol, hoc est, quia propter Christum, qui est sol justitiae, ista libenter amplector et patior; sciens certissime quia quanto in hoc saeculo pro ipsius amore fuero humilior et abjectior, 564Ctanto in aeterna felicitate coram majestate ejus ero gloriosior et acceptior. Filii matris meae pugnaverunt contra me, ubi infirmus majorem dolorem patitur, ibi saepius manum apponere cogitur. Quod sancta Ecclesia his facere videtur, quae de haereticis et falsis Christianis iterando conqueritur dicens: Filii matris meae pugnaverunt contra me. Mater sanctae Ecclesiae gratia est Spiritus sancti, a qua regenerati sunt in sacro baptismate haeretici et falsi Christiani, qui adversus eamdem Ecclesiam pugnant verbis et factis et pravis doctrinis. De quibus Joannes in Epistolis suis ait: « Ex nobis exierunt, sed non erant ex nobis. Si enim essent ex nobis, permansissent utique nobiscum (I Joan. II). » Hinc 564DPaulus dicit: « Scio quia post discessum meum intrabunt in vos lupi graves, qui non parcent vobis (Act. XX). » Posuerunt me custodem in vineis. Sancta Ecclesia hic loquitur ex persona sanctorum apostolorum, de quibus luce clarius constat quia facti fuerint plantatores et custodes vinearum, id est Ecclesiarum de gentibus congregatarum. Vineam meam non custodivi, id est Synagogam de qua nati sumus, propter infidelitatem suam et cordis caecitatem deseruimus, et ad gentes, quae cum gaudio susceperunt verbum Dei, transitum fecimus. Unde Paulus ad Judaeos dicit: « Vobis oportebat loqui primum verbum Dei, sed quia repulistis illud, et indignos vos judicastis aeternae vitae, ecce convertimur ad gentes (Act. XIII). » Ergo quasi custodes positi sunt apostoli in 565Agentium vineis, quando dictum est eis: « Ite, docete omnes gentes (Matth. XXVIII). Sed tunc vineam suam non custodierunt, quando Judaei perfidi illos de Synagoga expulerunt, comminantes ne in nomine Jesu alicui loquerentur (Act. IV).

HERE BEGINS THE SIXTH TOME From that which is said: I am black, but comely, O daughters of Jerusalem, as far as my vineyard I have not kept (Cant. I).

561CThey who seek gold, and linger in the golden mountains, for this reason, when the upper soil has been dug away, search through the lower and deeper parts of the earth, that with labor and sweat they may deserve to come to the long-desired weight of gold. But when by long digging they perhaps find gold, although they behold it covered over with rust drawn across it, and blackened with the filth of the earth, they nevertheless in no way despise it. For they know that if it be cast into the fire, and come between the hands of the hammerers, it will at once lay aside its accidental blackness together with the rust and the filth, and without delay will receive the natural brightness of gold with a flaming splendor. Who then are they who seek gold, if not they who love the wisdom and the spiritual understanding of the Holy Scriptures? And what are the golden mountains, if not 561Dthe holy doctors, that is, the prophets and the apostles? Of whom it is written: « I have lifted up my eyes to the mountains, whence help shall come to me (Psal. CXX). » What then does the mass of gold covered with rust and filth signify, if not the writing of the prophets, which is exceedingly obscure, and wrapped up in divers enigmas and figures? Which it is needful that we should boil down in the fire of the Holy Spirit so long, until, the dross of the killing letter being cast away, we may come to wisdom and to spiritual understanding. As therefore gold, although outwardly discolored with rust and filth, is by no means scorned, 562Cbecause its inward gleam and brightness is considered, so the writing of the prophets, although it be hedged about with the hard rind of the outward letter, is nevertheless in no way to be scorned, because within there lies hidden a precious and spiritual understanding. That therefore what we say may now be more clearly made plain, let us weigh what it is that the Bride, that is, holy Church, says to the maidens: I am black, she says, but comely, O daughters of Jerusalem — black indeed outwardly, but comely inwardly; as if she should say: I am gold darkened by the blackness of an accidental rust, but if fire be applied and I be burned through, I shall appear the brightest gold, that is, refined gold. For thus says the Scripture: « As gold is tried by fire, so tribulation of temptations tries the just (Prov. XVII). » [Vulg. Prov. 17:3 Sicut igne probatur argentum et aurum camino, ita corda probat Dominus, "as silver is tried by fire and gold in the furnace, so the Lord trieth the hearts"] And Jerusalem is interpreted the vision of peace, and he calls the daughters of 562DJerusalem the faithful souls, whom above he had called maidens. Whose study and highest intent it is that they may be able to please God alone, who according to the apostle Paul (Ephes. II) is our peace, and hath made both one. Let holy Church then say: O you, daughters of Jerusalem, that is, O you, faithful souls, who continually desire to see God, who is our peace, be not troubled, do not wonder, that I am black in the passion of my martyrs, but rejoice and be glad, that I am comely in the receiving of the eternal rewards. Black indeed I am, 563Aand I mourn on account of the present persecutions which heretics and evil Christians inflict upon me, but I am comely and I rejoice, knowing that through many tribulations I must enter into the kingdom of God. Now she subjoins a comparison of her blackness and of her comeliness, saying: As the tents of Cedar I am black, and as the skin of Solomon I am comely. [Vulg. Cant. 1:4 sicut pelles Salomonis, plural, "the skins (curtains) of Solomon"; the singular is what the whole exposition below rests upon] Cedar was a son of Ismael the son of Abraham, and Cedar is interpreted darkness. And so it is related of him that he endured the greatest persecution from his neighbors, and, ever shifting his tents, fled from place to place. Which assuredly agrees well with holy Church, which, set amid the darkness of straits and the persecutions of adversaries, says: « We have not here a lasting city, but we seek one to come (Hebr. XIII). » Of these 563Badversaries indeed the same Church complains, saying in the Psalms that « they have hated me without cause (Psal. XXIV; Joan. XV). » But concerning the skin of Solomon I do not remember that I have found anything written, save in this place only, neither in the New nor in the Old Testament. For this reason a great question is raised for us. But it is to be known that not without a mighty mystery is that found in the divine letters which with so great difficulty and obscurity is scarcely understood. But unless we shall have understood the whole from the part, we shall assuredly grasp no spiritual understanding in this place. For it is the custom of Holy Scripture sometimes to take the whole from the part, sometimes the part from the whole. Which assuredly in this place it is necessary to do. For we read in the book of Kings 563C328 that in his own time the most mighty king Solomon, when all Israel had been gathered together, set out with great array to Gabaon, because there was the greater high place (III Reg. III), and the tabernacle which the Lord had commanded to be made by the hand of Moses his servant, when the children of Israel went out of the land of Egypt. Which tabernacle was made after the pattern and likeness of a certain spiritual and heavenly tabernacle, which was shown in the mount to Moses the servant of God. Moreover thus says the Lord to Moses: « See, he says, that thou make the tabernacle according to the form of that tabernacle which thou sawest in the mount (Exod. XXV). » [Vulg. Exod. 25:40 inspice et fac secundum exemplar quod tibi in monte monstratum est, "look and make it according to the pattern that was shown thee in the mount"] Therefore the earthly tabernacle was made after the pattern of the heavenly tabernacle. And that the tabernacle which was set up by Moses was of skins, 563DDavid testifies, who said to Nathan the prophet: « Seest thou that I dwell in a house of cedar, and the ark of God is in the midst of skins (I Par. XVII), » that is, in a tabernacle set up of skins. [Vulg. I Par. 17:1 arca foederis Domini sub pellibus est, "the ark of the covenant of the Lord is under skins"] Whom then did Solomon, who is interpreted the peaceable one, designate other than Christ, of whom Paul says that through his own blood he made peace as to the things that are in heaven and the things that are on earth. And what does it signify that the same Solomon went with all Israel to Gabaon, if not this, that Christ ascended into heaven with those whom he drew out from the lower regions. Whence David says: « Christ ascending on high, led captivity captive (Psal. LXVII). » [Vulg. Psal. 67:19 ascendisti in altum, cepisti captivitatem, "thou hast ascended on high, thou hast taken captivity"; the wording here follows Ephes. 4:8, which does not name Christ] And what is that greater high place, if not the knowledge of the highest and incomprehensible Trinity? For we cannot climb a greater high place than that we should 564Aknow, as it is from eternity, that the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit are one and true God. And of what thing did that tabernacle bear the figure, which was built of the skins of dead animals, if not of the present Church, which was founded and established by our holy Fathers, that is, the prophets and the apostles? Therefore by the skin of Solomon we ought spiritually to take the whole tabernacle which was built of skins, that is, the whole from the part. Otherwise in this sentence we shall find no spiritual sense at all. Let the Bride then say, that is, holy Church: I am black as the tents of Cedar, that is, just as Cedar once suffered persecution from his neighbors, so I daily suffer harassment from heretics and false Christians, 564Bbut I am comely as the skin of Solomon, that is, as the tabernacle of Solomon built of skins, which, on account of the manifold ornaments of gold and of silver which it contained within, shone with an inestimable manner and an incomparable brightness. Do not consider me that I am swarthy, that is, do not despise me on account of the innumerable oppressions which I endure from adversaries, because for those very things I look for heavenly glory from the eternal judge in the day of judgment. Because the sun hath discolored me, for that reason, she says, you ought not to hold me in contempt if amid tribulations I appear swarthy, that is, black, because the sun hath discolored me, that is, because for the sake of Christ, who is the sun of justice, I gladly embrace and suffer these things; knowing most certainly that the humbler and the more abject I shall have been in this world for love of him, 564Cthe more glorious and the more acceptable I shall be in eternal happiness before his majesty. The sons of my mother have fought against me: where a sick man suffers the greater pain, there he is the more often compelled to lay his hand. Which holy Church seems to do in these things, who complains of heretics and false Christians by repeating, saying: The sons of my mother have fought against me. The mother of holy Church is the grace of the Holy Spirit, by which heretics and false Christians were regenerated in holy baptism, who fight against that same Church with words and with deeds and with depraved teachings. Of whom John in his Epistles says: « They went out from us, but they were not of us. For if they were of us, they would surely have remained with us (I Joan. II). » Hence 564DPaul says: « I know that after my departure grievous wolves will enter in among you, who will not spare you (Act. XX). » [Vulg. Act. 20:29 lupi rapaces … non parcentes gregi, "ravening wolves … not sparing the flock"] They have set me keeper in the vineyards. Holy Church here speaks in the person of the holy apostles, of whom it stands clearer than light that they were made planters and keepers of the vineyards, that is, of the Churches gathered from the Gentiles. My vineyard I have not kept, that is, the Synagogue of which we were born we forsook, on account of her unbelief and the blindness of her heart, and we made our passage over to the Gentiles, who received the word of God with joy. Whence Paul says to the Jews: « To you it behooved to speak the word of God first, but because you have thrust it away, and have judged yourselves unworthy of eternal life, behold we turn to the Gentiles (Act. XIII). » Therefore the apostles were set as it were keepers in 565Athe vineyards of the Gentiles, when it was said to them: « Go, teach all nations (Matth. XXVIII). But then they did not keep their own vineyard, when the faithless Jews drove them out of the Synagogue, threatening them that they should speak to no one in the name of Jesus (Act. IV).

RECAPITULATIO A SUPERIORIBUS, QUALITER ISTA SENTENTIA, SCILICET, « NIGRA SUM, SED FORMOSA, » REFERATUR AD PERSONAM CHRISTI.

Ista sententia, scilicet Nigra sum, sed formosa, potest rationabiliter a bono intellectu referri ad carnem Christi in cruce 329 pendentis, et quasi dicentis: O vos, filiae Jerusalem, id est, o vos, fideles animae, nigra quidem sum propter imminentis passionis dolorem et districtissimam 565Bmortem, sed formosa et decora post paululum futura sum, propter gloriosam resurrectionem et admirabilem ascensionem. Unde Paulus dicit: « Videmus Jesum propter passionem mortis gloria et honore coronatum (Hebr. II). » Sicut tabernacula Cedar, id est quemadmodum quondam Cedar vicini sui persecuti sunt, ita et Judaei amici mei et proximi mei adversum me steterunt, et vocem suam clamantes: « Crucifige illum (Luc. XXIII; Joan. XIX), » contra me levaverunt. Quod etiam Jeremias propheta longe ante prophetaverat in persona Christi ita dicens: « Facta est mihi haereditas mea quasi leo in silva, dedit contra me vocem suam (Jer. XII). » De leone refertur quia silvam vociferando circuit 565Cin qua commoratur, et hoc ideo facit, ne animalibus intra silvam manentibus egrediendi facultas pateat, ut postea facilius animali, cui voluerit, mortem inferat. Quod satis decenter et convenienter valet Judaico populo aptari, qui quasi leo totam silvam saeculi hujus circuiens dicit: « Videtis quia nihil proficimus, ecce totus mundus post ipsum vadit. Si dimittimus eum sic, omnes credent in eum (Joan. XII). » Qui etiam Judaicus populus contra Christum vocem suam dedit, quando ad Pilatum clamavit: « Si hunc dimittis, non es amicus Caesaris (Joan. XIX), » et quare hoc diceret illico subjunxit: « Nos legem habemus, et secundum legem debet mori. (ibid.). » Per Cedar ergo qui tenebrae interpretatur, tenebras mortis; per pellem vero Salomonis debemus 565Dintelligere gloriam resurrectionis. Dicat ergo caro Christi pendens in cruce: Nigra sum sicut tabernacula Cedar, sed formosa sum sicut pellis Salomonis, id est tenebras mortis possunt mihi videri inferre, sed gloriam resurrectionis non valent auferre. Nolite me considerare quod fusca sum, quia decoloravit me sol, hoc est: Nolite me aspicere in ea calamitate et miseria qua modo pro vobis pendeo in cruce, sed aspicite me jam resurrectione clarificatam, et ad judicium in mea majestate venientem. Quia decoloravit me sol, id est quia sol justitiae, qui in me inhabitat, et per mortem sic me decolorari voluit, et post resurrectionem incomparabili gloria me clarificabit. Unde idem Filius dicit in Evangelio: « Clarifica me, Pater, apud temetipsum claritate quam 566Ahabui antequam mundus fieret (Joan. XVII). » Filii matris meae pugnaverunt contra me. Mater Christi, secundum carnem, Synagoga fuit, quia et ipsa per patriarchas et reges, et per beatam Mariam originem duxit. Qualiter autem Judaei qui sunt matris ejus, scilicet Synagogae filii, contra ipsum in passione pugnaverunt, Psalmista testatur, dicens: « Filii hominum dentes eorum arma et sagittae, et lingua eorum gladius acutus (Psal. LVI). » Posuerunt me custodem in vineis, vineam meam non custodivi. Manifestum est quia perfidi Judaei et viventem et mortuum persecuti sunt Christum, in tantum ut etiam vana sollicitudine illius custodirent sepulcrum, et quanto magis illum a se per incrudelitatem ejecerunt, tanto magis illum ad gentes, quae erant crediturae 566Btransmiserunt. « Vinea autem Domini exercituum, sicut dicit Isaias, domus Israel est (Isa.
