Respondeat ergo justum est Christus sanctae Ecclesiae, et dicat: Tu quaeris a me ut indicem tibi ubi pascam et accubem, et ego tibi respondebo, quia in meridie pasco 578Bet accubo, hoc est in Patre meo, qui est verax meridies, maneo sicut mansi ab aeterno.

CONCLUSION OF THE SENTENCE.

Therefore it stands for certain, and no man doubts thereof, that if the Father is the midday, and if the Son abides in the Father, without doubt he abides in the midday. Let Christ therefore answer — it is just — to holy Church, and say: Thou seekest of me that I show thee where I feed and lie down, and I will answer thee that at midday I feed 578Band lie down, that is, in my Father, who is the true midday, I abide as I have abode from eternity.

RECAPITULATIO A SUPERIORIBUS, QUALITER AD PERSONAM PATRIS REFERRI DEBEAT ISTA SENTENTIA, SCILICET, « INDICA MIHI, » ETC.

Qui aeternos montes de superioribus rigat suis, qui terram inferius jacentem de fructu operum suorum satiat et inebriat (Psal. X), ipse potens est nobis dare spiritum spiritualis sapientiae, quo repleti valeamus intelligere occulta sacramenta in Scripturis divinae legis quae desideramus. Sicut enim quondam Moyses egregius propheta (Exod. XIX) prius montem ascendit, et postea cum Domino familiare colloquium de arcanis rebus habuit, sic necesse est ut sancta Ecclesia modo faciat. Quasi enim montem magnum et 578Cexcelsum conscendit, quando transitoria cuncta despiciens et seipsam transcendens, ad collocutionem Sponsi sui, id est Filii Dei accessit, et ab eo quaesivit dicens: Indica mihi, quem diligit anima mea, ubi pascas, ubi cubes, etc. Ascenso igitur altissimo monte, hoc est postquam perfruita est Filii Dei Patris dulcissima allocutione, necesse est ut ad ipsum Deum Patrem humiliter accedat, et ab eo supplici devotione ubi pascat, ubi cubet requirat. Ad exordium ergo nostrae orationis redeamus, et quid sint aeterni montes, vel quae sint supernae rorationes, quaeve sit terra inferius jacens vel satietas et ebrietas fructuum Dei subtiliter requiramus. Aeternos montes illos arbitror debere intelligi, de quibus Psalmista ad Dominum 578Ddicit: « Illuminans tu mirabiliter a montibus aeternis (Psal. LXXV). » Montes ergo aeterni, patriarchae sunt et prophetae et apostoli, a quibus nos sumus mirabiliter illuminati, quia per exempla patriarcharum, per eloquia prophetarum, per doctrinam apostolorum Christianae religionis fidem agnovimus, qui prius remoti longe a cognitione Dei in tenebris et in umbra mortis sedebamus, unde Isaias propheta ait: « Populus gentium qui ambulabat in tenebris, vidit lucem magnam, habitantibus in regione umbrae mortis, lux orta est eis (Isai. IX). » Vere lux magna et admirabilis populis gentium est orta, quando fides Christi per praedicationem apostolorum in cordibus eorum est plantata et radicata, et sicut coeperunt in se Christum habere habitatorem, qui prius in cordis 579Ahospitio recipiebant diabolum pessimum seductorem, et animarum suarum crudelissimum interfectorem. Unde et Paulus dicit: « Per fidem Christum habitare in cordibus nostris (Ephes. III). » Denique et beatus Augustinus: « Fides, inquit, in corde tuo, Christus est in corde tuo. » Luce ergo clarius constat quia, nisi lumen fidei in cordibus nostris resplendeat, in tenebris infidelitatis sedebimus, et Christum habitatorem nullatenus habebimus. De his montibus Psalmista dicit: « Montes excelsi cervis, petra refugium herinaciis (Psal. CIII). » Quod petra Christum significet Paulus apostolus edocet, dicens: « Petra autem erat Christus (I Cor. X). » Montes vero excelsi ipsi sunt, qui superius 337 apellati sunt montes sancti, videlicet prophetae et 579Bapostoli. Consuetudo cervorum talis esse dicitur, ut ad montes fugiant quando a venatoribus fugantur. Omnibus liquet quod herinacii aculeis et spinis sunt pleni. Per haec duo animalia duo genera hominum peccatorum designantur, quae in gremio sanctae Ecclesiae continentur. Unum genus est, quod pro peccatis suis poenitentiam agit et plorat, et de actibus propriis diffidens, per suffragia et orationes sanctorum ad misericordiam Dei pervenire se sperat. Quod genus hominum merito cervis comparatur, qui ad montes fugiunt ut a morte liberentur. Aliud vero genus hominum est quod occultis et publicis peccatis subjacet, et quanto amplius desperans diversis criminibus se ipsum subdit, tanto longius a Deo recedit; dumque abjecto Dei timore, coram 579Chominibus peccare non formidat, quasi in morem herinacii plenum spinis et aculeis se esse manifestat. Sane aliquando hoc genus hominum in articulo mortis compungitur, et considerata peccatorum suorum mole in interiori homine suo valde cruciatur, dumque ad poenas aeterni cruciatus iturum se non dubitat, divina aspiratione tactus ad Christum, qui est vera petra, redire festinat, qui sinum clementiae, et fontem pietatis et misericordiae mortalibus aperiens, per prophetam suum clamat: « Nolo mortem peccatoris, sed ut convertatur et vivat (Ezech. XVIII). » Montes igitur excelsi refugium sunt cervis, et petra refugium est herinaciis, quia sicut peccatores poenitentes et bene operantes, per suffragia 579Det orationes salvantur, sic miseri, desperati et peccatorum suorum spinis circumdati, a Christo recipiuntur, si tamen ad extremam vitae suae horam ad ipsum convertantur. Sic enim divina Scriptura ait: « Peccator quacunque hora ingemuerit, salvus erit (ibid.). » Ecce ostendimus Deo adjuvante quantum potuimus, quid sint aeterni montes; nunc restat ut demonstremus quid sint supernae rigationes. Moyses namque dicit: « Concrescat ut pluvia doctrina mea, et fluat ut ros eloquium meum (Deut. XXXII). Isaias quoque ait ad Dominum: « Quia ros lucis, ros tuus (Isa. XXVI). » Manifestum est ergo quod divinae Scripturae appellentur rigationes supernae, 580Aquibus rigati sunt prophetae et apostoli, et velut ore coelesti fecundati. De ista rigatione Paulus apostolus loquitur dicens: « Terra bibens desuper venientem imbrem, et fructificans opportunam, ab his, a quibus colitur, accipit benedictionem (Heb. VI). » De hac iterum roratione coelesti David loquitur dicens: « Fluminis impetus laetificat civitatem Dei (Psal. XLV). » Civitas vero Dei est corpus vel anima viri justi, quae plenius tunc laetificatur, quando fluentis divinorum eloquiorum delectabiliter irroratur. Hinc iterum David dicit: « Inebriabuntur ab ubertate domus tuae, et torrente voluptatis tuae potabis eos (Psal. XXXV). » Domus namque Dei est sancta Ecclesia, ubertas ejusdem domus est divina Scriptura, qua sobrie et religiose fidelis anima debriatur, 580Bquando omnia quae sunt hujus mundi, spirituali excessu sopita, obliviscitur, et soli Deo puritate mentis conjungitur. Torrens autem voluptatis Dei est abrenuntiatio hujus saeculi, quia sicut torrens omnia ante se posita obruit et praecipitat, sic qui perfecte saeculo renuntiat, prospera et adversa despicit, carnem suam cum vitiis et concupiscentiis libenter crucifigit, nihil est quod propositum mentis ejus retardare valeat, donec ad ipsum pervenire possit, quem super omnia et supra seipsum diligit. Cum ergo constet quia abrenuntiatio hujus mundi sit voluptas magna et delectatio Dei, manifestum est quod qui nec sibi ipsis, nec mundi desideriis vel concupiscentiis renuntiant, nullam in cordibus suis acceptabilem delectationem prorsus Deo praeparant. 580CEant ergo impii et affluant divitiis hujus saeculi, et sciant procul dubio quia quanto amplius huic mundo et suae carni male vivendo serviunt, tanto longius a Deo recedunt, et diabolo crudeli dominatori se conjungunt. Sic enim ait Joannes apostolus: « Filioli, nolite diligere mundum, neque ea quae in mundo sunt, quia si quis dilexerit mundum, non est charitas Patris in eo (I Joan. II). » Ex quibus verbis liquido apparet quia dilectio hujus mundi ab humanis cordibus charitatem Dei removet, et quoniam Deus charitas esse dicitur, remota charitate a corde nostro, sine dubio et Deus removetur.

Igitur ostensis aeternis montibus, propalatis supernis 580Drigationibus, mens nostra Spiritus sancti rore afflata jam dilucidare conabitur quae sit illa felix et beata terra quae de fructu operum Dei satiatur. Sane de terra locuturi, non longe a nobis recedamus, quia nos ipsi terra sumus. Sic enim dicit Dominus ad Adam: « Terra es, et in terram ibis (Gen. III). » Caveamus denique ne forte, quod absit! nos simus illa terra quae a Domino serpenti in cibum est concessa. Liber enim Genesis (ibid.) dicit quia hac maledictione Deus diabolum in serpente percussit, ut videlicet terram comedat omnibus diebus vitae suae pro eo quod hominem sua fraude decepit, qui ab ipso Deo creatus erat rectae 581Aet simplicis naturae. Quod etiam Scriptura testatur dicens: Deus fecit hominem simplicem et rectum (Eccle. VII); simplicem, ut malum nunquam faceret, rectum autem, ut ab ipso Deo nunquam recederet. Verum quia primus parens noster spe deductus inani, Deo et diabolo se posse obedire putavit, pro simplicitate duplicitatem incurrit, quia rectitudinem mentis quam a Deo acceperat, et postquam ipsi soli placere 338 debuerat, ad cognitionem mali, blanditiis mulieris delinitus intorsit, juxta Dei dispensationem actum est, ut statum rectitudinis aeternae amitteret, et quoniam cum stare posset stare noluit, modo vero cum stare vellet stare non possit. Sed jam nunc ad terram redeamus de qua paulo ante loqui coeperamus. Terra quae data est 581Bdiabolo in cibum, Judaei erant qui Dominum Jesum interficere cupiebant. Quibus et ipse Dominus ait: « Vos ex patre diabolo estis, et desideria patris vestri perficere vultis. Ille homicida ab initio erat, in veritate non stetit (Joan. VIII); » ac si diceret: Vos terra estis diaboli, idcirco de fructu operum ejus desideratis satiari, et quia ille homicida comprobatur esse, et in veritate nullatenus stetisse, vos patrem vestrum sequentes me vultis interficere, et in me qui sum veritas, nec vultis stare, nec credere. Nos ergo simus illa terra quae fructificans a Domino benedictionem percepit, sicut David dicit: « Dominus dabit benignitatem, et terra nostra dabit fructum suum (Psal. LXXXIV). » Dat igitur Dominus nostrae terrae benignitatem, quando cordi 581Cnostro inspirat sui amoris suavitatem, ut pro ipso dura et aspera delectabiliter sustineamus, et ea quae animam in mortem resolvunt vitia carnis humanae, funditus a nobis abjiciamus. Dat, inquam, suam benignitatem nobis Dominus quando mentibus nostris intimat cognitionem sui ipsius, qua cognitione mentes nostrae quasi spiritali potu debriatae gaudia hujus mundi pro nihilo ducunt, quia ad ipsum cujus cognitio est vita aeterna, spreta saeculi fallaci gloria pervenire contendunt. Unde idem Dominus in Evangelio ait. « Haec est vita aeterna ut cognoscant te, et quem misisti Filium tuum unum et verum Deum esse (Joan. XVII). » Terra autem nostra fructum suum dat, quando mens nostra in superna contemplatione 581Drapta ad Deum suspirat, et cum ipso obliviscens suae fragilitatis unus spiritus effici desiderat, quem sincero charitatis amore prae omnibus creaturis ut dignum est amat. Terra, inquam, nostra tunc fructum suum dabit, quando corpus nostrum, per bona opera quae modo agit post hujus vitae fragilis terminum multis tentationibus probatum Deo reddet spiritum suum, dicens cum Psalmista: « In manus tuas commendo spiritum meum (Psal. XXX). » Cujus videlicet carnis fructus felici gaudio tunc duplicabitur, quando ipsa caro per resurrectionis gloriam clarificata una cum suo spiritu aeterna beatitudine donabitur; ut, sicut in hoc saeculo ambo sustinuerunt pro Deo poenas et tribulationes, sic post diem judicii pari gaudio recipiant 582Aillius summae et incomparabilis gloriae promissas a Deo mansiones. De quibus idem Dominus discipulis suis ait: « In domo Patris mei multae mansiones sunt (Joan. XIV). » Igitur quoniam intentio mentis nostrae prout vires ei suffragantur, et laboravit ostendere quae sit terra quae de fructu operum Dei satiatur; nunc superest ut de ipsis operibus Dei, et fructu operum ipsius ad laudem et gloriam nominis ejus loquatur. Opera autem Dei generaliter sive universaliter omnes creaturae sunt, quae ab ipso Deo sapientissima ordinatione creatae sunt. De qua admirabili creatione sic protestatur auctoritas divinae Scripturae: « Qui vivit in aeternum creavit omnia simul (Eccle. XVIII). » Unde David propheta ad Dominum dicit: « Omnia in sapientia fecisti (Psal. 582BCIII). » Vere etenim Deus omnia in sapientia sua fecit, quia in Filio suo, qui est ejus sapientia et verbum et virtus, cuncta ineffabiliter condidit. Quod idem propheta in Spiritu sancto sentiens alio loco declarat, dicens: « Et tu in principio, Domine, terram fundasti, et opera manuum tuarum sunt coeli (Psal. CI). » Filium autem Dei Propheta hic principium appellat, quem in inferiori psalmo sapientiam ejus nuncupaverat. Per coelum vero et per terram, quae his duobus elementis continentur, intelligere debemus; quoniam infra ambitum horum elementorum omnes creaturas quae a Deo creatae sunt, esse credimus. Quod Moysen eximium prophetarum in Spiritu sancto sensisse arbitrantur, qui de creatione mundi edisserens sic loquitur, dicens: « In principio 582Ccreavit Deus coelum et terram (Gen. I); » ac si dicat: In Filio suo, qui est principium omnis creaturae quam ipse condidit, coelum et terram, et ea quae his continentur creavit. Verumtamen inter caetera opera quae Deus omnipotens per Filium suum condidisse creditur, quoddam admirabile et speciale opus idem Filius fecisse legitur. De quo opere Isaias propheta divino spiritu tactus ita protestatur: « Opus ejus alienum fuit ab eo, ut faceret opus suum, prius fecit opus alienum (Isa. XXVIII). » Quae verba sancti prophetae quia valde obscura sunt, et quoniam in aliis prophetis facile reperiri non possunt, sollicita intentione a nobis debent pensari, eo quod a tanto propheta nequaquam prolata sunt 582Dsine pondere magni sacramenti. Habent enim ista verba intra se ingens mysterium ab exteriori cortice litterae quasi quodam velamine coopertum. Quae verba nisi enucleatius discutiantur, latens mysterium quod intus est nullatenus in lucem propalatur. Quid est ergo quod propheta dicit quia opus Filii Dei alienum ab ipso Deo fuit? Quid enim est quod ait ut faceret opus suum prius fecit alienum? Quaeramus ergo aliquod exemplum, quo adducto et discusso lucidius ostendamus, latens sacramentum quod in hac sententia pandere desideramus. Quidam dominus ad quemdam servum suum loquitur, dicens (Matth. XXI): Vade prius operare in vinea mea, et post haec operaberis 339 in vinea tua, et scias pro certo quod nullatenus in vinea tua operaberis, nisi prius in 583Avinea mea operatus fueris. Audiens igitur ille, operatur domini sui vineam, et post haec facit opus in propriam vineam suam, sicque prius facit opus alienum, ut postea faciat opus suum. Sic nimirum, sic Dominus Jesus Christus egit. Prius fecit opus alienum ut postea faceret opus suum. Nasci etenim, pati, crucifigi, mori et resurgere alienum opus fuit ab ejus divinitate. Ipse invisibilis, impassibilis, immortalis, utpote Deo Patri et Spiritui sancto coaeternus et consubstantialis. Opus namque proprium Filii Dei redemptio exstitit generis humani. Sed nisi prius nasceretur, pateretur, moreretur, et resurgeret, nullo modo genus humanum libero arbitrio suo perditum a morte redimeret. Fecit ergo Filius Dei opus alienum, ut postea faceret opus 583Bsuum, quia prius sustinuit mortem quae non conveniebat ejus divinitati, ut postea genus humanum redimeret, quod congruebat ejus divinitati. Ecce ostendimus, non tamen ut dignum est, quae sunt opera Patris et Filii generalia, vel quae sunt Filii tantummodo specialia, sicuti est mortem pati, quod nec ejusdem Filii, nec ipsius Patris congruit divinitati. Mors quippe humana ad humanitatem Christi solummodo respicit, divinitas autem Patris, et ejusdem Christi et Spiritus sancti sine experientia mortis, vel alicujus passionis prorsus permanet et permansit. Demonstremus ergo quid sit fructus operum Dei quo fructu nos, qui terra sumus, debemus satiari. Fructum namque operum Dei hunc esse aestimamus, si consideratis operibus ipsius 583CDomini qui omnia fecit, haec laudemus et glorificemus. Fructum, inquam, operum Dei hunc esse sciamus, ut ex consideratione creaturarum, ipsi Creatori gratias et laudes ut dignum est referamus. Quippe qui ex nulla existente materia omnia quae facta sunt, mirabiliter condidit, et primum hominem ineffabili potentia de limo terrae creavit, et ei insensibile spiraculum vitae inspiravit. Praeterea, quod majoris gloriae et excellentioris dignitatis est, eidem homini prae caeteris creaturis imaginem et similitudinem suam donare dignatus est. De nulla enim creatura nisi de solo homine testatur divina Scriptura (Gen. I), quod ad imaginem et similitudinem Dei sit formata vel creata. Age ergo, o homo, 583Dage, inquam, gratias clementiae et bonitati Creatoris tui. Noli, quaeso, noli esse ingratus collatis tibi ab ipso beneficiis: responde quantum potes visceribus ejus charitatis. Cui juxta possibilitatem tuam tali conditione, si vis, potes respondere, si ipsius mandatis quae tibi ob salutem tuam tradidit, volueris obedire, et si te immunem ab omni peccato custodieris, 584Aet in ejus servitio usque ad ultimum vitae tuae terminum perseveraris. Magnae ergo auctoritatis et dignitatis es, o homo! Deus omnipotens ad imaginem suam te creavit, Filius ejus Dominus Jesus Christus sanguine suo redemit, a Spiritu sancto clementer illuminatus es, a septiformi gratia ejusdem Spiritus sancti ad cognitionem sanctae Trinitatis imbutus es.

Imaginem Dei in te repraesentas, quandiu non peccas, si vero aliquando peccato consentis, recedente imagine Dei a te, ipsi diabolo assimilaris qui peccando et superbiendo a Deo recessit, et seipsum, qui erat signaculum similitudinis gloriae Dei, sicut Ezechiel propheta dicit (cap. XXVIII), irrecuperabiliter damnavit et perdidit. Superius diximus 584Bquod sicut olim Moyses prius montem Sina ascendit, et sic demum de arcanis et mysticis rebus cum Domino soliloquium habuit: sic sancta Ecclesia fecerat. Quasi enim montem magnum excelsumque ascendit, quando ad soliloquium Sponsi sui, qui est « speciosus forma prae filiis hominum (Psal. XLIV), » accessit, et ab eo requisivit, dicens: Indica mihi, quem diligit anima mea, ubi pascas, ubi cubes, etc. Accedat ergo ad soliloquium Patris omnipotentis, et remotis a se sordibus totius iniquitatis ab ipso simili modo charitatis affectum requirat, ubi ipse cubet, et ubi ipse pascat. Igitur antequam Deus Pater omnipotens Sponsae Filii sui colloquatur, vel priusquam ipsa Sponsa ab eodem Patre Sponsi sui audire mereatur: Intellige, filia, et vide, et inclina 584Caurem tuam, et obliviscere populum tuum, et domum patris tui, et tunc ubi pasco vel cubo, indicabo tibi; antequam istud fiat, necesse est ut mens nostra pervigili intentione requirat ubi in divinis eloquiis Filius Dei mons appelletur, ne forte nostra locutio ab aliquo minus erudito de falsitate redarguatur, pro eo praecipue quod Christum montem appellavimus, et ei montem Sina per similitudinem aequiparavimus. Multis igitur auctoritatibus praeter missis, quae hoc quod dicimus protestantur, sola sufficiens et idonea auctoritas ut dignum est in medium proferatur, cui gratiae spiritualis filii et divinae legis amici aures spirituales gratanter praebeant, et inimici veritatis, vel minus sapientes non habeant 584Dquid contradicant. Daniel propheta secretorum Dei conscius, memoria abundantiae suavitatis Spiritus sancti refertur admirabile somnium illius magnae et terribilis visionis, quam rex Babylonis viderat, exponens, inter caetera quae sibi proloquitur, Christum montem interpretatur dicens: Videras, etc.

RECAPITULATION FROM THE FOREGOING, IN WHAT MANNER THIS SENTENCE OUGHT TO BE REFERRED TO THE PERSON OF THE FATHER, NAMELY, « SHOW ME, » ETC.

He who watereth the eternal mountains from his upper places, who satisfieth and maketh drunk the earth lying beneath from the fruit of his works (Psal. X), he himself is able to give us the spirit of spiritual wisdom, filled with which we may be strong to understand those hidden mysteries in the Scriptures of the divine law which we desire. For as once Moses, that excellent prophet (Exod. XIX), first ascended the mountain, and afterwards had familiar converse with the Lord concerning secret things, so it is needful that holy Church do now. For she climbs as it were a great and 578Clofty mountain when, despising all transitory things and transcending her very self, she came to the colloquy of her Bridegroom, that is, of the Son of God, and sought of him, saying: Show me, thou whom my soul loveth, where thou feedest, where thou liest down, etc. The highest mountain therefore being ascended — that is, after she has fully enjoyed the most sweet address of the Son of God the Father — it is needful that she come humbly to God the Father himself, and seek of him with suppliant devotion where he feeds, where he lies down. Let us return therefore to the beginning of our discourse, and let us subtly inquire what the eternal mountains are, or what the bedewings from above are, or what is the earth lying beneath, or the satiety and drunkenness of the fruits of God. Those, I judge, ought to be understood as the eternal mountains of which the Psalmist says to the Lord: 578D« Thou enlightening wondrously from the eternal mountains (Psal. LXXV). » The eternal mountains therefore are the patriarchs and the prophets and the apostles, by whom we have been wondrously enlightened, because through the examples of the patriarchs, through the utterances of the prophets, through the doctrine of the apostles we came to know the faith of the Christian religion — we who before, removed far from the knowledge of God, were sitting in darkness and in the shadow of death; whence Isaiah the prophet says: « The people of the gentiles that walked in darkness hath seen a great light; to them that dwelt in the region of the shadow of death, a light is risen (Isai. IX). » [Vulg. Isa. IX, 2, Populus qui ambulabat in tenebris, without gentium] Truly a great and wondrous light has risen upon the peoples of the gentiles, when the faith of Christ was planted and rooted in their hearts through the preaching of the apostles, and as they began to have Christ as an indweller within them, who before were receiving into the 579Alodging of the heart the devil, that most wicked seducer and most cruel slayer of their own souls. Whence also Paul says: « That Christ dwell by faith in our hearts (Ephes. III). » [Vulg. Ephes. III, 17, in cordibus vestris, "in your hearts"] And lastly blessed Augustine: « Faith, he says, in thy heart, Christ is in thy heart. » It stands therefore clearer than light that, unless the light of faith shine back in our hearts, we shall sit in the darkness of unbelief, and shall in no wise have Christ as an indweller. Of these mountains the Psalmist says: « The high mountains for the harts, the rock a refuge for the hedgehogs (Psal. CIII). » That the rock signifies Christ, Paul the apostle teaches, saying: « And the rock was Christ (I Cor. X). » And the high mountains are those same who above 337 were called the holy mountains, namely the prophets and 579Bapostles. The custom of harts is said to be such, that they flee to the mountains when they are put to flight by hunters. It is plain to all that hedgehogs are full of prickles and thorns. By these two animals are designated two kinds of sinful men, which are contained in the bosom of holy Church. One kind is that which does penance and weeps for its sins, and, distrusting its own acts, hopes to come to the mercy of God through the suffrages and prayers of the saints. Which kind of men is rightly compared to the harts, which flee to the mountains that they may be delivered from death. But the other kind of men is that which lies under hidden and public sins, and the more, despairing, it subjects itself to divers crimes, by so much the further does it withdraw from God; and while, the fear of God being cast off, it dreads not to sin before 579Cmen, it shows itself to be, after the manner of the hedgehog as it were, full of thorns and prickles. Yet sometimes this kind of men is pricked with compunction in the article of death, and, the mass of its sins being considered, is greatly tormented in its inner man; and while it doubts not that it is going to the pains of eternal torment, touched by a divine inbreathing it hastens to return to Christ, who is the true rock — who, opening to mortals the bosom of clemency and the fountain of tenderness and mercy, cries through his prophet: « I will not the death of the sinner, but that he be converted and live (Ezech. XVIII). » [Vulg. Ezech. XXXIII, 11 nolo mortem impii, "the death of the wicked"; Ezech. XVIII, 23, which Migne cites, reads numquid voluntatis meae est mors impii] The high mountains therefore are a refuge for the harts, and the rock is a refuge for the hedgehogs, because as penitent sinners who work well are saved through suffrages 579Dand prayers, so the wretched, the despairing, and those hedged about with the thorns of their own sins are received by Christ, if only at the last hour of their life they be turned to him. For thus Holy Scripture says: « In whatsoever hour the sinner shall have groaned, he shall be saved (ibid.). » [Vulg. Ezech. has no such sentence; cf. Ezech. XXXIII, 12, impietas impii non nocebit ei, in quacumque die conversus fuerit ab impietate sua] Behold, God helping, we have shown as much as we could what the eternal mountains are; now it remains that we demonstrate what the waterings from above are. For Moses says: « Let my doctrine gather as the rain, and let my speech flow as the dew (Deut. XXXII). Isaiah also says to the Lord: « For thy dew is the dew of light (Isa. XXVI). » It is manifest therefore that the Holy Scriptures are called the waterings from above, 580Aby which the prophets and apostles were watered, and as it were made fruitful by a heavenly mouth. Of this watering Paul the apostle speaks, saying: « The earth, drinking in the rain that cometh from above, and bringing forth what is seasonable, receiveth blessing from those by whom it is tilled (Heb. VI). » [Vulg. Heb. VI, 7, Terra enim saepe venientem super se bibens imbrem, et generans herbam opportunam illis a quibus colitur, accipit benedictionem a Deo] Of this heavenly bedewing again David speaks, saying: « The stream of the river maketh the city of God joyful (Psal. XLV). » And the city of God is the body or the soul of a just man, which is then more fully made joyful, when it is delightfully bedewed by the streams of the divine utterances. Hence again David says: « They shall be inebriated with the plenty of thy house, and thou shalt give them to drink of the torrent of thy pleasure (Psal. XXXV). » For the house of God is holy Church, the plenty of that same house is Holy Scripture, by which the faithful soul is soberly and religiously made drunk, 580Bwhen, lulled by a spiritual excess, it forgets all the things that are of this world, and is joined to God alone by purity of mind. And the torrent of God's pleasure is the renunciation of this world, because as a torrent overwhelms and hurls down all things set before it, so he who perfectly renounces the world despises things prosperous and adverse, gladly crucifies his flesh with its vices and concupiscences; there is nothing that can hold back the purpose of his mind, until he may come to him whom he loves above all things and above his very self. Since therefore it stands firm that the renunciation of this world is a great pleasure and delight of God, it is manifest that they who renounce neither their own selves nor the desires or concupiscences of the world prepare in their hearts no acceptable delight at all for God. 580CLet the impious therefore go and abound in the riches of this world, and let them know beyond doubt that the more they serve this world and their own flesh by living wickedly, by so much the further do they withdraw from God, and join themselves to the devil, that cruel lord. For thus says John the apostle: « Little children, love not the world, nor the things that are in the world; for if any man shall have loved the world, the charity of the Father is not in him (I Joan. II). » [Vulg. I Joan. II, 15, Nolite diligere mundum, neque ea quae in mundo sunt. Si quis diligit mundum… — without Filioli, and with the present diligit] From which words it clearly appears that the love of this world removes the charity of God from human hearts; and since God is said to be charity, charity being removed from our heart, without doubt God also is removed.

Therefore, the eternal mountains being shown, the waterings from above 580Dbeing made plain, our mind, breathed upon by the dew of the Holy Spirit, will now endeavor to elucidate what that happy and blessed earth is which is satisfied from the fruit of God's works. Assuredly, being about to speak of earth, let us not withdraw far from ourselves, because we ourselves are earth. For thus the Lord says to Adam: « Earth thou art, and into earth thou shalt go (Gen. III). » [Vulg. Gen. III, 19, pulvis es, et in pulverem reverteris, "dust thou art, and into dust thou shalt return"] Let us beware lastly lest perchance — which God forbid! — we be that earth which was granted by the Lord to the serpent for food. For the book of Genesis (ibid.) says that God struck the devil in the serpent with this curse, namely that he should eat earth all the days of his life, for that he deceived by his guile the man who had been created by God himself of an upright 581Aand simple nature. Which Scripture also attests, saying: "God made man simple and upright (Eccle. VII)"; simple, that he should never do evil, and upright, that he should never withdraw from God himself. [Vulg. Eccle. VII, 30, quod fecerit Deus hominem rectum, without simplicem] But because our first parent, led on by an empty hope, thought that he could obey both God and the devil, he incurred duplicity in place of simplicity; and because the rectitude of mind which he had received from God, and after which he ought to have pleased him alone, 338 he twisted — being softened by the blandishments of the woman — toward the knowledge of evil, it was brought about according to God's dispensation that he lost the state of eternal rectitude, and since, when he could stand, he would not stand, now indeed, when he would stand, he cannot stand. But now let us return to the earth of which we had begun to speak a little before. The earth which was given 581Bto the devil for food was the Jews, who desired to kill the Lord Jesus. To whom the Lord himself also says: « You are of your father the devil, and the desires of your father you will to accomplish. He was a murderer from the beginning, in the truth he stood not (Joan. VIII); » as if he should say: You are the earth of the devil, therefore you desire to be satisfied from the fruit of his works; and because he is proved to be a murderer, and in no wise to have stood in the truth, you, following your father, will to kill me, and in me, who am the truth, you will neither to stand nor to believe. Let us then be that earth which, bearing fruit, has received blessing from the Lord, as David says: « The Lord will give kindness, and our earth shall yield her fruit (Psal. LXXXIV). » The Lord therefore gives kindness to our earth when he breathes into our 581Cheart the sweetness of his love, that for him we may delightfully bear things hard and harsh, and may utterly cast away from us those vices of human flesh which dissolve the soul into death. The Lord gives us, I say, his kindness when he makes known to our minds the knowledge of his own self; by which knowledge our minds, made drunk as it were with a spiritual draught, count the joys of this world as nothing, because, the deceitful glory of the world being despised, they strive to come to him whose knowledge is eternal life. Whence the same Lord says in the Gospel. « This is eternal life, that they may know thee, and him whom thou hast sent, thy Son, to be the one and true God (Joan. XVII). » [Vulg. Joan. XVII, 3, Haec est autem vita aeterna: ut cognoscant te solum Deum verum, et quem misisti Jesum Christum] And our earth yields her fruit when our mind, caught up in supernal contemplation, 581Dsighs after God, and, forgetting its own frailty, desires to be made one spirit with him whom, with the sincere love of charity, it loves above all creatures as is worthy. Our earth, I say, will then yield her fruit when our body, proved by many temptations through the good works which it now performs, shall after the end of this frail life render its spirit to God, saying with the Psalmist: « Into thy hands I commend my spirit (Psal. XXX). » The fruit of which flesh, namely, will then be doubled with a happy joy, when that same flesh, made bright by the glory of the resurrection, shall together with its spirit be endowed with eternal beatitude; so that, as in this world both endured pains and tribulations for God, so after the day of judgment they may with equal joy receive 582Athe mansions of that highest and incomparable glory promised by God. Of which the same Lord says to his disciples: « In my Father's house there are many mansions (Joan. XIV). » Therefore, since the intention of our mind, according as its powers support it, has also labored to show what that earth is which is satisfied from the fruit of God's works, it now remains that it speak of those works of God themselves, and of the fruit of his works, unto the praise and glory of his name. And the works of God, generally or universally, are all creatures, which were created by God himself with a most wise ordering. Of which wondrous creation the authority of Holy Scripture thus testifies: « He that liveth for ever created all things together (Eccle. XVIII). » Whence David the prophet says to the Lord: « Thou hast made all things in wisdom (Psal. 582BCIII). » For truly God made all things in his wisdom, because in his Son, who is his wisdom and word and power, he ineffably founded all things. Which the same prophet, perceiving in the Holy Spirit, declares in another place, saying: « And thou in the beginning, O Lord, didst found the earth, and the heavens are the works of thy hands (Psal. CI). » [Vulg. Psal. CI, 26, Initio tu, Domine, terram fundasti, without in principio] And here the Prophet calls the Son of God the beginning, whom in a lower psalm he had named his wisdom. And by heaven and by earth we ought to understand the things which are contained in these two elements; since we believe that all creatures which were created by God are within the compass of these elements. Which they judge that Moses, the choicest of the prophets, perceived in the Holy Spirit, who, discoursing of the creation of the world, thus speaks, saying: « In the beginning 582CGod created heaven and earth (Gen. I); » as if he should say: In his Son, who is the beginning of every creature which he himself founded, he created heaven and earth, and the things which are contained in them. Nevertheless, among the other works which God almighty is believed to have founded through his Son, the same Son is read to have done a certain wondrous and special work. Of which work Isaiah the prophet, touched by the divine spirit, thus testifies: « His work was alien from him; that he might do his own work, he first did an alien work (Isa. XXVIII). » [Vulg. Isa. XXVIII, 21, ut faciat opus suum, alienum opus ejus: ut operetur opus suum, peregrinum est opus ejus ab eo] Which words of the holy prophet, because they are exceedingly obscure, and since they cannot easily be found in the other prophets, ought to be weighed by us with anxious attention, in that they were by no means uttered by so great a prophet 582Dwithout the weight of a great mystery. For these words have within them a vast mystery, covered by the outer rind of the letter as by a kind of veil. Which words, unless they be more thoroughly discussed, the hidden mystery which is within is in no wise brought forth into the light. What then is it that the prophet says, that the work of the Son of God was alien from God himself? For what is it that he says, that in order to do his own work he first did an alien one? Let us therefore seek some example, by which, when it has been brought forward and discussed, we may more lucidly show the hidden mystery which in this sentence we desire to lay open. A certain lord speaks to a certain servant of his, saying (Matth. XXI): "Go first, work in my vineyard, and after this thou shalt work 339 in thine own vineyard; and know for certain that thou shalt in no wise work in thine own vineyard, unless thou shalt first have worked in 583Amy vineyard." Hearing this therefore, he works his lord's vineyard, and after this he does the work upon his own proper vineyard; and thus he first does an alien work, that afterwards he may do his own. So assuredly, so did the Lord Jesus Christ act. He first did an alien work, that afterwards he might do his own work. For to be born, to suffer, to be crucified, to die and to rise again was a work alien from his divinity. He himself invisible, impassible, immortal, being as he is coeternal and consubstantial with God the Father and the Holy Spirit. For the proper work of the Son of God was the redemption of the human race. But unless he were first born, and suffered, and died, and rose again, in no way would he redeem from death the human race, lost by its own free will. The Son of God therefore did an alien work, that afterwards he might do his 583Bown work, because he first endured the death which did not befit his divinity, that afterwards he might redeem the human race, which did befit his divinity. Behold, we have shown — yet not as is worthy — what are the general works of the Father and of the Son, or what are the works special to the Son only, such as to suffer death, which befits neither the divinity of that same Son nor of the Father himself. For human death regards the humanity of Christ only, but the divinity of the Father, and of that same Christ and of the Holy Spirit, remains and has remained utterly without experience of death or of any suffering. Let us then demonstrate what the fruit of God's works is, with which fruit we, who are earth, ought to be satisfied. For we reckon this to be the fruit of God's works: that, the works of the Lord himself 583Cwho made all things being considered, we should praise and glorify them. Let us know, I say, this to be the fruit of God's works: that from the consideration of creatures we should render thanks and praises, as is worthy, to the Creator himself. Who indeed out of no existing matter wondrously founded all things that were made, and by ineffable power created the first man from the slime of the earth, and breathed into him the insensible breath of life. Moreover — which is of greater glory and of more excellent dignity — he deigned to give to that same man, above the other creatures, his own image and likeness. For of no creature save of man alone does Holy Scripture bear witness (Gen. I) that it was formed or created to the image and likeness of God. Render therefore, O man, 583Drender, I say, thanks to the clemency and goodness of thy Creator. Be not, I beseech thee, be not ungrateful for the benefits conferred upon thee by him: answer, as much as thou canst, the bowels of his charity. To whom, according to thy possibility, thou canst answer on this condition, if thou wilt: if thou art willing to obey his commandments, which he delivered to thee for thy salvation, and if thou keep thyself free from all sin, 584Aand persevere in his service unto the last term of thy life. Of great authority therefore and of great dignity art thou, O man! God almighty created thee to his own image; his Son the Lord Jesus Christ redeemed [thee] with his own blood; by the Holy Spirit thou hast been mercifully enlightened; by the sevenfold grace of that same Holy Spirit thou hast been imbued unto the knowledge of the holy Trinity.

Thou representest the image of God in thyself so long as thou sinnest not; but if at any time thou consentest to sin, the image of God withdrawing from thee, thou art made like to the devil himself, who by sinning and by being proud withdrew from God, and his own self, who was the seal of the likeness of the glory of God, as Ezekiel the prophet says (cap. XXVIII), he irrecoverably damned and destroyed. Above we said 584Bthat, as once Moses first ascended Mount Sinai, and so at length had a soliloquy with the Lord concerning secret and mystical things, so holy Church had done. For she ascends as it were a great and lofty mountain when she came to the soliloquy of her Bridegroom, who is « beautiful in form above the sons of men (Psal. XLIV), » and sought of him, saying: Show me, thou whom my soul loveth, where thou feedest, where thou liest down, etc. Let her come therefore to the soliloquy of the almighty Father, and, the filth of all iniquity being removed from her, let her seek of him in like manner, with the affection of charity, where he himself lies down, and where he himself feeds. Therefore, before God the almighty Father speaks with the Bride of his Son, or before that same Bride deserves to hear from the same Father of her Bridegroom: Understand, O daughter, and see, and incline 584Cthine ear, and forget thy people and the house of thy father, and then where I feed or lie down I will show thee [Vulg. Psal. XLIV, 11, Audi, filia, et vide, et inclina aurem tuam, "Hearken, O daughter"] — before that come to pass, it is needful that our mind seek out with ever-watchful attention where in the divine oracles the Son of God is called a mountain, lest perchance our speech be convicted of falsehood by someone less learned, chiefly for this, that we have called Christ a mountain, and have made Mount Sinai equal to him by way of likeness. Many authorities therefore being passed over praeter missis, which testify to this which we say, let one sufficient and fitting authority alone be brought forth into the midst, as is worthy, to which the sons of spiritual grace and the friends of the divine law may gladly lend their spiritual ears, and the enemies of truth, or the less wise, may not have 584Danything to contradict. Daniel the prophet, privy to the secrets of God, is filled with the memory of the abundance of the sweetness of the Holy Spirit; and, expounding the wondrous dream of that great and terrible vision which the king of Babylon had seen, among the other things which he speaks forth to him he interprets Christ as the mountain, saying: Thou hadst seen, etc.

The Latin is Migne's printing of Moralitates in Cantica canticorum (PL 203, coll. 489C–584D), by Philip of Harvengt, from the Corpus Corporum transcription. Parenthetical references in small type stand on Migne's page — editorial identifications inherited from the edition he reprinted or supplied by his shop, not necessarily the author's own; the author's own citations run in the prose itself. Every gilt column mark is an address: migne.app/pl/203/489c resolves to the first.
